#205. State AGs are trying to stop Character.AI from auctioning off users' chat histories

The proposed sale would include explicit conversations with children, officials say.

Character.AI is still drawing controversy and legal scrutiny even during its bankruptcy proceedings. A group of 42 attorneys general are trying to block the chatbot company from auctioning off users' chat histories and other sensitive information.

But the auction for Character.AI won't actually safeguard users' data at all, according to the state AGs. "Character.AI's plan to auction off its platform, including the IP addresses and private chats histories of its users — many of them children — with zero protection for those users is appalling," California Attorney General Rob Bonta said in a statement. "If this sale goes through, users' sensitive data would go into the hands of a buyer even though Character.AI promised consumers that their data would not be sold."

According to Bonta's office, the proposed sale includes chat histories, including conversations "in which AI characters drew children and adults into explicit sexual conversations." The buyer would also get access to chats in which users interacted with AI characters posing as physicians and therapists and discussed self harm, eating disorders, suicidal thoughts and other sensitive topics.

The AGs have also raised concerns about the fact that only one anonymous bidder has been disclosed so far. That entity, called Smirks and Pangs Acquisition LLC, was formed "one day before Character.AI picked it as the base bid for the auction," and its source of funding hasn't been disclosed. "Nothing in the agreement restricts what the buyer may do with the data after closing, including selling it," the AGs say in a court filing.

The auction is currently scheduled for December 1. The AGs are asking the bankruptcy court to block the sale to Smirks and deny a "break-up fee" that would allow the company to collect $350,000 if Character.AI sells to another buyer. They're also asking for children's chatbot conversations and "sensitive" data to be barred from any potential sale.

"Children and families who shared their most personal thoughts with Character.AI never imagined those conversations could be auctioned off to the highest bidder," Colorado Attorney General Phil Weiser said in a statement. "A company's financial troubles do not give it the right to disregard its responsibility to protect consumers, especially children.

#204. Israeli military veterans, a backbone of protest movement, vow to keep demonstrating

In a sea of Israeli flags, Yiftach Golov holds one that looks a little different.

Among the hundreds of thousands of protesters who took to the streets for the 13th week in a row on Saturday, Golov hoists a brown flag that represents a group called “Brother and Sisters in Arms.”

They are veterans – many, like Golov, from elite forces – who now feel they are fighting on a new battlefield: To save Israeli democracy.

“We believe this is our responsibility to go once again called to the flag of the nation to stop this madness to defend Israel,” Golov said, as he weaved his way through the protesters on Tel Aviv’s Kaplan street, between the high-rises that house many of Israel’s high tech companies.

During the second intifada, in the early 2000s, Golov served in a special forces reconnaissance unit. He was never before particularly political, focusing more on getting his PhD in biophysics from Tel Aviv University.

But when the protest movement against the Israeli government’s judicial overhaul plan began in January, Golov attended one a demonstration and soon became one of thousands of veterans, and now military reservists, who have taken up the cause as their new mission.

Some, including elite Air Force reservists, have taken it a step further, threatening not to heed the call to train or even serve in protest of the government’s plans planned judicial changes, which would give the governing parties more control over Israel’s judiciary.

Others have taken to becoming some of the most active organizers and demonstrators. Last week, a group from Brothers and Sisters in Arms protested by carrying a figure wrapped in the Israeli flag on a stretcher, the way they would carry a wounded comrade off the field.

While Golov says he has not taken the drastic step to refuse service, he understands the motivation.

“We’re fighting for justice and liberty, just like the American story, that’s the values that that are being represented symbolized back when we look at our flag, that’s something that was lacking lacking for the last few decades. So basically, we reclaim the flag,” he said.

Palestinian taxi driver recounts surviving attack by right-wing protesters

Fellow members of the group, all wearing brown shirts with the organization’s logo, come up and say hello. They’re sprinkled all throughout the protests. One is even leading the “Pink Front,” a group of coordinated drummers who look like they are dressed for a rave, and often lead the chants at the protests.

They’re using skills they learned in the military – how to organize, how to mobilize – now for the protests. But more importantly, they say they have the same type of motivation.

“The very deep feeling that you are part of something bigger than yourself, that (you’re) allowed to sacrifice anything that is needed, whether it’s your career, health, seriously mental health,” Golov said. “We all have a mission, you’re willing to do it at any cost. You’re very determined, you know that you are on the right side, you’re carrying the torch of light. That keeps us being highly motivated despite the fact that we’re not sleeping for days.”

Israel’s protest movement is made up of many disparate groups, but the pressure from Israel’s much vaunted veterans has been seen as a key to moving the needle.

Last Monday, after weeks of sustained protests and the largest general strike in Israeli history, Prime Minister Benjamin Netanyahu announced a pause to the legislation, to allow time for negotiations with the opposition.

But despite the announcements, protesters are still out in the streets in large numbers. CNN affiliate Channel 12 in Israel estimated the size of Saturday’s demonstration in Tel Aviv at about 150,000 people. Organizers claimed it was 230,000.

Last week’s mass protests and widespread strike action came after Netanyahu said he had decided to fire Defense Minister Yoav Gallant for advocating a delay in passing the legislation – a move that Netanyahu has since delayed, sources told CNN, due to “the present security situation.”

In his televised speech calling for a delay, Gallant had said the pause in the legislation was needed “for the security of Israel,” citing the refusal of some Israel Defense Forces reservists to train in protest of the government plans. He said pressing ahead with the proposals could threaten Israel’s security.

Under pressure at home and from allies abroad, Netanyahu said he would delay votes on the remaining legislation until after the Knesset’s Passover recess in April “to give time for a real chance for a real debate.”

“Out of the responsibility to the nation, I decided to delay … the vote, in order to give time for discussion,” he added.

But Netanyahu indicated that the delay was only temporary. He insisted that the overhaul was necessary, and reiterated criticism of refusal to train or serve in the military in protest at the planned changes. “Refusing is the end of our country,” he said.

Many protesters don’t believe that the pause is real, or say it’s simply a stalling tactic to give Netanyahu some breathing room and get the protesters to go home before he plows on with the reforms.

“We will start doing deactivation only when we will know 100% that Israel state will stay a functional democratic country. Whatever needs to be done for that,” Golov said.

#203. Ladybird browser's first downloadable alpha is surprisingly capable

An independent browser engine arrives on Linux and macOS, and we’ve tried it.

Note: At the time of writing, I was unaware of criticism directed at Ladybird creator Andreas Kling’s public statements. Readers interested in this background can find further context in accounts by Kelson Vibber and Drew DeVault.

After four years of work to build a browser engine from scratch, the Ladybird project released its first downloadable alpha on November 23, offering prebuilt versions for macOS and Linux. Previously, curious users had to compile the browser themselves from its source. Now it’s an installable package with automatic updating.

Ladybird is most notable for what it isn’t: another browser fork. Where Chrome, Edge, Brave, and Opera all run on Google’s Blink engine, and Firefox depends on Gecko, Ladybird’s rendering engine and its LibJS JavaScript engine were written from scratch. “The engine is our own, built from the ground up, and not a fork,” project lead Andreas Kling wrote in Monday’s announcement. “The browser around it is modest, but functional.”

Nearly the entire modern web currently runs on three browser engines. There’s WebKit (Safari), Gecko (Firefox), and the biggest of them all, Blink (Chromium). Those limited options mean that web developers often end up optimizing sites for Chromium specifically, since that’s where the majority of traffic is, and as a result, the whole web platform tends to follow suit toward whatever the dominant engine supports.

An independent implementation of web standards has value not just by providing additional competition in the market, but because it can’t help but reveal places where sites depend on Chromium behaviors rather than published specifications. The fact that Ladybird exists isn’t likely to shift the market tides any time soon, but every new browser engine increases the number of implementations that standards can be tested against, which can surface Chromium-specific assumptions and push the ecosystem toward the spec.

The Ladybird project was borne out of Kling’s SerenityOS project, an attempt to develop a new independent OS from scratch, before splitting off as a separate project. Kling formed the Ladybird Browser Initiative, a 501(c)(3) nonprofit funded by sponsorships and donations. The project’s site highlights that there are no sponsored search engine deals supporting the project like many other popular browsers, and sponsoring entities have no say in the project roadmap.

The Ladybird alpha is a rolling release channel that updates automatically, and the downloadable package is a huge step to getting the browser onto the PCs of everyday users. “Ladybird improves quickly, and until now you had to build it from source to see that,” Kling wrote. Though the browser began as C++, in keeping with the ethos of SerenityOS, the project has since started shifting toward Rust, starting with its JavaScript engine.

While it’s exciting to have a new engine option for browsing, the project leader notes that this is still a very early release. The browser build contains a built-in tool for reporting any websites that fail to render or crash the browser. Kling writes, “We want security nerds to hack it! A new browser gets hacked, over and over, and that’s part of the process, so we make no promises about security today.”

Microsoft users are still out in the cold on this one for now. The project says that future support for Windows is planned, but it’s macOS and Linux only for now.

I tested the Ladybird alpha version 2026.47.8 on my AceMagic W1 mini PC AMD Ryzen 7 8745HS with 16GB DDR5 and running Fedora Linux 44 with the new KDE Plasma 6.8.3, and the most surprising thing about the alpha build was how little it feels like alpha software.

The browser seemed to handle most anything I threw at it without issue. Day-to-day tabbed browsing mechanics felt snappy and normal. A 50MB test video file downloaded quickly and smoothly. With a dozen tabs open, switching between them was instant, and the browser felt responsive throughout. Options menus were surprisingly fleshed out for a build this young.

Google Maps loaded instantly, located me, and handled everything I threw at it: adding and removing layers, zooming out from the Midwest, and dropping into Street View in Alberta, Canada in around three seconds. Everything rendered fast enough that my mini PC’s fan spun up noticeably. GitHub’s site, including repository navigation, worked without a hitch. So did YouTube and the news sites I tried.

I could stall the browser, but it took some effort. With about 10 tabs open, I was streaming a 4K test video file from my personal Jellyfin server. Everything worked fine at first, but after skipping around in the file over and over again, the browser eventually got stuck. Mind you, this is an alpha build that I was trying to trip up, not a representation of something inherently broken.

After spending an evening with Ladybird, I can say that while it won’t be replacing Firefox for me anytime soon, I was genuinely impressed that a scratch-made engine could perform this well in such an early state. Most new browser projects are forks of something that already exists. Ladybird is the rare exception, an engine written from nothing, and the alpha runs well enough that if you have a Mac or Linux PC handy, it’s worth downloading a build and poking around yourself. Just remember Kling’s warning: This is nowhere near trustworthy enough to make your daily driver, yet.

What the alpha demonstrates is that a small team of independent developers, funded by donations, has built a browser engine from scratch that handles real web browsing without a fuss. That it works this well this soon, using no existing engine code, is an achievement. I thought I was a Firefox loyalist, but I’ll be keeping an eye on where this one goes.

Ars Technica has been separating the signal from the noise for over 25 years. With our unique combination of technical savvy and wide-ranging interest in the technological arts and sciences, Ars is the trusted source in a sea of information. After all, you don’t need to know everything, only what’s important.

#202. 7 gadgets that'll instantly upgrade your Thanksgiving feast

These tools will make turkey day just a little easier.

We may receive a commission on purchases made from links.

Thanksgiving is supposed to be a time for rest, relaxation and appreciating all the good things that have happened to you over the past year. However, there's no denying that it's also a ton of work for whoever is in charge of preparing your Thanksgiving meal. Not only do you have to consider the cooking itself, there's all the other things that come with making a holiday feast like choosing the right recipes, gathering all the ingredients and scheduling which dishes need to be cooked at what time so everything is ready together. And all that doesn't even include managing anyone who feels like helping, which can turn into its own logistical issue.

So to help smooth out the chaos that is holiday cooking, we've put together a collection of essential cooking gadgets that can save you time and take some of the stress out of preparing your Thanksgiving bounty.

Most people probably wouldn't consider a knife to be very techy, and for most blades that sentiment rings true. But on days like Thanksgiving where you're going to be doing a ton of chopping, cutting, slicing and more, it can't hurt to have a little extra help. And then there's the turkey, because it isn't going to carve itself. Furthermore, if you want your bird to look anything like it does in cooking videos with those neat, super clean slices, you're going to need a sharp tool to get there.

For cooking nerds who like to geek out about the science of sharpness, one of my favorite knives on the market right now is the Seattle Ultrasonics C-200. In addition to just being a really nice knife featuring a three-layer san mai blade made out of Japanese AUS-10 steel, inside its handle there's a motor that vibrates the knife 30,000 times per second. While the oscillations are so small that you can't actually feel it shaking, those vibrations increased the knife's effective sharpness by up to 50 percent. It excels at delicate foods like tomatoes that often like to stick to the side of your blade, but also denser things like potatoes and meat, which obviously includes turkey. The C-200 even comes with an IP65 rating for dust and water resistance, so there's no worry about ruining its electronics with an errant splash. And if you opt for the wireless charging tile, you'll never have to worry about keeping its battery topped up. Granted, starting at $399, it certainly isn't cheap, but that price is very much in the same range as other premium chef knives from big name companies like Shun, Wusthoff and others.

Alternatively, if you want a dedicated gadget for helping you carve a turkey, you can always turn to more traditional electric knives like those from Cusinart, NutriChef, Hamilton Beach and more. Admittedly, these types of knives aren't nearly as pretty as blades like the C200 and the need to plug them in makes them harder to use on an everyday basis. But if you want a relatively easy and affordable way to make serving your Thanksgiving turkey go a bit more smoothly, a classic electric knife is definitely worth a look.

Even if you prefer side dishes like mashed potatoes, stuffing or a green bean casserole, there's no denying that the turkey is the star of Thanksgiving dinner. And if you want it to come out just right, you need a decent thermometer. Not having one is basically gambling that you get lucky with your timing and the bird turns out well. That said, there are a range of options, from simple digital sticks like the affordable ThermoPop 2 to fancy probe thermometers like the second-gen model from Combustion Inc.

Basic options require a bit more work as you can't leave them in the bird while it's in the oven, whereas more expensive versions have wireless support that allows you to leave them inside the turkey as it cooks. Some options like those from Combustion Inc. even account for carry-over cooking, which is when the temperature of the food continues to rise even after you take it out of the oven. You just need to decide how much you want to spend and what kind of features you really want. One of my more affordable favorites is the Sense from Chef IQ, which offers a great balance between price and performance. Just don't be a fool and try to rely strictly on the little pop-out thermometers that sometimes come with the bird, as those aren't known for their accuracy or precision.

A big issue during Thanksgiving is not having enough room in your oven for everything that needs to be baked or roasted. One easy solution to this is turning to an air fryer, which works almost exactly like the convection function on an oven, but in a smaller volume that's faster to heat and sometimes offers better air circulation. Another really nice thing about air fryers is that they can be quite affordable, with name brand models like the Instant Pot Vortex or the Ninja Air Fryer Pro, both of which are currently going for as little as $80.

Upgrading to more expensive fryers like the Philips 3000 can give you more capacity, while even pricier options like the Wonder Oven Pro from Our Place have as many as eight different functions for things like baking, roasting, dehydrating, proofing and more. And perhaps the best thing is that when the holidays are over, you can easily move these into your pantry, closet or garage and reclaim some of your precious kitchen counter space.

A good digital scale falls in the same general camp as a decent thermometer and there isn't an experienced baker that would be caught dead without one. They're super easy to use and in my opinion, they are a staple of any well-equipped kitchen. You can just plop a mixing bowl on top, zero out the scale and then add ingredients in as needed while you get precise measurements, which can be especially critical for baked goods and confections. OXO makes a very nice and rather affordable digital scale that starts at around $65, but you can find cheaper options from well-known brands like KitchenAid for barely more than $20.

A lot of people think blenders are big, bulky contraptions that make a ton of noise and take up too much room in your kitchen. And while the most powerful ones often are, they don't need to be, as there are affordable options that aren't much bigger than a teapot like these ones from Magic Bullet and Ninja. Regardless, they are great for making a post-dinner milkshake or a lovely fall-inspired soup (butternut squash anyone?).

Alternatively, you can opt for an immersion blender (aka a hand blender), which can do much of the same functions as a big countertop model, but gives you even greater control and the ability to blend things directly on the stovetop using the pots you already own. This can make them better choices for whipping up sauces like hollandaise (which is delicious when poured over roasted asparagus), mayonnaise or even a classic cranberry sauce to go with your turkey. I prefer cordless options like those from KitchenAid as they have more than enough battery life to complete multiple dishes and the lack of a wire makes them super easy to use. But if your budget is tight, traditional cabled options are totally fine too and often a more affordable choice.

Even for people like me who prefer to chop things by hand, a food processor can be an incredible time saver during the holidays. Not only do they make quick minces of veggies, they can also be used to grate cheese, shred things like potatoes (for an easy Thanksgiving hash), or whip up dips like salsa. Some options like the Cusinart Mini Food processor are stupendously cheap at under $30, while pricer models like the Ninja Professional Plus Food Processor come with a bunch of attachments to handle all sorts of dishes.

Cooking food in a water bath might seem like an unnecessarily complex thing to do for the holidays, but it can actually save you time and effort. A good sous vide machine like those from Anova or Breville allow you to set an exact temperature and hold it there almost indefinitely. This allows you to cook things like meat with precision, so there's no worrying about if something is going to come out medium-rare or well-done.

And even on Thanksgiving where turkey is the main protein, you can still use a sous vide machine to make dishes like superb rosemary mashed potatoes or luscious glazed carrots. Some models even come with Wi-Fi, so you can start and set them remotely using your phone. On top of that, all they need to function is an outlet, so a sous vide machine can be a great option for people with small kitchens or a limited number of burners on their stove.

#201. Desperate appeals from women on death row in Iran after protests

Twin sisters Romina (left) and Taraneh Rahimi were arrested in late January, their cousin says

In recent weeks, the 21-year-old has been unable to walk properly after her legs were beaten, and an ear-infection caused by an untreated head injury has affected her balance, her cousin says.

She is "keeping her spirits up" but "there are days she breaks down in tears," Masoud Nourmohammadi tells BBC News Persian.

He says Taraneh and her twin sister Romina were arrested after joining the anti-government protests that swept the country in January.

When the twins' mother first saw them in prison, their faces were so swollen and bruised, and their hair pulled out, that she could only recognise them by their voices, Nourmohammadi says, speaking from the US.

He says Taraneh signed a confession after interrogators threatened that her sister would be raped in front of her.

Taraneh is one of six women who have been sentenced to death this year in connection with the January protests, according to the UN's human rights rapporteur for Iran, Mai Sato.

The figure for men is much higher. However, women protesters and dissidents are being sentenced to death in numbers not seen for decades, as part of a "horrific rise" in politically motivated executions, Amnesty International's Iran researcher Raha Bahreini says.

January's protests began over economic grievances and spread to become Iran's largest since the 1979 Islamic Revolution

The Iranian embassy in London told the BBC that claims of an increase in the use of the death penalty in protest-related cases were "misleading".

It categorically rejected allegations of the use of torture and any allegations that the death penalty has been imposed solely for peaceful protest, which it said is a "legitimate, and protected right".

It said death sentences in relation to the "riot in January 2026" were issued in cases involving "intentional murder" or "Moharebeh (the taking up of weapons with the intent to threaten or wreck the people's lives or property)".

The twins were seized in late January, Nourmohammadi says.

It was two weeks after the end of the protests, which began in late December over economic grievances and grew to become Iran's largest against the clerical establishment since the 1979 Islamic Revolution.

The US-based Human Rights Activists News Agency (Hrana) says it has identified more than 7,000 people killed in the crackdown that followed, most of them protesters, but believes the true figure is much higher.

The Iranian government accuses the US and Israel of fomenting the unrest, blaming "terrorists" and "rioters" for the deaths, which it says totalled 3,117 including some members of the security forces.

The twins' family did not find out where they were being held for more than three months, their cousin says. They were tried over protests in Isfahan, in an area of the city where a member of the Islamic Revolutionary Guard Corps (IRGC) security force was killed.

Taraneh loves horses and would like to set up her own breeding centre one day, her cousin says

Court documents have not been published, but the BBC has seen part of a sentencing document which says Taraneh was convicted of moharebeh, which is a capital offence under Iran's Islamic Penal Code. The offence included "drawing a bladed weapon with the intent to endanger people's lives", according to the document.

Romina was sentenced to 25 years in jail for the lesser charge of aiding and abetting moharebeh, according to Sato, the UN rapporteur.

Nourmohammadi, who has been in contact with relatives and others in Iran familiar with the case, says both women deny all the charges against them and that there is no evidence Taraneh had a "bladed weapon".

Sato says the sisters were part of a case in which 10 people were sentenced to death, but that there was "no clarification of the acts attributed to each individual".

According to sources familiar with the case, the twins' lawyers have submitted a complaint alleging they were tortured and sexually abused during interrogation, but the investigation into it is being overseen by an official they accuse of torture.

This image of Mahboubeh Shabani, sentenced to death in September, has been shared with human rights groups

Voice messages from other women on death row contain a mixture of desperation and defiance.

Mahboubeh Shabani - widely reported to be 33 and a civil engineer - was sentenced to death in September under Iran's espionage law after transporting injured protesters, according to rights groups.

She called for help for herself and other prisoners from jail in the city of Mashhad in north-eastern Iran: "Everyone is in a terrible place mentally, especially those whose sentences have been finalised," she said.

Also jailed in Mashhad is Najmeh Amini, reported to be 23, who was initially arrested in the wake of the January protests, and later sentenced to death after being convicted of committing blasphemy in a social media post, according to rights groups.

"As long as I am alive, I have one hope, and that is to see a free and prosperous Iran before my life ends by the noose," a voice message attributed to her says.

In addition, Mojdeh Hashemi Bazargani - reported to be aged 27 - is also facing execution, according to rights groups. They say she was convicted of charges including "espionage", after being arrested in April, during the current war between Iran and Israel and the US.

The US-based Iranian rights group Tavaana has shared audio it says is of a phone call she made from prison, describing being beaten in the head, neck and body and made to sign a confession.

The woman in the recording says: "They accused me of espionage. They say you've been paid for it. If I had been paid for espionage, I would have a car or a house. I have nothing."

Najmeh Amini has been sentenced to death after being convicted of committing blasphemy in a social media post, rights groups say

Amnesty International describes the charges in all three of these cases and also the twins as "politically motivated".

In response to the BBC's questions, the Iranian Embassy in London said "any allegations of the use of torture in connection with the mentioned cases are categorically rejected". It did not comment further on individual cases, but said "confessions obtained under duress have no legal validity".

At least 39 people have already been executed in connection with the January protests, according to Sato.

She notes a "sharp" increase in arrests and sentences across both men and women, and sees the rise in death sentences for women as an "escalation and expansion" of an "established pattern of repression".

Iranian human rights lawyer Musa Barzin, who is based in Turkey, says "it appears that factors such as gender or age now carry less weight in determining the severity of punishment".

Official detail on cases is often scant and some may go unreported.

However, Sato says as well as the six women she identified as being arrested in connection with protests, a further three have received death sentences this year - for "blasphemy" and "corruption on Earth charges, in relation to the war in March" and "espionage and moharebeh".

In comparison, these nine sentences are more than Hrana recorded for women for similar offences over the whole decade to 2025 - with no more than three in any one year.

The death sentences in three of the cases Sato identified as being linked to the protests have since been overturned â though two of those have been sent for retrial. She says she is concerned by an "increasingly frequent pattern" where overturned death sentences are later "reimposed by a parallel branch of the judiciary once public attention has subsided".

Taraneh (left) and Romina both say they gave baseless confessions after being tortured

Sato says there was a "turning point" for women in Iran during the 2022 protests over the death of Mahsa Amini, who died in custody after being detained over an alleged breach of Iran's dress code.

Women "were at the forefront of challenging state policies and structures of control," says Sato. "Their political agency, visibility and leadership have made them targets of state repression."

The Iranian embassy said judicial proceedings in Iran are conducted in accordance with the Constitution and rule of law. It adds: "Cases involving the death penalty have included charges such as serious criminal offences, violence, terrorism, espionage, cooperation with hostile intelligence services and Moharebeh."

Iran executes more people per head of population than any other country, according to UN experts. Human rights groups say last year saw the highest number in decades.

Hrana recorded 2,003 executions in the year to October 2026. The vast majority - including 47 women - were for crimes such as murder and drug trafficking.

Nourmohammadi says the family are trying as best they can to support the twins.

Romina enjoys music and often asks relatives to play songs over the phone, he says.

Taraneh loves horses and has always wanted to set up a horse breeding centre, he says, and they tell her they plan to help her do so in the future: "That's how we try to give her hope."

#200. €561 Billion and Still Not Enough: Germany's 2027 Budget Exposes a Coalition on the Brink

The budget for 2027 has reached new highs, and pension funds, defense spending and a weak economy are increasing the pressure. At the same time, tensions are mounting within the government.

At first glance, everything seems to be business as usual. Lawmakers in the Bundestag, the lower house of parliament, are holding four days of final debates on how much money the federal government may spend in the coming year, and on what. These follow a familiar pattern: The government pats itself on the back, while the opposition voices criticism. But anyone listening more closely will notice that there are doubts even in the ranks of the coalition parties, the conservative Christian Democratic Union (CDU), their Bavarian allies, the Christian Social Union (CSU), and the center-left Social Democratic Party (SPD).

"As a country, we are in a difficult situation geopolitically, technologically, and demographically, and next year's budget is an accurate reflection of that," Chancellor Friedrich Merz (CDU) acknowledged on Wednesday in the Bundestag.

"If we don't change course, in a few years we'll find ourselves in a situation where all federal revenue will have to be spent on social programs, interest payments, and defense," he reflected on Wednesday during a four-hour debate focused on government policy as a whole, rather than the individual budgets of the ministries.

To view this video please enable JavaScript, and consider upgrading to a web browser that supports HTML5 video

This government has now been in office for about 20 months. Back in early 2025, expectations were enormous. Merz had promised to bring the budget back in line with the debt brake enshrined in the constitution, which stipulates that the government can spend only slightly more than it collects (with exceptions now made for defense and security).

Yet for 2027, spending — at just under €561 billion ($614 billion) — will be higher than ever before, and tax revenues are nowhere near sufficient to cover this, meaning Germany is looking at record borrowing for the year totaling €210 billion.

The largest opposition party, the far-right Alternative for Germany (AfD) calculated that the government will incur €401,000 in new debt every minute next year. In the Bundestag, AfD co-leader Tino Chrupalla called it a "debt orgy" that will paralyze the country. Merz will "go down in history as the chancellor who led Germany to bankruptcy," he exclaimed.

Germany's budget deficit has only ever been higher in the COVID-19 pandemic. The current pressures are obvious: The economy is struggling, the population aging, and Russia's war against Ukraine is about to enter its sixth year.

To view this video please enable JavaScript, and consider upgrading to a web browser that supports HTML5 video

The defense budget is now by far the highest in the history of the Federal Republic, with €109 billion allocated for 2027, an increase of €27 billion on last year. Added to this is the multi-year, debt-financed special military fund, intended to modernize the armed forces, bringing the total defense budget to €139 billion.

The AfD and the socialist Left Party have sharply criticized this. Left Party parliamentary leader Heidi Reichinnek accused the government of pursuing "militarization and boundless rearmament on the one hand, and brutal social cuts and an orgy of budget cuts on the other."

The AfD's Chrupalla said that the temporary reduction in the electricity tax, pushed through at the last minute, would save the average family a mere €100 a year, while Ukrainian President Volodymyr Zelenskyy "walks out of the chancellery here in Berlin with a check for €200 million after just one afternoon."

"Whose chancellor are you, Mr. Merz?" he asked.

The opposition Greens did not agree. While they also criticized the budget as "unserious," Green Party Bundestag member Britta Haßelmann described the AfD's stance on Ukraine as shameful. She rejected the idea that money was being wasted on Ukraine, saying that supporting Ukraine bolstered security for Germany and Europe.

The chancellor shares this view. "You can't say it often enough, either to the far right or the far left: Russia knows exactly what it wants. And Russia wants more than just Ukraine," Merz said.

He added that Russia is escalating tensions in the Baltic region, in Poland, in the Black Sea, and in space; and that Moscow is also seeking escalation in Germany, even at the cost of lives. "That is why we must show Russia its limits," he said.

But it is becoming increasingly difficult for the government to justify the high spending on Ukraine. Germany's pension and health care costs are only getting bigger: By far the largest single budget item is that of the Ministry of Labor and Social Affairs, which is rising to over €200 billion, even with cuts to unemployment benefits.

Work is currently underway on a reform of the pension system — something that many see as urgent, as the workforce born in the 1960s, when more than a million children were born in Germany every year, is on brink of retiring. Birth rates nearly halved by the mid-1970s and declined even further afterward. For now, so-called "boomers" still make up one-third of the current workforce — but soon they will be drawing pensions.

Even now, Germany's pensions cannot be financed solely with contributions on wages and salaries. In 2025, just over €420 billion in pensions were paid out, with the government contributing €127 billion of that amount. By 2027, the government aims to save €3 billion on this figure, which is likely to further increase pension contributions.

The coalition parties are stuck in a row over how to reform the pension system. Chancellor Merz insisted in the Bundestag that the coalition was solving the country's fundamental problems, but he warned that in future, tax revenues "will no longer be enough to afford everything we have become all too accustomed to," he said. "All of us here will have to give up some of the ideas we cherish."

The SPD, on the other hand, is counting on increasing federal revenue. Its parliamentary leader Matthias Miersch emphasized in the Bundestag that large fortunes and inheritances must contribute more to "public costs."

That, he said, must also apply to large companies. Currently, heirs to family-owned businesses often pay significantly less in taxes than heirs to real estate or financial assets, because they can be exempted from inheritance tax if they continue to run the business. This is intended to protect them from having to pay taxes in the event of a breakup or sale of the company.

According to Miersch, the government must now think about taxing the wealthy more. "For us, this issue is by no means off the table; on the contrary, we are putting it on the agenda," he said. The government's revenue situation is "an issue we need to discuss."

All in all, the outlook for 2027 is strained — and not just financially. The political differences between the CDU/CSU and the SPD are likely to make working together even more difficult. Conflict is inevitable, especially with several state elections scheduled for the spring.

The chancellor knows this and tried to appear confident that better collaboration was possible. "We must seek compromise in our joint decision-making, represented by these — admittedly quite different — coalition partners," he said.

#199. Can you play GTA 6 from an external hard drive on PS5 or Xbox Series X/S?

Rockstar’s behemoth takes up a lot of storage space.

Grand Theft Auto 6 is the biggest game of the year by some distance, having racked up $3.79 billion in pre-orders and day-one sales. On PlayStation 5 alone, more than 39 million unique players took control of dual protagonists Jason and Lucia on opening weekend, making it the biggest game debut in the 32-year history of PlayStation.

There will be many other folks who want to play the game and haven't had a chance to jump in yet. One thing they should note is that, with a hefty install size, GTA 6 will take up a significant chunk of their PS5 or Xbox Series X/S storage space.

You might be wondering whether you can install GTA 6 on an external hard drive to avoid having to delete other games and data from your console. You can move GTA 6 to your external storage when you're not playing it. However, to run the game locally, you'll need to have it installed on your system's built-in storage, an SDD you've installed in your PS5 or a compatible NVMe expansion card that's plugged into your Xbox.

As with many other recent games, GTA 6 needs to take advantage of the fast-loading capabilities of the PS5 and Xbox Series X/S. The consoles are unable to load data for these titles quickly enough from an external hard drive over a USB connection. For the same reason, disc-based PS5 and Xbox Series X games are installed onto system storage before you can play them. The PS5 can run PS4 games from external storage, though. Likewise, an Xbox Series X/S can run older Xbox One and Xbox 360 titles directly from an external hard drive.

#197. New York state bans AI chatbot toys for kids

Companies violating the five year ban will face a $15,000 per day fine.

New York governor Kathy Hochul has signed into law a five-year ban on the sale of AI chatbot toys, including dolls and robots that use the internet to converse with children. The new law comes with some teeth, including steep daily fines for each violation enforced by the state's attorney general.

The governor's press release cited the US Public Interest Research Group Education Fund (PIRG), that tested chatbot toys marketed to children aged three to 12. Some of the toys talked about sexually explicit subjects and provided advice that could create a fire hazard in homes. The toys had few or zero parental controls and the longer the conversation, the more safety limits weakened. Some toys could even record a child's voice or scan their face.

"AI chatbots in children's toys are exposing young people to inappropriate content and putting their mental health at risk," said New York attorney general Letitia James.

Violators face civil penalties up to $15,000 per day for each violation, according to the governor's office. James added that her branch would "not hesitate to enforce this law and other measures to stop AI systems from doing harm. We won't allow our children's health to become the collateral damage of big tech."

Hochul and New York State legislators recently enacted another law called "AI Chatbot Restrictions for Minors," prohibiting unsafe chatbot features for children under 18, with civil penalties of up to $25,000 per infraction. Hochul also signed an executive order this summer, issuing a one-year ban data centers using 50MW or more.

Anti-AI measures were a politically popular during US midterm elections, as Americans voted to restrict the country's data center boom. Data centers were the subject of 45 ballot measures in 10 states and 39 measures were passed to restrict the facilities in some form.

In a statement, Amazon said the products it sells must follow US laws. "We require all products sold by third-party sellers in our store to comply with applicable laws and regulations," Amazon told NBC News. "Amazon is committed to the safety of children and families. We support efforts across the industry and government to put appropriate guardrails in place as this technology develops." However, the retail giant sells some 12 million different toys in the US (as of 2023), so complying with the NY AI chatbot law could be a tall order.

#195. The FCC has authorized SpaceX to deploy 1 million satellites

This constellation would increase the number of satellites in orbit by roughly 50-fold.

In an extraordinary decision, the Federal Communications Commission on Wednesday granted SpaceX conditional authorization to deploy a constellation of up to 1 million Starmind satellites designed to serve as orbital data centers.

If fully deployed, this constellation would increase the number of satellites in orbit by roughly 50-fold.

“The United States is moving decisively to ensure that this breakthrough technology creates opportunity, drives investment, and delivers lasting benefits for the American people,” FCC Chairman Brendan Carr said in a statement on Wednesday. “This action opens a bold new frontier for the computing infrastructure needed to power America’s leadership and growth.”

The decision, announced a day before the Thanksgiving holiday in the United States, represents a major regulatory victory for SpaceX and its founder, Elon Musk, who has allied himself with the Trump administration.

This announcement clears a major hurdle to deploying data centers in space, although it does not establish that orbital data centers are technically or economically viable. Nevertheless, the FCC authorization appears designed to give the company wide latitude as it works through these remaining issues.

For example, SpaceX has seven years to deploy a single operational Starmind satellite under the order. SpaceX has until November 2035 to deploy 100,000 operational satellites, and November 2040 for the full constellation. SpaceX, therefore, has a 14-year regulatory runway to deploy the most ambitious industrial project ever approved for spaceflight.

The authorization includes some conditional aspects. SpaceX has proposed operating in six orbits, ranging from 550 km to about 1,000 km. The company sought a waiver from FCC rules to allow satellites above 600 km to be disposed of into “Earth disposal orbits or heliocentric orbits.” That is, instead of burning them up in Earth’s atmosphere, SpaceX wants the flexibility to move retired satellites into other orbits around Earth, or potentially into orbit around the Sun.

The FCC did not grant this waiver request “because SpaceX has not provided sufficient information for the Commission to determine whether its alternative disposal methods are compliant with the Commission’s requirements.” The company will need to obtain a waiver before deploying any Starmind satellites that are not intended to be disposed of via atmospheric reentry.

However, in other ways the FCC appears to have been incredibly lenient with SpaceX. This is particularly the case with environmental concerns raised by numerous parties that commented on SpaceX’s application to the FCC.

Half a dozen organizations, such as the Secure World Foundation and American Astronomical Society, raised concerns about deploying so many satellites into orbit, citing collision risks, orbital debris, atmospheric pollution from so many satellite reentries, impacts on astronomical observations, and more. The FCC also received approximately 1,500 public comments concerning SpaceX’s application.

Some of these environmental issues, the FCC said, lay beyond the scope of its review. For example, on concerns about the environmental implications of thousands or potentially tens of thousands of rocket launches, the FCC said, “Potential concerns related to the emissions of rocket launches and the subsequent impacts on the human environment as an indirect result of the Starmind system are outside of the Commission’s purview.”

The FCC also rejected calls for further environmental scrutiny of pollution from satellite reentries, including concerns about aluminum oxides and their potential effects on the ozone layer. “Other concerns, including impacts on the atmosphere caused by satellites burning up on re-entry, remain speculative and not supported in the record,” the FCC authorization states.

As part of its decision, the FCC cited a significant policy change adopted in October, when the agency concluded that space-based activities generally fall outside its obligations under the National Environmental Policy Act. The FCC reasoned that as these activities occur beyond US territory, they don’t constitute federal actions requiring environmental review. As a result, the agency declined to conduct a comprehensive environmental assessment of SpaceX’s Starmind constellation.

Ars Technica has been separating the signal from the noise for over 25 years. With our unique combination of technical savvy and wide-ranging interest in the technological arts and sciences, Ars is the trusted source in a sea of information. After all, you don’t need to know everything, only what’s important.

#194. Peter Magyar meets Volodymyr Zelenskyy: Hungary and Ukraine make a fresh start after the icy relations of the Viktor Orban era

After years of frosty and hostile relations under Hungary's Kremlin-friendly former PM Viktor Orban, his successor, Peter Magyar, and Ukrainian President Volodymyr Zelenskyy seek to reset their countries' ties.

The location and the gesture were carefully chosen: Hungarian Prime Minister Peter Magyar and Ukrainian President Volodymyr Zelenskyy stood side by side, laid flowers and bowed their heads at the military cemetery in the center of the Ukrainian city of Uzhhorod.

One of those buried there is an ethnic Hungarian who was one of the first soldiers killed in Russia's full-scale war of aggression against Ukraine.

Magyar intentionally chose not to begin his first visit to Ukraine as prime minister at a government building. Instead, his Tuesday afternoon visit began at a place where he could demonstrate his solidarity with Ukraine more clearly than anywhere else.

Uzhhorod, known as Ungvar in Hungarian, is the capital of Ukraine's Transcarpathia region, which is home to around 80,000 members of Ukraine's Hungarian minority. Thousands of ethnic Hungarians are fighting Russian occupying forces on the front lines.

For Hungary, where ethnic Hungarian minorities in neighboring countries traditionally play an important role, the war is therefore closer to home than it is for other European countries.

Magyar described his visit to Ukraine as a "historic day" in a Facebook post. Before the ceremony at the cemetery in Uzhhorod, he said: "I bring with me the solidarity of the Hungarian people. We stand with Ukraine against the Russian aggressor."

Zelenskyy responded on X, saying "We appreciate this gesture of respect toward those who sacrificed their lives for Ukraine's independence."

The meeting between Magyar and Zelenskyy marked a turning point in Hungary's approach to Ukraine.

The two leaders repeatedly shook hands throughout the day. Such scenes would have been unthinkable just a few months ago. The propaganda of Magyar's pro-Russian predecessor, Viktor Orban, portrayed Ukraine as a "mafia state" and "lawless country" on an almost daily basis.

Under Orban, relations between Hungary and Ukraine reached a historic low.

Now the two countries are seeking a political reset, which is important not only for Hungary and Ukraine but for Europe as a whole. After all, Orban's repeated obstruction of support for Ukraine drove the EU to the brink of despair.

During talks in Uzhhorod, the two leaders also agreed to closer economic cooperation.

The talks centered on Hungarian diesel supplies to Ukraine, greater use of Ukrainian gas storage facilities by Hungarian companies, and joint infrastructure projects, including the opening of new border crossings and a direct rail link between Budapest and Kyiv.

Nevertheless, no formal agreements were signed on Tuesday.

At a subsequent news conference, journalists asked questions about sensitive issues between the two countries: minority rights, Ukraine's EU integration and a new EU loan for Ukraine.

Throughout the day, the two men were visibly keen to avoid any discord.

Magyar, for example, described his angry outburst over Ukraine's "Carpathian Eight" initiative in parliament in late September as a "misunderstanding" and suggested that Hungary might, after all, take part.

Zelenskyy's cautious replies to journalists suggest that he knows his country's path to EU membership also runs through Budapest.

One of Magyar's statements on Tuesday was key: "We've finally put a stop to this completely unnecessary and inexplicable political escalation."

Even before taking office in early May 2026, Magyar had promised to reset relations with Ukraine. But the process has not been entirely smooth.

In many respects — but certainly not all — Magyar is distancing himself from his predecessor. He has made no bones of the fact that he sees Russia as the aggressor, but he rejects both arms deliveries to Ukraine and even the transit of weapons across Hungarian territory.

Magyar previously accused Ukraine of treating ethnic Hungarians as second-class citizens — a claim that is not borne out by the facts. He pressed for greater minority rights and for a time blocked the opening of negotiating chapters in Ukraine's EU accession talks.

Magyar also departed from diplomatic convention by choosing the location of his meeting with Zelenskyy in Ukraine: Indeed, it was Magyar who invited Zelenskyy to Transcarpathia.

Magyar's sometimes confrontational tone toward Ukraine can also be explained by the need to avoid appearing too pro-Ukrainian to the Hungarian public.

According to a November 2026 poll conducted by the think tank Globsec, 33% of Hungarians would now support their country providing military aid to Ukraine. Although this is much more than during Orban's time in office, many Hungarians remain skeptical of Ukraine.

That's why Olena Halushka, a Ukrainian political scientist at the International Centre for Ukrainian Victory (ICUV), sees some aspects of Magyar's visit as a concession to public opinion in Hungary.

"Magyar has taken over a deeply polarized country," she told DW's Ukrainian Service. "A visit to Transcarpathia is more of a step for the Hungarian population than for Ukraine."

Ukraine accommodated not only Magyar's choice of location but also Hungary's demands for better protection of minority rights, which were set out in a 10-point plan in June. That's because EU integration is one of the country's top political priorities.

After planned restrictions on the use of minority languages were withdrawn in late 2023, Ukraine's parliament in early October passed a law on minority language rights that in some respects goes even further than European standards.

Hungary subsequently dropped its obstruction of the opening of negotiating clusters for Ukraine's EU accession.

Laszlo Zubanics, chairman of the Hungarian Democratic Federation in Ukraine (UMDSZ, one of two Hungarian political parties in Transcarpathia), told DW that the Magyar-Zelenskyy meeting was "historic."

He particularly welcomed the fact that Ukraine will probably implement practical improvements in the protection of minority rights "not only by the end of 2027, but much sooner, within just a few months."

A Hungarian diplomat who spoke to DW on condition of anonymity also called the meeting "historic," adding that after the Orban government's anti-Ukrainian policies, trust now needs to be rebuilt, and that personal meetings between the two leaders play an important role in that process.

The diplomat went on to say that Hungary not only wants to ensure that the rights of the Hungarian minority are protected, it also has a strong interest in being involved in Ukraine's reconstruction.

Overall, the diplomat concluded, both sides are very keen to expand economic cooperation.

Hungarian political scientist Daniel Hegedus of the Institute for European Politics (IEP) in Berlin told DW that while the meeting was a "symbolic milestone," it will be important to see "how the two political leaders capitalize on the symbolic political capital generated by this meeting."

No observers have suggested that this is the grand finale of reconciliation between Hungary and Ukraine.

Nevertheless, Tuesday's reset sends an important signal to Europe, namely that if the political will is there, a deep gulf that has existed for years can be transformed into a set of diverging interests that can be addressed through negotiations.

Or, as Zelenskyy put it, now there are "all the opportunities to build hopefully constructive relations."

#192. Neuralink receives FDA approval for 'pivotal' brain implant trial in ALS patients

The study will evaluate the efficacity and safety of the company’s LINK device.

Elon Musk's Neuralink has received FDA permission to launch the first "pivotal" trial for its implantable LINK brain-computer interface, the company announced. The study represents a more advanced step in the company's efforts to develop a brain implant that allows people with paralysis to control devices using their brain alone. It will be open to a limited number of patients with ALS (amyotrophic lateral sclerosis), a progressive neurodegenerative disorder that damages nerve cells controlling voluntary muscle movement.

"We've received FDA approval to launch LINK, the first pivotal trial for an implantable brain-computer interface in the US," Neuralink announced on X. "This builds off of our work with 30+ participants with Neuralink to date."

Neuralink hit a milestone last year when the company revealed that its first participant with ALS named Bradford Smith was able to communicate using brain signals alone. "Neuralink has given me freedom, hope, and faster communication. ... It has improved my life so much. I'm so happy to be involved in something big that will help many people," Smith said at the time.

We've received FDA approval to launch LINK, the first pivotal trial for an implantable brain-computer interface in the US. This builds off of our work with 30+ participants with Neuralink to date.

Those with ALS may be eligible. To learn more:https://t.co/dWhb5N4GdR pic.twitter.com/Vor7iXXCBA

— Neuralink (@neuralink) November 25, 2026

The FDA authorization was granted on an experimental basis, but it's a key step forward for Neuralink's LINK brain-implant system. The study will evaluate the ability of patients to control digital devices through thought, "with a focus on functional outcomes that improve independence in daily life," Neuralink wrote. It will also assess the system's long-term safety and reliability.

WIth Neuralink's latest system, the company's N1 implant is surgically placed in the skull, then the company uses its R1 robot to insert flexible electrodes into the brain. Neural activity from the brain associated with movement is then interpreted by software, which translates signals into commands for controlling computers or digital devices. The technology is designed to help patients communicate with the outside world, with the eventual goal to let them operate devices without keyboards or other physical controls.

Neuralink said patients with ALS may be eligible for the LINK trial, but it didn't specify other requirements or the number of participants. It now has approval recruit participants, then conduct the study under an approved clinical protocol. If the technology proves to be safe, the trial may allow Neuralink to apply for more advanced trials and eventually release LINK onto the market.

#191. X is adding its first-ever parental controls

Any responsible adult should probably check on their teen's X usage.

Elon Musk's X has finally introduced parental controls for kids aged 13 to 17, allowing parents and guardians to manage their teen's experience on the platform. Teens' accounts will now default to protected mode, with sensitive media restricted and targeted advertising blocked. The new settings will also give families "additional tools on top of those defaults," the platform announced.

Those extra tools let adults in charge of teens see how much time their kids have spent on X and set a daily limit, require approval for new followers, and limit For You recommendations to accounts the teen already follows. They can also filter specific words, turn off sensitive media content, allow parents to control who can send direct messages, and see which accounts their teen has chatted with one-on-one (without seeing the messages themselves).

To use the new parental controls, follow the guide in X's help center. "Linking starts with the parent or guardian and finishes with the teen. Both users have to agree," the platform states.

X emphasized that the new controls let parents manage content without prying into their kids' lives. "Parents and guardians cannot read private messages, see searches or viewing history, post as their teen, or access the account," the company wrote. Parents will be able to control up to five teens' accounts.

There are reasons that parents should probably keep teens far away from X. In the last five years, it's been rife with issues including hate speech, AI-generated CSAM, misinformation, deepfake images, a lack of moderation, unauthorized AI training, propaganda, algorithms that amplify partisan content, AI bots, human trolls, and the omnipresence of awesome role model Elon Musk.

Even X admits that it's not aimed at kids or teens. "While X is not designed for or promoted to children, and users must be at least 13 or older where required by law, we take extra care with the young people who are here," the company wrote. While claiming that "child safety is a top priority at X," the platform likely introduced the new rules to comply with current or future regulations in multiple states and countries.

#190. Moment Nasa spacecraft hit asteroid - and what happened next

The first images of an asteroid that Nasa deliberately crashed into have revealed a stadium-sized crater on its surface.

Video released by Nasa showed the moment of impact in 2022, while new images show the extent of the damage done to the asteroid.

Dimorphos, the asteroid, posed no threat to Earth, but the crash was a test of our planetary defences to see if it was possible to knock a space rock off course.

Four years on, a European Space Agency (ESA) spacecraft called Hera is scouring the scene to fully assess the aftermath of the collision.

The BBC's science editor Rebecca Morelle explains.

#189. SpaceX gets FCC approval to deploy 1 million satellites for its AI orbital data center

The FCC expects the initiative, called Starmind, to deploy it first satellite by November 2033.

The Federal Communications Commission (FCC) has approved SpaceX's request to deploy 1 million satellites to build its AI orbital data center. SpaceX filed its application at the beginning of the year, presenting the initiative called Starmind as the better alternative for ground-based data centers. In its filing, the company argued that "orbital data centers are the most efficient way to meet the accelerating demand for AI computing power," since they use "solar power with little operating and maintenance costs."

But can space-based data centers for AI really work? Critics doubt they can. Adam Becker, an astrophysicist and a critic of Elon Musk's space and AI ambitions, told AP that "it's not going to happen. The idea behind sending AI data centers outside our planet is so that they don't consume resources their ground-based counterparts do, like energy and water for cooling.

However, critics of the plan argue that space is a vacuum that traps heat inside objects and doesn't make cooling down hardware easier. In space, heat has to leave the satellites via radiation, and Becker explained that there are "basic physical limits" to radiating the heat out for all of them. In addition, that many satellites could cause in-orbit collisions and generate a huge amount of space junk.

FCC Chairman Brendan Carr believes that with this approval, though, America is "leading the world into the era of super intelligence." It opens a "bold new frontier," he added. Under the terms the FCC has approved, SpaceX has permission to deploy satellites into orbital layers spanning 50 kilometers, at altitudes between 500 meters and 1,002 kilometers.

The company is required to deploy at lease one authorized satellite and place it into its assigned orbit by November 25, 2033. By the same date in 2035, it must have already deployed 100,000 satellites to their respective orbits. Three years later, on November 25, 2028, it must have already deployed 500,000 of the authorized satellites. All 1 million satellites must have already been placed in their assigned orbits no later than November 25, 2040. Musk previously said that SpaceX will start launching the satellites in 2027, but we'll have to wait and see if the company can stick to that timeline.

#188. Can Rassvet, 'Russia's Starlink' change the course of the war in Ukraine?

SpaceX's US Starlink satellite system has played a key role in countering the Russian military in Ukraine, but now a Russian counterpart is under development. What could this mean for Ukraine?

Both Russian and international media refer to the satellite communications network Rassvet (Dawn) as the "Russian Starlink," a satellite internet service operated by the US company SpaceX.

Rassvet, which was developed by the Russian aerospace company Bureau 1440, operates in low Earth orbit.

Interest in the system surged after the Russian military lost access to Starlink terminals, which until then had ensured communications and internet access in the battle zone in Ukraine.On the front, for example, Starlink helps drone pilots and commanders receive images from drones equipped with cameras. Military personnel also communicate together thanks to Starlink.

Though Russia does not fall within Starlink's area of coverage, the Russian military managed to acquire "illegal" terminals through third countries and was thus using the same system as the Ukrainian army.

In early 2026, SpaceX, in coordination with the Ukrainian Defense Ministry, introduced a "whitelist," which registered all devices on Ukrainian territory authorized to operate on the Starlink network. Any terminals being used by Russian forces were subsequently deactivated, and reports of serious communication problems on the front then began to appear in Russian social media channels.

In May, the US news channel Bloomberg reported that the Ukrainian army had recaptured about 400 square kilometers (ca. 155 square miles) of Ukrainian territory since Russian forces lost access to Starlink.

To view this video please enable JavaScript, and consider upgrading to a web browser that supports HTML5 video

Two first launches of serial Rassvet satellites took place in March and July 2026.

According to information from Ukraine's military intelligence (HUR) published by the Reuters news agency in early November, Russia has started using Rassvet for military communications over Ukraine and to carry out attacks.

The system is reportedly being used to improve strike accuracy, expand capabilities of reconnaissance drones and to strengthen resilience against electronic warfare.

HUR spokesperson Andriy Yusov also told Reuters that "the constellation is currently insufficient to provide uninterrupted coverage of Ukrainian territory."

Georgiy Trishkin, a rocket analyst and operator of Telegram channel Tekhassky Vestnik (The Texas Herald), told DW that Russia aims to have a functioning "sovereign" alternative to Starlink — consisting of 292 satellites in orbit — running by the end of 2027.

According to Serhii Beskrestnov, aka Flash, an advisor to the Ukrainian president, the main danger posed by the Rassvet system is that it could allow the Russian army to control drones over Ukrainian territory.

He also pointed out on the platform Facebook that the Ukrainian military cannot use Starlink to control drones over Russian territory, since the US service does not work there.

Inspired by the Starlink model, the Russian military also wants to be able to equip drones with terminals, which will enable connectivity in more remote parts of Ukrainian territory. Bureau 1440 unveiled some initial models last month, but according to Trishkin, the similarities between Rassvet and Starlink stop at the "basic features and concept."

"When it comes to details, these are completely different projects. No one can currently match SpaceX's technological edge — and especially its lead in production technology," he said, pointing out that while the Starlink constellation comprised over 10,000 satellites, Rassvet would not have more than 300 in its first phase.

He added that Bureau 1440 would struggle to make enough of the kits used on the ground. While SpaceX can manufacture about 15,000 per day, this would be a "remarkable achievement even over the course of a year" for the Russian company.

He also said that the terminals produced by Bureau 1440 were much more expensive than those by Starlink as they relied "on foreign components."

"Right now, no one can beat SpaceX in terms of price, volume and quality of service," Trishkin said. "The next step for the company is mobile satellite services, where it's making very rapid progress. Will Bureau 1440 be able to respond? Unlikely for the time being."

SpaceX's Starlink was primarily designed as a civilian service, while Starshield is more for government and military sectors.

According to DW sources, Rassvet was likely designed to support battlefield communications and thus does not currently require as many satellites as Starlink.

Ukrainian military expert Oleh Starikov said that if Rassvet continues to develop as it has then it could fulfil the mission put to it by the Russian military. He added that SpaceX CEO Elon Musk was developing a system that would work around the world, whereas Bureau 1440's satellites sufficed for Russian military purposes.

But he also underlined the massive expansion of Russia's satellite network generally, which — on January 1, 2022, before the full-scale invasion of Ukraine — comprised 102 satellites according to Dmitry Rogozin, who at the time was general director of the Russian space agency Roscosmos.

In September of this year, Rogozin's successor Dmitry Bakanov said that the number almost quadrupled.

To view this video please enable JavaScript, and consider upgrading to a web browser that supports HTML5 video

Recent restrictions to Starlink services in Ukraine have brought the issue to the fore again. In mid-November, Ukrainian President Volodymyr Zelenskyy said that Starshield had been throttled in Ukraine.

"Unfortunately, our units have been recording significant disruptions to Starshield's operations over the territory of Ukraine for several days now — along the entire front line and in the temporarily occupied territories of Ukraine," he said, adding that the restrictions occurred at speeds of 161 km/h and higher.

Restrictions on the use of Starlink were first put in place on October 14 and then lifted, according to US President Donald Trump, on October 23.

When asked by DW whether the Ukrainian military could gain access to Rassvet, Trishkin said it was "only a matter of time" before terminals fell "into foreign hands and are subsequently examined."

This article was originally published in Russian.

#187. Backblaze explains why it's sharing customer information with advertisers

Backblaze tells Ars it won’t share users’ backup, storage files with advertisers.

The current policy adds: “Under no circumstances do we rent, trade, or share your address or e-mail address with any other company for their marketing purposes without your consent.” It also makes no mention of advertisers.

This has concerned some users, but a Backblaze spokesperson told Ars Technica that “Backblaze does not sell or share sensory or surveillance data, such as call recordings, for targeted advertising, and we do not disclose it to advertising or marketing partners.” They added:

Notably, no “sensitive personal information,” such as social security numbers, credit card numbers, or “precise geolocation,” is shared with advertisers, the policy states. However, identifiers, commercial information, “Internet or Other Similar Network Activity,” and “geolocation data” may be “sold or shared for targeted Advertising”

Critically, the upcoming policy “only applies to the information collected in respect of your account and not to the content that you store on our system.” Backblaze’s broader terms of service also state that: “Files stored using Backblaze Computer Backup are encrypted prior to being sent to us.”

Renatta Siewert, Backblaze’s director of communications and brand programs, reiterated to Ars that Backblaze doesn’t share the contents of backup or storage files with advertisers:

Customers can use this link to opt out of the selling or sharing of their personal information for cross-context behavioral advertising.

After all, you don’t need to know everything, only what’s important.
