#184. A new approach to a Covid-19 nasal vaccine shows early promise

Scientists in Germany say they’ve been able to make a nasal vaccine that can shut down a Covid-19 infection in the nose and throat, where the virus gets its first foothold in the body.

In experiments in hamsters, two doses of the vaccine – which is made with a live but weakened form of the coronavirus that causes Covid-19 – blocked the virus from copying itself in the animals’ upper airways, achieving “sterilizing immunity” and preventing illness, a long-sought goal of the pandemic.

WHO experts revise Covid-19 vaccine advice, say healthy kids and teens low risk

Although this vaccine has several more hurdles to clear before it gets to a doctor’s office or drug store, other nasal vaccines are in use or are nearing the finish line in clinical trials.

China and India both rolled out vaccines given through the nasal tissues last fall, though it’s not clear how well they may be working. Studies on the effectiveness of these vaccines have yet to be published, leaving much of the world to wonder whether this approach to protection really works in people.

The US has reached something of a stalemate with Covid-19. Even with the darkest days of the pandemic behind us, hundreds of Americans are still dying daily as the infection continues to simmer in the background of our return to normal life.

As long as the virus continues to spread among people and animals, there’s always the potential for it mutate into a more contagious or more damaging version of itself. And while Covid infections have become manageable for most healthy people, they may still pose a danger to vulnerable groups such as the elderly and immunocompromised.

Researchers hope next-generation Covid-19 vaccines, which aim to shut down the virus before it ever gets a chance to make us sick and ultimately prevent the spread of infection, could make our newest resident respiratory infection less of a threat.

China approves its first mRNA Covid-19 vaccine

One way scientists are trying to do that is by boosting mucosal immunity, beefing up immune defenses in the tissues that line the upper airways, right where the virus would land and begin to infect our cells.

It’s a bit like stationing firefighters underneath the smoke alarm in your house, says study author Emanuel Wyler, a scientist at the Max Delbruck Center for Molecular Medicine in the Helmholtz Association in Berlin.

The immunity that’s created by shots works throughout the body, but it resides primarily in the blood. That means it may take longer to mount a response.

“If they are already on site, they can immediately eliminate the fire, but if they’re like 2 miles away, they first need to drive there, and by that time, one-third of the house is already in full flames,” Wyler said.

Mucosal vaccines are also better at priming a different kind of first responder than injections do. They do a better job of summoning IgA antibodies, which have four arms to grab onto invaders instead of the two arms that the y-shaped IgG antibodies have. Some scientists think IgA antibodies may be less picky about their targets than IgG antibodies, which makes them better equipped to deal with new variants.

The new nasal vaccine takes a new approach to a very old idea: weakening a virus so it’s no longer a threat and then giving it to people so their immune systems can learn to recognize and fight it off. The first vaccines using this approach date to the 1870s, against anthrax and rabies. Back then, scientists weakened the agents they were using with heat and chemicals.

The researchers manipulated the genetic material in the virus to make it harder for cells to translate. This technique, called codon pair deoptimization, hobbles the virus so it can be shown to the immune system without making the body sick.

“You could imagine reading a text … and every letter is a different font, or every letter is a different size, then the text is much harder to read. And this is basically what we do in codon pair deoptimization,” Wyler said.

In the hamster studies, which were published Monday in the journal Nature Microbiology, two doses of the live but weakened nasal vaccine created a much stronger immune response than either two doses of an mRNA-based vaccine or one that uses an adenovirus to ferry the vaccine instructions into cells.

The researchers think the live weakened vaccine probably worked better because it closely mimics the process of a natural infection.

The nasal vaccine also previews the entire coronavirus for the body, not just its spike proteins like current Covid-19 vaccines do, so the hamsters were able to make immune weapons against a wider range of targets.

As promising as all this sounds, vaccine experts say caution is warranted. This vaccine still has to pass more tests before it’s ready for use, but they say the results look encouraging.

Health officials consider another round of bivalent boosters for the most vulnerable Americans

“They did a very nice job. This is obviously a competent and thoughtful team that did this work, and impressive in the scope of what they did. Now it just needs to be repeated,” perhaps in primates and certainly in humans before it can be widely used, said Dr. Greg Poland, who designs vaccines at the Mayo Clinic. He was not involved in the new research.

The study began in 2021, before the Omicron variant was around, so the vaccine tested in these experiments was made with the original strain of the coronavirus. In the experiments, when they infected animals with Omicron, the live but weakened nasal vaccine still performed better than the others, but its ability to neutralize the virus was diminished. Researchers think it will need an update.

It also needs to be tested in humans, and Wyler says they’re working on that. The scientists have partnered with a Swiss company called RocketVax to start phase I clinical trials.

Other vaccines are further along, but the progress has been “slow and halting,” Poland said. Groups working on these vaccines are struggling to raise the steep costs of getting a new vaccine to market, and they’re doing it in a setting where people tend to think the vaccine race has been won and done.

In reality, Poland said, we’re far from that. All it would take is another Omicron-level shift in the evolution of the virus, and we could be back at square one, with no effective tools against the coronavirus.

“That’s foolish. We should be developing a pan-coronavirus vaccine that does induce mucosal immunity and that is long-lived,” he said.

At least four nasal vaccines for Covid-19 have reached late-stage testing in people, according to the World Health Organization’s vaccine tracker.

The nasal vaccines in use in China and India rely on harmless adenoviruses to ferry their instructions into cells, although effectiveness data for these has not been published.

Two other nasal vaccines are finishing human studies.

Covid vaccine maker Novavax says it may not survive

One, a recombinant vaccine that can be produced cheaply in chicken eggs, the same way many flu vaccines are, is being put through its paces by researchers at Mount Sinai in New York City.

Another, like the German vaccine, uses a live but weakened version of the virus. It’s being developed by a company called Codagenix. Results of those studies, which were carried out in South America and Africa, may come later this year.

The German team says it’s eagerly watching for the Codagenix data.

“They will be very important in order to know where whether this kind of attempt is basically promising or not,” Wyler said.

They have reason to worry. Respiratory infections have proved to be tough targets for inhaled vaccines.

FluMist, a live but weakened form of the flu virus, works reasonably well in children but doesn’t help adults as much. The reason is thought to be that adults already have immune memory for the flu, and when the virus is injected into the nose, the vaccine mostly boosts what’s already there.

Sign up here to get The Results Are In with Dr. Sanjay Gupta every Tuesday from the CNN Health team.

Still, some of the most potent vaccines such as the vaccine against measles, mumps and rubella use live attenuated viruses, so it’s a promising approach.

Another consideration is that live vaccines can’t be taken by everyone. People with very compromised immunity are often cautioned against using live vaccines because even these very weakened viruses may be risky for them.

“Although it’s strongly attenuated, it’s still a real virus,” Wyler said, so it would have to be used carefully.

#183. The Screens app will let you turn your iPhone Duo into an itty bitty MacBook

The app’s latest update will turn half the display into a keyboard for tiny typing.

Edovia's Screens is a remote desktop app, which gives you a way to control your computer for the iPhone, iPad, another Mac or even the Vision Pro. Screens 5.10.6, its latest update, adds the ability to turn your iPhone Duo into a tiny MacBook when it's in a partially open, clamshell configuration. When the phone is folded halfway, you can use the app's new Laptop Mode to display your remote desktop on the upper half of the screen and a keyboard with trackpad below.

Its keyboard supports multiple languages, and you can swipe down on keys to see alternative characters like you can on the iPad. The trackpad supports Screens' gestures. Of course, they're not a complete replacement for actual physical keyboards and trackpads, but they could be good enough to use in a pinch, or even regularly if you're used to typing on a screen or don't need to type anything lengthy.

The iPhone Duo's form factor is so unlike previous iPhones that we'd love to see more fun ways developers can use it to create unique experiences for the device's users. Some developers, however, might not find it that easy: Instagram's Adam Mosseri recently said that it will be "pretty challenging" to design Instagram "in a way that feels great" for the device.

#182. Man sentenced to 47 months in prison after using AI to impersonate streamer N3on

David Anthony Sanchez scammed victims out of $260,000 worth of cryptocurrency.

A Florida man who is known as "Dave the Jewler" has been sentenced to 47 months in prison after running a cryptocurrency scam that involved using AI to impersonate a well-known streamer. David Anthony Sanchez, who pleaded guilty to money laundering and aggravated identity theft, was also ordered to pay restitution to the victims.

As spotted by Aftermath, the US Attorney's Office for the Southern District of Florida said Sanchez used AI to clone the voice of Mikyle "N3on" Rafiq in order to convince the people behind a memecoin project that the streamer had agreed to endorse, promote and take part in their scheme. The scammer pretended to be both N3on and the streamer's representative while communicating with the project's organizers.

Sanchez fooled them into transferring around $260,000 worth of cryptocurrency to accounts he controlled. He then converted some of the crypto into US dollars that he used for his personal benefit.

"Behind every fraud scheme are real people who suffer real financial harm," Yara Lorenzo Klukas, acting US Attorney for the Southern District of Florida, said. "In this case, the defendant exploited artificial intelligence to manipulate the trust of unsuspecting victims and deprive them of their money. Today's sentence reflects our commitment to protecting victims, safeguarding the integrity of our financial markets and holding accountable those who exploit emerging technologies to defraud others."

N3on's not exactly a stranger to AI clones of himself, as it happens. Aftermath noted that he tried using a deepfake of himself to keep his Kick stream going around the clock. N3on's likeness was also used in an AI-generated movie that was released a few months ago.

#180. Workers at GTA 6 studio Rockstar Games vote overwhelmingly against union recognition

The IWGB Rockstar Game Workers Union will continue to organize, however.

Rockstar Games workers in the UK have overwhelmingly voted against a union recognition effort, with three-quarters rejecting an agreement with the IWGB Rockstar Game Workers Union. The vote took place at the same time the company released Grand Theft Auto 6, which smashed records as it generated $3.79 billion in pre-orders and first-day sales.

Over 93 percent of Rockstar workers across offices in Edinburgh, Leeds, Lincoln, Dundee and London took part in the two-week ballot. Of those, 1,163 turned down formal recognition of their branch of the IWGB (Independent Workers Union of Great Britain), while 381 were in favor, according to IGN.

The Rockstar Game Workers Union will continue to organize without a formal agreement with the company. However, the result means Rockstar management won't be obligated to officially recognize the union or hold regular meetings with representatives.

"For anyone at Rockstar who believes in a fairer workplace, a fairer sector and a fairer society — this isn't a sign to retreat, it's a sign to double down on what we stand for," the union said in a statement. "Hundreds of our coworkers voted in favor of our union at Rockstar, yet sadly we recognize we have more work to do to build understanding and win the trust of more of our colleagues. We're ready to put in the effort to make that happen." It added that it will keep battling for "pay transparency, contractual overtime pay and protections against AI and layoffs."

"We are extraordinarily proud of our team, the culture we've built together and the launch of Grand Theft Auto 6," Jennifer Kolbe, Rockstar Games head of publishing, said in a statement. "This result reflects our team's strong support for continuing the direct relationships, collaboration and open dialogue that have helped make Rockstar Games so innovative and strong. As we look ahead, we're excited to build on all we've accomplished together."

Rockstar had indicated a willingness to work with its workers on unionization. "We go where the team goes," Klobe told Bloomberg for a story about the making of GTA 6. "So if they want a union, they will have a union."

In October last year, Rockstar fired dozens of workers, claiming they had shared confidential details about GTA 6 and made disparaging remarks about the company. The IWGB issued formal legal claims against Rockstar in which it accused the Take-Two subsidiary of union busting and unlawfully firing those workers. An employment tribunal heard closing evidence last week and a final judgement in the case is pending.

#179. Google search alternative Kagi launches Gmail rival

Additionally, Kagi is rolling out a calendar as part of the mail service. Right now, both the email and calendar interfaces are web-only, but native Android and iOS apps are planned for the fourth quarter of next year.

The email interface, currently in public beta, is minimalist. It emphasizes keyboard commands, as well as a private and ad-free experience. (You can see more details in Kagi’s blog post about what they mean by private.) Other feature offerings include unlimited aliases and domains, an anti-lock-in policy for user data, 50GB of storage, rule-based filtering with Sieve, an MCP server, and several optional, off-by-default AI features, like chatting with an agent to search email history, do large-scale re-labeling, or define additional filtering rules.

Some major features are still missing, though. As noted, there are no native apps yet. Critically, there is no contacts address book, but that is also coming in 2027 (Q1 in this case) according to the roadmap.

Like the search engine, Kagi Mail is a paid service. It’s not included in the $5 per month basic Kagi plan, nor is it in the $10 per month unlimited search plan. It is, however, included in the $25 per month Kagi Ultimate subscription, which also offers frontier AI models.

That’s a lot to pay for email when Gmail has historically been such a strong product. To most people, it seems ridiculous to imagine paying for a subscription, and that’s understandable.

Kagi also argues that Google’s search results—and increasingly its email as well—are polluted or corrupted by ad sales incentives. On that, several Ars staffers agree—my colleague Lee Hutchinson wrote at length about why he ditched Google Search for Kagi a year ago. A couple other Ars staffers have since made the same move, myself included—but to be fair, search is a very big part of our day-to-day work, and that’s not true to the same degree for everybody.

The pitch for email is a little harder, because most people probably don’t perceive as much impact of Google’s business incentives on Gmail. That has maybe changed recently, as Google has begun inserting Gemini into Gmail aggressively, but Kagi has AI features, too—the difference is that they’re generally more conservative and sensible. (You can disable either Gemini in Gmail or Kagi’s AI features in Kagi Mail, too.)

Based on a few hours of use, Kagi Mail seems like it’s going in the right direction, but it will need refinement to permanently unseat Gmail. Your needs may vary, but there are many good reasons to consider alternatives to Google’s business model and products.

Ars Technica has been separating the signal from the noise for over 25 years. With our unique combination of technical savvy and wide-ranging interest in the technological arts and sciences, Ars is the trusted source in a sea of information. After all, you don’t need to know everything, only what’s important.

#178. NVIDIA reportedly halts AI chip shipments to Southeast Asia

Companies like Dell have stopped shipping server hardware to Malaysia, Singapore, Thailand and Indonesia.

NVIDIA has reportedly put a stop to shipments of AI server hardware to four Southeast Asian countries. According to The Financial Times, a handful of the company's manufacturing partners, including Dell and Supermicro, told clients in Malaysia, Singapore, Thailand and Indonesia that the chipmaker is not allowing their server orders to be sent to them. The Financial Times reports it's unclear if the halt is temporary. The news comes just days after NVIDIA reinstated Jensen Huang as its chief executive.

In recent years, Chinese AI labs have turned to Malaysia, Singapore, Thailand and Indonesia to gain access to NVIDIA's most powerful chips. They used a gap in US export controls by renting access through cloud compute providers that bought servers with NVIDIA GPUs inside. In recent weeks, the US Commerce Department moved to close that loophole. First by introducing new rules aimed at stopping that exact practice, and second by targeting Megaspeed, the company at the center of the NVIDIA scandal.

On Wednesday, the Commerce Department added Megaspeed to its "Entity List." Companies, including NVIDIA, now need a US license to ship goods covered by US export rules to Megaspeed. Huang had resigned from his position on November 12 after reports surfaced that he had engaged in multiple extramarital affairs, including one involving Alice Huang, a former Megaspeed executive that some US officials suspect was a Chinese intelligence asset. Megaspeed had leased access to NVIDIA chips to Chinese companies. The Justice Department is also investigating whether Megaspeed broke US export rules.

Under the new US rule, data centers that were already providing large amounts of computing power to companies based in China or other location covered by the rule have 90 days from November 19 to get a license if they want to keep doing so. "There's this new rule but no guidance on execution," one cloud executive told the outlet. "No one knows how to apply for this license while the 90-day clock is ticking. It's causing sheer chaos in the region." Demand from Chinese tech companies led to a data center construction boom in Southeast Asia. NVIDIA booked $104 billion in revenue through its Singapore subsidiary in 2025, representing a 72 percent increase from the year before.

#177. Amazon workers around the world plan strikes and protests on Black Friday and Cyber Monday

Protests are planned in Los Angeles and Chicago, while a union called for strikes at 11 Amazon sites in Germany.

Amazon workers and their supporters are once again set to go on strike and hold protests in more than 20 countries across the Black Friday and Cyber Monday period. Trade union federation UNI Global said the workers are pushing Amazon to negotiate with unions and respect employees' right to organize.

Teamsters are planning protests on Cyber Monday (November 30) in Los Angeles and Chicago. "As part of a global day of action, Amazon workers are demanding fair wages, safer working conditions, union recognition and respect on the job," a local Teamsters union representing workers in Southern California said.

German trade union Verdi called strikes at 11 Amazon sites on Black Friday (November 27). It claimed the wages the company pays often aren't enough for workers to live on and that warehouse work puts a "considerable physical strain on employees." According to Deutsche Presse-Agentur, Verdi also said Amazon uses digital trackers to keep close tabs on around 40,000 employees. An Amazon spokesman denied Verdi's claims and said average starting pay was above the German minimum wage.

Amazon workers and their allies are also set to mobilize in Canada, Australia, India, Bangladesh, Poland, Luxembourg, Brazil, Colombia, Uruguay, Indonesia, Japan, the Philippines, Thailand, Fiji, Singapore, South Africa and elsewhere. UNI Global said this is the seventh year of the Make Amazon Pay campaign, which it convenes alongside Progressive International. Engadget has contacted Amazon for comment.

Earlier this month, workers at a warehouse in Delta, British Columbia, became the first in North America to win a union contract with Amazon. The terms of the three-year pact were imposed through binding arbitration. Among other things, the contract ensures pay increases (up to $29 CAD per hour by the time it ends), an extra week of vacation for workers with 10 years of service and severance of up to 18 weeks.

British Columbia's labor relations board ordered arbitration in August despite Amazon's objections. After an arbitrator imposed the agreement, Amazon vice-president J. Ofori Agboka said the process was "improper [and] unfair" and that it "defies common sense," adding that the company would take the issue to court if need be.

In April, the NLRB ordered Amazon to recognize and bargain with a union at the JFK8 warehouse on Staten Island, New York. The company is contesting the order. JFK8 workers voted to unionize in 2022, becoming the first Amazon warehouse in the US to do so.

#176. Russia-Ukraine war extends to the Baltic and the Mediterranean

By attacking Russian vessels around the world, Kyiv is trying to up the pressure on Moscow and also sending out signals to its Western allies. What is the impact on global maritime trade?

First there was an attack on a Russian cargo vessel, the Sparta II, off the Finnish coast, then on the Russian corvette Merkury and a dry cargo vessel that was allegedly carrying weapons off the coast of Libya. These spectacular Ukrainian operations in regions far from the Ukrainian capital Kyiv have caused a stir. They came shortly after several suspected Russian missiles crashed in Poland, not far from where an international military exercise was underway.

Intelligence agencies have long warned that Russia was preparing some form of provocation along NATO's eastern flank.

What are the risks of Russia's war in Ukraine escalating further and drawing in other regions?

It is no secret that Russia has long since expanded its operations beyond Ukraine: With military provocation, hybrid operations and acts of sabotage, Moscow has attempted to put pressure on the countries that openly support Ukraine.

And Ukraine has also targeted Russia's military and economy beyond its borders.

Since the summer, attacks by both Russia and Ukraine have increased in the Black Sea. Not only have Russian and Ukrainian cargo ships been targeted, but also vessels from other countries, particularly Turkey.

Furthermore, in the past few years, Ukraine has managed to deal significant blows to Russia, deep within Russian territory, thanks to aerial and maritime drones. It has targeted military airfields and other strategic targets such as oil refineries, including one in Omsk, Siberia, as well as military vessels in the eastern Black Sea.

Russia has access only to a very narrow strip of the Baltic Sea coast, which is flanked by Finland and Estonia. Even ahead of the attack on the Sparta II, Ukraine had struck in the area multiple times, targeting oil terminals in the region around St. Petersburg, in Ust-Luga and Primorsk.

Russia uses the Baltic Sea to transport supplies to its Kaliningrad exclave and to export oil and other goods to the rest of the world, with the help of its "shadow fleet."

Ukrainian special forces are also said to have carried out operations against Russian military facilities in Syria, well before the fall of the Assad regime in December 2024. They are also reported to have lent support to local Tuareg fighters in Mali who were able to inflict heavy losses on the Malian army and allied Russian mercenaries.

Kyiv is thus attempting to harm Russia's military and economy worldwide, even if in military terms this does not help Ukraine directly.

The Russian corvette that was sunk was part of Russia's Black Sea Fleet but because of Turkey's blockade of the Bosporus and the Dardanelles, it would not have been allowed to return to the Black Sea. Instead, it was escorting Russian cargo ships in the Mediterranean. Primarily, the attacks send a political message: Russian ships can no longer feel safe — anywhere in the world.

To view this video please enable JavaScript, and consider upgrading to a web browser that supports HTML5 video

"We are not speaking about random shadow vessels which simply transport oil above the price cap and as a result bring revenues for the Russian treasury," security expert Elisabeth Braw from the Atlantic Council told DW. Sparta II was suspected of carrying military equipment, which Moscow denies.

"Ukraine is showing that it knows exactly, and secondly that it's able to hit them wherever and whatever waters they are in," Braw continued. "And this is something I don't think Ukraine has been able to do at the beginning of the war."

Officially, Kyiv's goal is to disrupt Russian logistics and supply routes, but military experts and politicians also see the attacks as a clear political signal to Western allies. They suspect that Ukrainian President Volodymyr Zelenskyy is sending a clear message of strength with an eye toward possible future peace negotiations.

In an interview on the German news outlet Tagesschau, security expert Joachim Weber from the University of Bonn suggested that Zelenskyy was paying less attention to the US, out of a sense of disappointment. He said that Ukrainian attacks in distant maritime areas were a sign that there was less inhibition and that Ukraine was ready to expand the conflict geographically to increase pressure on Russia, despite the risks.

Weber warned that war could become omnipresent if there was an increase in attacks on civilian vessels or ships that were considered suspicious in international waters. He said that Russia was providing more military escorts for the ships of its shadow fleet. "The likelihood that an accidental exchange of fire between armed forces, coast guards or anyone could occur is rising."

Braw pointed out that violations of the Law of the Sea were not only rising with regard to the Russia-Ukraine war but also in relation to other conflicts and playing out in "the South China Sea, the Strait of Hormuz and the Red Sea."

She said about 90% of global trade today was conducted via the oceans and she feared that others might conclude it was not "really to their advantage" to follow the rules of international maritime law.

She added that they might find it "beneficial in the short term to violate maritime rules" for short-term gains. "That would make the world's oceans significantly less safe for everybody."

This article was originally written in German.

#175. The $500 billion beauty industry's 'green' ambitions are a patchwork at best. And they're falling short

The escalating climate crisis is shifting many people’s purchasing patterns and this extends to the $500 billion dollar global beauty industry which is grappling with a range of sustainability challenges across product manufacturing, packaging and disposal.

'Delay means death': We're running out of ways to adapt to the climate crisis, new report shows. Here are the key takeaways

Strategy and consulting firm Simon Kucher’s Global Sustainability Study 2021 found 60% of consumers around the world rated sustainability as an important purchase criterion, and 35% were willing to pay more for sustainable products or services.

This shift in consumer preferences has propelled many beauty brands to set environmental goals: to move away from single-use and virgin plastics, provide recyclable, reusable and refillable packaging and offer more transparency around products’ ingredients so customers can ascertain how “green” their purchase is.

However, consumers still struggle to understand the sustainability credentials of many products, according to the British Beauty Council. This is because the industry’s clean-up efforts have been inconsistent, and fall short of making a recognizable impact in the absence of collective goal-setting, global strategy and standardized regulations.

There is no international standard for the beauty industry on how much product ingredient information to share with customers — or how to do so. Brands can set their own rules and goals, giving rise to confusion and “greenwashing,” where sustainability claims are often touted but not substantiated.

Companies often use marketing language like “clean beauty” to make it seem like their products are natural, for example, when they may not actually be organic, sustainable or ethically made.

After rebranding in the West, many beauty companies are still offering to 'whiten' skin elsewhere

“The term ‘clean beauty’ has become quite dangerous. It’s used to sell more products,” according to British Beauty Council CEO Millie Kendall, who added that such buzzwords are losing traction in the UK as British customers wise up to their shortcomings. “Customers need better marketing information and certification information.”

In a 2021 report calling on the industry to have “the courage to change” their business practices, the British Beauty Council wrote that, all too often, even natural ingredients involved in manufacturing products give way to “over-consumption, non-regenerative farming practices, pollution, waste and neglect.”

“The only way out of this is transparency,” Kendall told CNN.

Jen Lee, chief impact officer at US-based brand Beautycounter, said she continues to see confusion over ingredients among consumers. (In 2013, the company launched and published “The Never List,” which currently cites more than 2,800 chemicals — including heavy metals, parabens and formaldehyde — it claims to never use in its products.)

“Natural vs. synthetic ingredients has been a conversation. People think natural is safer, but it’s not always the case,” Lee explained. “Natural ingredients formulated in the industry can have toxic load. Heavy metals can occur in natural components of the earth.”

Mother loses peripheral vision from apparent exposure to mercury in beauty creams. Toxic levels in her home put family at risk, say experts

“We used to be more natural and organic,” added Sasha Plavsic, founder of makeup brand ILIA Beauty. “What was challenging is (that) raw materials were difficult to source or would come in inconsistently or products wouldn’t perform.”

Most makeup is created and molded at high temperatures, Plavsic explained. Purely organic materials often fall apart in this heat, leading to inconsistent results and subpar product performance. “Not every synthetic is bad,” Plavsic said. “Sometimes, it helps create the best in class formula.”

The industry’s plastic packaging is a particular sustainability challenge — 95% is thrown away and the vast majority is not recycled, according to the British Beauty Council.

The cosmetics business is the fourth biggest plastic packaging user globally — after food and beverage, industrial packaging and pharmaceuticals — and plastic is about 67% of the industry’s packaging volume, according to Vantage Market Research. Beauty giant L’Oreal used 144,430 metric tons of plastic in its packaging material in 2021, for example, according to the Ellen Macarthur Foundation (EMF). Estee Lauder Companies reported its brands produced 71,600 metric tons of plastic in product packaging that same year.

And only 9% of the global plastic waste is recycled, according to a report from the Organisation for Economic Co-operation and Development. The United States only recycles 4% of its plastic waste.

Single-use plastic is wreaking havoc on the planet. Here's what you can do to minimize your impact

Many brands are trying to phase out harmful plastics from their operations and adopt post-consumer recycled (PCR) plastic. (L’Oreal has set a target of 50% PCR plastic usage by 2025, while Estee Lauder is targeting 25% “or more” PCR plastic — but both are far from achieving their targets.)

“Between 60-70 major global brands have made unprecedented progress” in PCR plastic usage across industries, EMF’s Plastic Initiative Lead Sander DeFruyt told CNN. But DeFruyt stressed that PCR plastic must be adopted in conjunction with brands removing single and virgin plastics from their usage cycles to truly make a difference.

However, PCR plastic is not easy to find — low recycling rates around the world mean there is limited supply. Meanwhile, demand for it is growing demand across industries, DeFruyt said. This competition hikes up its price, which is already higher than virgin plastic.

Hair care brand FEKKAI claims that it used up to 95% PCR content in its packaging, but pricing and supply issues posed a challenge, forcing it to currently aim for containers and packaging that feature at least 50% PCR in its packaging.

“PCR plastic is more expensive than stock plastic. The cost is hard and then sourcing it is too,” founder Frédéric Fekkai told CNN. “PCR is close to our heart, but there is a massive demand, so finding recycled plastic is difficult.”

Beauty retailers plays a pivotal — and under-utilized — role, with control over stocking decisions and supply chains. But many vary when it comes to the standards they set for brands they sell.

“Smaller businesses do more, full stop,” said Jessi Baker, founder of the technology platform Provenance, which helps brands display their sustainability credentials for customers. “They move more nimbly. Some of them are born-good brands — climate friendliness was part of their setup. They don’t need to restructure their entire supply chain. Their culture already has it compared to the larger brands who need to work hard to change.”

A history of beauty trends -- and the standards that shaped them

Sephora launched its “Clean + Planet Positive” initiative in 2021, which labeled products that met its set criteria. (This is separate from the French retailer’s “Clean at Sephora” program, which is currently facing a consumer lawsuit alleging it carries a significant percentage of products understood by customers to be harmful.) Target launched a similar program in 2022, featuring a “Target Zero” icon for both online and in-store offerings that either have reusable, recyclable, compostable or reduced plastic packaging, or feature waterless or concentrated products.

Still, many steps taken by brands and retailers do not even begin to touch on the waste and pollution generated throughout supply chains, manufacturing and shipping, all huge problems for the industry to grapple with.

The gaps in standardization in the beauty ecosystem can, to some extent, be filled by certifications such as the US-born B Corporation, or B Corp. This accreditation, one of the most well-known in the beauty space, is issued by the non-profit B Lab, which scores a company on a variety of criteria around ethics and sustainability. However beneficial it may be among eco-conscious consumers, though, it is currently completely voluntary for brands to apply for.

Governments and multinationals enforcing regulations and setting a base line for brands to operate from when making sustainability claims would go a long way to making change, many experts and business leaders believe.

Susanne Kaufmann, founder of her namesake beauty brand, says her efforts in Austria would reap better results if more countries around the world had stricter, more uniform garbage disposal laws.

A trash heap 62 meters high shows the scale of India's climate challenge

“I package our product in a recyclable material,” Kaufmann said. (Her products’ packaging, which is refillable and reusable, is made from 75% recycled plastic — and is 100% recyclable.) If I send this to the US, the garbage is not separated… and it’s not recyclable,” she explained, referring to inconsistencies in recycling laws across the United States.

And when it comes to ingredients, the European Chemicals Agency lists 2,495 substances banned from use in cosmetic products marketed for sale or use in the bloc. But the US Food and Drug administration only lists 11, making it more challenging for American consumers to find safer, greener options. The Environmental Working Group, a non-profit watchdog, studied lab tests of 51 sunscreen products in 2021 and found that only 35% of products met the EU standard, compared with 94% that passed the US standard.

However, while government can set minimum requirements, Mia Davis, vice president of sustainability and impact at beauty retailer Credo Beauty, says the needle will move in the private sector.

Sustainable ships: The world's most eco-conscious cruises

“Regulation can raise the floor a bit. A person who doesn’t know about any (sustainability issues) should still be able to walk into a bodega and get clean products… But that’s never going to be what the market can do,” she said. “Market leadership is key.”

In the absence of bold regulations or global standards on sustainability practices, this “leadership” — undertaken both by brands and customers in the beauty marketplace — is likely to be the most immediately impactful vector for addressing the industry’s climate shortcomings. It will take continued collective advocacy and initiative to see meaningful climate-conscious change.

#174. 1Password says it was hacked but users' vaults are safe

The attacker wasn't able to access people's vaults.

1Password has admitted that an attacker was able to infiltrate its system and steal some customer data, but it has assured users in its announcement that their vaults and logins are safe. On November 19, the company started investigating signs that an employee's corporate GitHub account had been compromised. The company found that the attacker used that account to access its source code and inject malicious code to its backend.

The attacker was then able to download customers' data, including people's names and email addresses, the dates their accounts were created and their last sign-in times. They were also able to steal users' public keys, but 1Password said it has no impact to their security. While the bad actor was also able to steal source code from the company itself, it doesn't contain customer data.

"Your vault data, the items in them, and the keys that protect them are safe and were not affected," 1Password wrote. The service doesn't have access to users' actual passwords, recovery keys and other credentials, as well as the secret keys that protect their vaults, so the attacker wasn't able to steal them. Since discovering the breach, 1Password has revoked the stolen token, disabled the affected GitHub account and added new detection tools for similar attacks.

While 1Password said that users' vaults remain safe, the company still advises being vigilant when it comes to phishing attempts. It warned that since users' emails were compromised in the breach, they could be used in attempts to grab people's passwords through fake account recovery and invitation emails.

#173. Musk's plan to send a million SpaceX data satellites into space approved

SpaceX is an aerospace company that builds rockets and spacecraft, as well as running satellite services

US regulators have given SpaceX the go-ahead to launch a million satellites into Earth's orbit to help build data centres in space - the first approval of its kind.

The company, which is owned by Elon Musk, wants to use sunlight to power satellites that will process data for AI and send the results back to Earth.

The Federal Communications Commission (FCC) said on Wednesday that the project, known as Starmind, would "support America's booming super intelligence economy".

However, rival firm Amazon has criticised it as a "lofty ambition rather than a real plan", while astronomers in the US said the move would "significantly undermine" the public's ability to study and observe the night sky.

There is increasing opposition to data centres on earth, including in the US where experts say they were a factor in the Republicans big losses in the midterm elections.

SpaceX believes the "orbital data centres" are the most cost and energy-efficient way to meet the growing demand for artificial intelligence (AI) computing power that is beyond "terrestrial capabilities".

FCC Chairman Brendan Carr said in a statement that the project "opens a bold new frontier for the computing infrastructure needed to power America's leadership and growth".

"This is the kind of American ingenuity that can unite our strengths in space and advanced technology, reinforce our economic leadership, and expand prosperity here at home," Carr said.

Responding to the announcement, Musk wrote on X: "Much appreciated. This will be a great asset to America and the world."

Did a data centre revolt affect the US midterm election results?

Why Canada is embracing AI data centres while the US pushes back

Under the FCC licence, deployment of the satellites must begin no later than November 2033. SpaceX will be required to have deployed 10% by November 2035, rising to 50% by November 2038.

The company has already said it wants to launch its first Starmind satellite next year.

It is unclear whether a million satellites will indeed be launched or whether SpaceX has sought approval for a higher number than it will use.

Like SpaceX's current Starlink network, which provides high-speed internet via more than 10,000 satellites, Starmind's satellites will operate in low-Earth orbit at altitudes ranging between 500-2,000km (310-1,242 miles).

Starmind's satellites will communicate with Starlink's and then relay the data to Earth.

The plans to put data centres in orbit and on the Moon

Speaking to the Today programme, on BBC Radio 4, Prof Sir Martin Sweeting, founder and executive chairman of Surrey Satellite Technology Ltd, said while the idea of data centres in space seemed "technically feasible", whether it "makes sense at scale yet" still needed to be analysed.

Being able to get the data from the network of satellites, he added, would depend on being able to use laser links from the ground to them.

"That has not yet been perfected to the level at which it would be able to support the huge amounts of data that are likely to be transferred backwards and forwards," he said.

Meanwhile, the American Astronomical Society (AAS) said it was "disheartened and dismayed" by the decision.

It said the proposed constellation would "significantly undermine decades of federal investment in astronomical facilities" as well as "the ability of astronomers and the public to enjoy and study the night sky".

It noted that the constellation "involves larger (and therefore brighter) and more numerous objects", meaning the satellites would "be visible as extremely bright objects for longer periods in the night sky".

Jan Hasselman, a senior attorney at the non-profit group Earthjustice, said in a statement: "Drastically expanding satellites in space has a direct impact on people's everyday lives as well as the future of our planet."

The BBC visited Arizona before the US midterm elections to understand America's data-centre boom

The orbiting data centre concept is also being explored by other firms - including Google and Blue Origin - and is being pitched as a greener alternative to traditional centres, which require enormous amounts of power to run and water for cooling.

The ever-expanding use of AI computing has seen a massive increase in the amount of data that needs to be stored and processed around the world.

New facilities continue to be built but it is becoming difficult to find places to put them. Data centres are large and sprawling, use enormous amounts of power and water for cooling, and increasingly local people don't want them built nearby.

An expert previously told the BBC that launching hardware into orbit remains expensive and that the infrastructure to protect, cool and power them can be complex - while a growing quantity of space debris puts the physical hardware at risk.

Separately, another cautioned that the growing number of low-orbit craft increases the possibility of crashes between objects - which could damage machines or send materials falling back to earth.

Starlink has been accused of creating congestion in space, something Musk denies. He has also rejected claims his satellites have been taking up too much room and crowding out competitors.

Big, noisy and coming to a town near you - why the data centre backlash is growing

Bezos' Blue Origin announces satellite rival to Musk's Starlink

#172. European spacecraft finds chaotic scene after asteroid deflection experiment

“DART made a really, really large mess. However, we didn’t really know what kind of mess.”

Four years ago, NASA dispatched a small spacecraft on a suicide mission to show how humanity might deflect an asteroid on a collision course with Earth. The first-of-its-kind experiment worked beautifully as the solar-powered space probe slammed into asteroid Dimorphos, permanently altering its trajectory.

Using telescopes, astronomers measured changes in the orbit of Dimorphos around a larger asteroid companion named Didymos in the aftermath of NASA’s Double Asteroid Redirection Test (DART) mission. The spacecraft released some 10 billion joules of energy as it impacted Dimorphos in September 2022 at more than 13,700 mph (6.14 kilometers per second), shortening the asteroid’s 12-hour orbit around Didymos by about 33 minutes, 25 times the most conservative expectation.

But scientists could only learn so much from DART. The collision destroyed the spacecraft, as expected. DART recorded images as it took aim on Dimorphos, but only faraway telescopes and a small CubeSat that briefly detected the impact flash could see what happened next.

“We knew right away that the impact was successful, that DART made a really, really large mess,” said Sabina Raducan, a planetary scientist at the International Space Science Institute in Bern, Switzerland. “However, we didn’t really know what kind of mess. We were still missing pieces of the puzzle.”

The European Space Agency’s Hera spacecraft recently arrived in the vicinity of Didymos and Dimorphos after a two-year journey from a launch pad in Florida. On Wednesday, ESA released Hera’s first views of the scar left by DART on Dimorphos. The images show the car-sized impactor carved out a crater the size of a stadium, nearly as big as Dimorphos itself, a rugged chunk of extraterrestrial rock measuring about 500 feet (151 meters) across.

Mission planners selected Didymos and Dimorphos for the deflection experiment because they are similar to many near-Earth asteroids, but there’s no risk of them striking our home planet. Scientists weren’t sure what Hera would find when it got to the double asteroid, currently orbiting the Sun about 120 million miles (193.5 million kilometers) from Earth.

“I would say we had two kinds of predictions: either we made a crater, or we reshaped the asteroid,” said Patrick Michel, Hera’s principal investigator from Côte d’Azur Observatory in Nice, France. “In fact, we did both because the crater is so large that the shape has changed. Therefore, we are in a sort of a transition between just cratering and global transformation, and we are still not sure how this can happen.”

Hera found Didymos, the larger of the two asteroids, spinning on its axis just as DART observed on final approach in 2022. Dimorphos is no longer so stable.

“As Hera returned more images, it became clear that the DART impact has left Dimorphos tumbling wildly in its orbit,” says Michael Kueppers, ESA’s Hera mission scientist.

The Hera spacecraft will fly closer to Didymos and Dimorphos over the next six months to obtain more detailed observations. Hera will release two small sub-satellites to attempt landings on Dimorphos and collect data on the asteroid’s composition, internal structure, and mass.

Scientists will use this information to reconstruct the dynamics of DART’s impact four years ago and better understand how a future deflection mission might move an asteroid off a trajectory that actually threatens Earth. Near-Earth asteroids come in many shapes and sizes, with different densities and makeups, so knowledge of Dimorphos’s physical properties will anchor predictive computer models teaching scientists how best to deflect all types of asteroids.

“Once we understand, we have a breakthrough, which really solves planetary defense,” Michel said in an interview with Ars.

In its initial reconnaissance this month, Hera spotted hundreds of boulders surrounding the double asteroid. This was not entirely unexpected. A picture taken by the Hubble Space Telescope shortly after DART’s impact showed a cloud of material streaming behind Didymos and Dimorphos, but scientists were astonished by the extent of the debris swarm in Hera’s first images.

“As we approached Didymos and Dimorphos, we quickly realized the asteroids were not alone,” said Naomi Murdoch, planetary scientist at the Institut Supérieur de l’Aéronautique et de l’Espace in Toulouse, France. “In fact, they had a lot of company.”

An image released by ESA on Wednesday combines 11 long exposure frames from Hera’s hyperspectral camera. Image processors on the ground digitally removed the relatively bright Didymos and Dimorphos to reveal a panoply of dimmer streaks and dots, each of which is a rock fragment ranging in size from a football to a washing machine, Murdoch said.

“Hera came to visit two asteroids, and we found hundreds of companions,” Murdoch said.

One obvious explanation for the boulders is they were thrown off Dimorphos when DART slammed into it. But scientists cautioned they need more information before they can say that with surety. Hera is the first space mission to undertake a sustained study of an asteroid with its own moon. The debris around the two asteroids might be relics from the creation of Dimorphos some time in the ancient past.

“Hera will allow us to study that in more detail,” Murdoch said. “We really need to know more about these boulders and characterize them to understand if they’re linked to DART, if they’re linked to another process, and what the consequences might be for other asteroid systems in the Solar System.”

The boulder field will create challenges for navigating around the asteroids. In particular, the boulders are an added risk for the two small CubeSats Hera is supposed to deploy in January.

“We saw a couple of images that didn’t reassure me,” said Margherita Cardi, project manager for the Milani CubeSat. “There is a mix of excitement and panic.”

Claudia Balossi, spacecraft systems engineer for Hera’s other CubeSat, named Juventas, said her mission’s planned trajectory would take it right through the boulders as it moves within a couple of miles of the asteroids, then finally toward a landing attempt on Dimorphos, where it will measure the miniature world’s tenuous gravity field.

“We are right in the middle of it,” Balossi said. “The smallest ones are probably just as big as we are and the biggest ones are very big. In the worst case, a boulder will land on us. We will activate our gravimeter right away [if that happens],” Balossi joked.

The good news is the asteroids’ gravity is so weak that Hera, Milani, Juventas, and the hazards around them all move at snail-like speeds. Hera’s current trajectory takes it around the asteroids at a relative velocity of just a quarter-mile per hour, or about 12 centimeters per second. At that speed, there’s no danger of a sudden catastrophic hypervelocity collision between the spacecraft and the boulders lurking nearby.

Scientists have seen this at an asteroid before. NASA’s OSIRIS-REx spacecraft kicked up an estimated six tons of rock and dust when it gently moved in to collect a sample from an asteroid named Bennu in 2020. Low gravity and weak surface cohesion are a recipe for outsized effects.

“These types of bodies, because of their low gravity, have a very counterintuitive behavior,” Michel said. “Each time we interact with them, whether to take a sample or to make an impact, the response of the surface was not the one we predicted, simply because our intuition relies on what we experience every day on Earth. Even if the rules of physics are the same in the Universe, they express very differently depending on local attraction.”

Ars Technica has been separating the signal from the noise for over 25 years. With our unique combination of technical savvy and wide-ranging interest in the technological arts and sciences, Ars is the trusted source in a sea of information. After all, you don’t need to know everything, only what’s important.

#171. Germany: 2 arrested, suspected of plotting synagogue attack

Federal prosecutors say they have ordered the arrest of two members of a pro-Iranian group on suspicion of planning an attack on a synagogue. They were detained in the cities of Heidelberg and Cologne.

Germany's federal prosecutor's office on Thursday said it had ordered the arrest of two Iraqi citizens in Germany who are believed to be members of a pro-Iranian terrorist group.

It identified the men as Mostafa Al-R. and Ameer J., and said that they were arrested in Heidelberg in Baden-Württemberg in the southwest, and in Cologne, several hours' drive to the north.

Officers from Germany's federal criminal police force carried out the arrests.

Prosecutors also announced searches and raids of 17 sites in total across three separate states, saying these were ongoing.

After two suspects were arrested, Hendrik Wüst, the premier of North Rhine-Westphalia, thanked the security agencies for their vigilance and dedication. "Protecting Jewish life is our shared responsibility," he wrote on X.

"Jewish life must be able to flourish freely and safely in our country. Anyone who attacks it attacks us all," Wüst added.

Prosecutors said the two men were "strongly suspected" of membership in a domestic terrorist organization.

They said that Mostafa Al-R. wa an associate of a man in the US known as Mohammad Al-Saadi, who is accused of setting up a group with the acronym HAYI (Harakat Ashab al-Yamin al-Islamiyya) that is thought to be "responsible for a series of attacks against US, Israeli and Jewish institutions in Europe from March 2026," in the aftermath of the outbreak of Donald Trump's war with Iran.

Mostafa Al-R. and Al-Saadi allegedly "founded a separate organization to prepare and carry out further attacks in Germany and Europe at the end of 2025."

Prosecutors say Ameer J. joined this group. It gathered funds and "recruited several external people to carry out explosive attacks."

"On the group's orders, unknown assailants headed on two consecutive nights in late March 2026 — presumably with explosives — to synagogues in Cologne and in one other European city," prosecutors wrote. "The plan was, for reasons as yet unknown, ultimately not carried out."

Prosecutors said that intelligence behind the arrests could be traced back to the federal domestic intelligence agency, the BfV.

More than 250 police officers from various forces were part of Thursday's operation.

Both suspects were expected to appear before judges later on Thursday for a decision on them being placed in investigative detention.

One other person is being sought as part of the investigation, with several others not currently suspected of criminal offenses affected by it.

The stated date for the plot to attack Cologne's synagogue, late March this year, would have roughly coincided with a series of crimes in Europe targeting Jewish people or institutions in the weeks after the outbreak of war with Iran.

These included an explosion in front of Liege synagogue in Belgium on March 9, which caused property damage but no injuries.

Police in the Netherlands investigated a fire at Rotterdam's synagogue a few days later. An explosive device was set off outside a Jewish school in Amsterdam soon after, prompting heightened security across the Netherlands.

In London, a Jewish ambulance service was targeted in arson attacks in March, not long before two Jewish men were stabbed in broad daylight in what investigators treated as a likely act of terrorism.

Several months later, in September, police in Wuppertal in Germany arrested an Afghan national on suspicion of trying to set a fire at the city's synagogue.

#169. Guinea orders residents to clean up the capital months after deadly rubbish dump collapse

Dozens of people were killed in August after a rubbish landfill collapsed in Conakry

Guinea has ordered residents to do a mass clean-up of the capital, Conakry, months after a rubbish landfill collapsed, killing dozens of people.

Prime Minister Amadou Oury Bah on Wednesday announced a deadline of 31 December to clear rubbish in the city and its suburbs, which has seen an increase in illegal dumping in recent months.

In August heavy rains caused a landslide at the capital's main waste dump, with the rubbish collapsing onto homes killing 31 people and injuring others.

The site in the city's Dar es Salaam area was subsequently closed permanently, which has led to piles of rubbish accumulating outside homes and on the streets.

At a public forum on sanitation, Sanitation Minister Aboubacar Camara said Conakry was generating about 3,000 tonnes of waste every day.

He said much of the garbage was ending up in the streets because of weaknesses in the collection system.

The prime minister called on local authorities to become more involved in organising waste collection, saying this could become a way to create jobs.

"The state will do its part, but the municipalities must also organise themselves to ensure waste collection and provide jobs for young people," the prime minister said.

"Conakry must be completely clean by the end of December," he added, relaying a directive from the country's president, Mamady Doumbouya.

He cited measures the government was taking to improve waste collection and recycling, including a national sanitation fund that would guarantee financing for the sector.

Camara said talks had been finalised with a waste collection operator, with a plan for equipment to move waste from transit and sorting centres to a new disposal site in BaretodÃ©, outside the capital.

The August tragedy at the Dar es Salaam landfill was not the first - 10 people were killed at the same site in 2017.

The government had ordered people living close to the site to move, but many residents chose to stay.

According to the prime minister, the government now plans to transform the closed waste site into an urban park.

#168. Germany news: Cologne court awards record damages in Catholic sex abuse case

A court has awarded the highest damages ever in Germany to a victim of sexual abuse linked with the Catholic Church. An alleged spy and suspected terrorists are also making headlines in Germany on Thursday. DW has more.

This is a roundup of the top news stories from Germany on Thursday, November 26, 2026:

The German government plans to acquire a stake in the Canada-based artificial intelligence technology firm Cohere for the symbolic sum of €1.

The Digital Ministry confirmed media reports that the Finance Ministry had approved the government's small stake in the multinational company.

According to a ministry spokesperson, the government is currently considering investing a larger sum.

Cohere would commit itself "to helping increase competitiveness in AI in Germany and to securing jobs, research and development and investment in Germany," the spokesperson said.

He added that the German government would receive "specific governance guarantees that safeguard the goal of digital sovereignty."

Cohere's goal is to develop sovereign AI services for the public administration, as well as finance, defense, energy, telecommunications, and healthcare.

This technology is designed to operate independently of major US data center operators.

The trial of a German-Ukrainian woman charged with spying for Russia began in Berlin.

The start of the proceedings was delayed in the morning after the accused developed health problems that required treatment by emergency medical workers, a spokeswoman for a Berlin court said.

Later, however, she was brought into the courtroom in a wheelchair, wearing dark sunglasses.

Several times throughout the afternoon, the defense attorneys said their client was feeling nauseous, and a bucket was brought in. A doctor was present in the courtroom.

The trial is expected to last until May 2027.

Read more about the charges against the woman, as well as the diplomatic fallout caused by the case.

IG Metall, representing millions of workers in Germany's metal and electrical industry, is the country's largest trade union.

The deal came after more than 24 hours of talks with employers.

Read the full story on what the union and industry leaders agreed to.

Germany national player Laura Freigang has been banned from playing until October 7, 2027, over three doping tests she missed, meaning that she will not be available to participate in the World Cup in Brazil next year, the German Football Federation (DFB) said in a statement on Thursday.

The ban comes amid a settlement between Freigang, 28, who is also the captain of Frankfurt Eintracht, and the DFB, the World Anti-Doping Agency (WADA) and Germany's National Anti-Doping Agency (NADA).

Freigang has been the subject of proceedings for several months, having been accused of not being at the location she specified for a doping control on three occasions between March 2025 and February 2026.

In June, she said that the missed tests "were not deliberately avoided appointments but inconsistencies or misunderstandings in the comprehensive details that have to be updated daily and which we as national team players have to update in the system on a daily and hourly basis."

Freigang has played 45 times for Germany, scoring 17 goals.

However, she has not recently been a regular in the team.

Read the full story on why Freigang will miss the 2027 World Cup.

The archdiocese in the western city of Cologne was ordered on Wednesday to pay a woman who was sexually abused by a priest for years €550,000 ($601,865) in damages — the highest amount a German court has ever awarded to a victim of abuse in theCatholic Church.

That amount will, however, by offset by the €360,000 that the Catholic Church's Independent Commission for Recognition Payments has already paid to the claimant.

The woman had previously had her claim rejected by the Cologne Regional Court, which, although it accepted that the victim had been severely sexually abused by a priest in the 1970s and 1980s, argued that the archdiocese could not be made responsible for the crimes because the man had acted "more or less as a private individual."

The higher regional court has now overturned that ruling at an appeal, saying that the priest had approached the claimant "under the cover of his church work" when he was a deacon.

It said he used his standing in the church to be allowed to take in foster children, of whom the claimant was one.

The priest was sentenced to 12 years in prison in 2022.

German Foreign MinisterJohann Wadephul has warned against an expansion of the Ukraine conflict into other regions after the Ukrainian attacks on Russian ships in Libya.

"Ukraine's ​right to self-defense is undisputed and we ​do support Ukraine, but I strongly ⁠warn ​against allowing ​this conflict to spread ​geographically," he ‌said at ​a ⁠press conference in Berlin with ⁠his ​Romanian counterpart Dan Neculaescu.

Wadephul also condemned reported Russian attacks on two foreign freight ships said to be carrying grain near Ukraine's Black Sea port of Odesa, saying such attacks did not concern only the security of nearby nations but also the food security of people in the Global South.

"It is clear that we cannot accept drone attacks on trading vessels," he said. "NATO will therefore step up its engagement in the region."

German President Frank-Walter Steinmeier has called for closer economic and political partnerships with African countries as he wound up what was probably his last trip to the continent as head of state.

Speaking in Kenya, Steinmeier said that there were great variations in the state of development between individual countries and even within some of them.

"But I believe it is worthwhile investing in Africa and in relations with African countries," he said.

He said this was not just meant with a view to possible investment by German companies drawn by Africa's mostly young national populations and developing markets.   "That is true, but we should not limit our view to that. Many African countries now have political weight and growing self-confidence and want a say in shaping the future of the world," he said. "Africa, it has become clear, is a continent that requires patience if you want to discover it," Steinmeier said. "There is no single Africa, but very many."

Before Kenya, Steinmeier visited Ivory Coast and Ghana. On the final day of his trip, he went to the Crescent Island nature reserve on Lake Naivasha, an area under threat from climate change.

Chocolate manufacturers in Germany produced less in 2025 than the year before.

According to figures from the Federal Statistical Office (Destatis) released on Thursday, around 1 million metric tons (1.1 US tons) of chocolate products containing cocoa were made in Germany in 2025, compared with 1.1 million tons in 2024.

Much of the chocolate produced in Germany is exported (916,200 tons in 2025), while considerable amounts are also imported (530,000 tons in 2025), with a quarter of that amount coming from neighboring Belgium.

Altogether 13.4% of exports went to France, followed by Poland (9.6%) and the UK (9.3%).

According to Destatis, citing the EU's statistical agency Eurostat, Germany remains the European leader in the production of gingerbread, or Lebkuchen, manufacturing 39.4% of the entire EU supply of the sweet treat, compared with Poland on 17.3% and the Netherlands on 17%.

German authorities say that two Iraqi citizens believed to be members of a pro-Iranian terrorist group have been arrested on suspicion of having planned a number of anti-Jewish attacks, including on a synagogue in the western city of Cologne.

A survey by police union GdP has shown that some 69% of police in Germany are in favor of a ban on private fireworks on New Year's Eve.

According to the survey of 9,000 members, more than 70% feel that the situation for police on the evening would improve if such a ban were in place.

Altogether 54% of respondents who had been on duty during the past 10 years of New Year's Eve festivities reported having been in a dangerous or threatening situation caused by fireworks.

A whole 195 respondents said they had been injured by fireworks on the evening.

The poll was prompted by an open letter to Interior Minister Alexander Dobrindt by a group calling itself #böllerciao (Goodbye firecrackers) with some 1.17 million signatories. The name is a play on the famous anti-Nazi song Bella Ciao.

The group includes 86 organizations, among them Environmental Action Germany (DUH) and the GdP, who have called for a ban on private fireworks to protect people, emergency personnel, animals and the environment.

Plans foreseeing a rise of €1 billion ($1.1 billion) in the budget for the Interior Ministry have been approved by the three parties in Germany's coalition government, after much debate centering on something that makes up a relatively small proportion of that money: the controversial planned centers for rejected asylum seekers in Rwanda.

According to Klaus-Peter Willsch of Chancellor Friedrich Merz's Christian Democrats (CDU), just €5 million are earmarked for the so-called return hubs, shared between several European countries.

He said the €20 million that has often been cited as being for the Rwanda migration centers was not only for the hubs in that country, but for other potential projects in third countries as well.

Despite the relatively small amounts of money involved, the Rwanda plans devised by Interior Minister Alexander Dobrindt of the Bavarian Christian Social Union (CSU) along with Austria, Denmark, the Netherlands and Greece came under fire not only from coalition partners the Social Democrats (SPD) but particularly from opposition parties.

The Green Party and the Left Party both rejected the idea for ethical reasons, with Green lawmaker Leon Eckert speaking of "state-organized human trafficking," while Dietmar Bartsch of the Left pointed out that the deal was being made with an authoritarian regime.

The far-right Alternative for Germany (AfD), for its part, said it found the plans too soft, with parliamentarian Marcus Bühl calling them a "smoke screen," as just 20 rejected asylum-seekers from Germany were to be initially sent to Rwanda under the scheme.

The trial of a German-Ukrainian woman due to go on trial on Thursday on charges of spying for Russia was delayed in the morning after the accused developed health problems that required treatment by emergency medical workers, a spokeswoman for a Berlin court said.
