#166. Hundreds of millions at risk from Chinese shopping app malware

It is one of China’s most popular shopping apps, selling clothing, groceries and just about everything else under the sun to more than 750 million users a month.

But according to cybersecurity researchers, it can also bypass users’ cell phone security to monitor activities on other apps, check notifications, read private messages and change settings.

And once installed, it’s tough to remove.

In a detailed investigation, CNN spoke to half a dozen cybersecurity teams from Asia, Europe and the United States — as well as multiple former and current Pinduoduo employees — after receiving a tipoff.

Multiple experts identified the presence of malware on the Pinduoduo app that exploited vulnerabilities in Android operating systems. Company insiders said the exploits were utilized to spy on users and competitors, allegedly to boost sales.

“We haven’t seen a mainstream app like this trying to escalate their privileges to gain access to things that they’re not supposed to gain access to,” said Mikko Hyppönen, chief research officer at WithSecure, a Finnish cybersecurity firm.

“This is highly unusual, and it is pretty damning for Pinduoduo.”

This is highly unusual, and it is pretty damning for Pinduoduo.

Malware, short for malicious software, refers to any software developed to steal data or interfere with computer systems and mobile devices.

Evidence of sophisticated malware in the Pinduoduo app comes amid intense scrutiny of Chinese-developed apps like TikTok over concerns about data security.

Some American lawmakers are pushing for a national ban on the popular short-video app, whose CEO Shou Chew was grilled by Congress for five hours last week about its relations with the Chinese government.

The revelations are also likely to draw more attention to Pinduoduo’s international sister app, Temu, which is topping US download charts and fast expanding in other Western markets. Both are owned by Nasdaq-listed PDD, a multinational company with roots in China.

While Temu has not been implicated, Pinduoduo’s alleged actions risk casting a shadow over its sister app’s global expansion.

There is no evidence that Pinduoduo has handed data to the Chinese government. But as Beijing enjoys significant leverage over businesses under its jurisdiction, there are concerns from US lawmakers that any company operating in China could be forced to cooperate with a broad range of security activities.

The findings follow Google’s suspension of Pinduoduo from its Play Store in March, citing malware identified in versions of the app.

An ensuing report from Bloomberg said a Russian cybersecurity firm had also identified potential malware in the app.

Pinduoduo has previously rejected “the speculation and accusation that Pinduoduo app is malicious.”

CNN has contacted PDD multiple times over email and phone for comment, but has not received a response.

Pinduoduo, which boasts a user base that accounts for three quarters of China’s online population and a market value three times that of eBay (EBAY), wasn’t always an online shopping behemoth.

Founded in 2015 in Shanghai by Colin Huang, a former Google employee, the startup was fighting to establish itself in a market long dominated by e-commerce stalwarts Alibaba (BABA) and JD.com (JD).

It succeeded by offering steep discounts on friends-and-family group buying orders and focusing on lower-income rural areas.

Pinduoduo posted triple digit growth in monthly users until the end of 2018, the year it listed in New York. By the middle of 2020, though, the increase in monthly users had slowed to around 50% and would continue to decline, according to its earnings reports.

It was in 2020, according to a current Pinduoduo employee, that the company set up a team of about 100 engineers and product managers to dig for vulnerabilities in Android phones, develop ways to exploit them — and turn that into profit.

According to the source, who requested anonymity for fear of reprisals, the company only targeted users in rural areas and smaller towns initially, while avoiding users in megacities such as Beijing and Shanghai.

“The goal was to reduce the risk of being exposed,” they said.

By collecting expansive data on user activities, the company was able to create a comprehensive portrait of users’ habits, interests and preferences, according to the source.

This allowed it to improve its machine learning model to offer more personalized push notifications and ads, attracting users to open the app and place orders, they said.

The team was disbanded in early March, the source added, after questions about their activities came to light.

PDD didn’t reply to CNN’s repeated requests for comment on the team.

Approached by CNN, researchers from Tel Aviv-based cyber firm Check Point Research, Delaware-based app security startup Oversecured and Hyppönen’s WithSecure conducted independent analysis of the 6.49.0 version of the app, released on Chinese app stores in late February.

Google Play is not available in China, and Android users in the country download their apps from local stores. In March, when Google suspended Pinduoduo, it said it had found malware in off-Play versions of the app.

The researchers found code designed to achieve “privilege escalation”: a type of cyberattack that exploits a vulnerable operating system to gain a higher level of access to data than it’s supposed to have, according to experts.

“Our team has reverse engineered that code and we can confirm that it tries to escalate rights, tries to gain access to things normal apps wouldn’t be able to do on Android phones,” said Hyppönen.

The app was able to continue running in the background and prevent itself from being uninstalled, which allowed it to boost its monthly active user rates, Hyppönen said. It also had the ability to spy on competitors by tracking activity on other shopping apps and getting information from them, he added.

Check Point Research additionally identified ways in which the app was able to evade scrutiny.

The app deployed a method that allowed it to push updates without an app store review process meant to detect malicious applications, the researchers said.

They also identified in some plug-ins the intent to obscure potentially malicious components by hiding them under legitimate file names, such as Google’s.

“Such a technique is widely used by malware developers that inject malicious code into applications that have legitimate functionality,” they said.

In China, about three quarters of smartphone users are on the Android system. Apple (AAPL)’s iPhone has 25% market share, according to Daniel Ives of Wedbush Securities.

Sergey Toshin, the founder of Oversecured, said Pinduoduo’s malware specifically targeted different Android-based operating systems, including those used by Samsung, Huawei, Xiaomi and Oppo.

CNN has reached out to these companies for comment.

Toshin described Pinduoduo as “the most dangerous malware” ever found among mainstream apps.

“I’ve never seen anything like this before. It’s like, super expansive,” he said.

Most phone manufacturers globally customize the core Android software, the Android Open Source Project (AOSP), to add unique features and applications to their own devices.

Toshin found Pinduoduo to have exploited about 50 Android system vulnerabilities. Most of the exploits were tailor made for customized parts known as the original equipment manufacturer (OEM) code, which tends to be audited less often than AOSP and is therefore more prone to vulnerabilities, he said.

Pinduoduo also exploited a number of AOSP vulnerabilities, including one which was flagged by Toshin to Google in February 2022. Google fixed the bug this March, he said.

I’ve never seen anything like this before. It’s like, super expansive.

According to Toshin, the exploits allowed Pinduoduo access to users’ locations, contacts, calendars, notifications and photo albums without their consent. They were also able to change system settings and access users’ social network accounts and chats, he said.

Of the six teams CNN spoke to for this story, three did not conduct full examinations. But their primary reviews showed that Pinduoduo asked for a large number of permissions beyond the normal functions of a shopping app.

They included “potentially invasive permissions” such as “set wallpaper” and “download without notification,” said René Mayrhofer, head of the Institute of Networks and Security at the Johannes Kepler University Linz in Austria.

Suspicions about malware in Pinduoduo’s app were first raised in late February in a report by a Chinese cybersecurity firm called Dark Navy. Even though the analysis didn’t directly name the shopping giant, the report spread quickly among other researchers, who did name the company. Some of the analysts followed up with their own reports confirming the original findings.

Soon after, on March 5, Pinduoduo issued a new update of its app, version 6.50.0, which removed the exploits, according to two experts who CNN spoke to.

Two days after the update, Pinduoduo disbanded the team of engineers and product managers who had developed the exploits, according to the Pinduoduo source.

The next day, team members found themselves locked out of Pinduoduo’s bespoke workplace communication app, Knock, and lost access to files on the company’s internal network. Engineers also found their access to big data, data sheets and the log system revoked, the source said.

Most of the team were transferred to work at Temu. They were assigned to different departments at the subsidiary, with some working on marketing or developing push notifications, according to the source.

A core group of about 20 cybersecurity engineers who specialize in finding and exploiting vulnerabilities remain at Pinduoduo, they said.

Toshin of Oversecured, who looked into the update, said although the exploits were removed, the underlying code was still there and could be reactivated to carry out attacks.

Pinduoduo has been able to grow its user base against a backdrop of the Chinese government’s regulatory clampdown on Big Tech that began in late 2020.

That year, the Ministry of Industry and Information Technology launched a sweeping crackdown on apps that illegally collect and use personal data.

The Personal Information Protection Law stipulates that no party should illegally collect, process or transmit personal information. They’re also banned from exploiting internet-related security vulnerabilities or engaging in actions that endanger cybersecurity.

Pinduoduo’s apparent malware would be a violation of those laws, tech policy experts say, and should have been detected by the regulator.

“This would be embarrassing for the Ministry of Industry and Information Technology, because this is their job,” said Kendra Schaefer, a tech policy expert at Trivium China, a consultancy. “They’re supposed to check Pinduoduo, and the fact that they didn’t find (anything) is embarrassing for the regulator.”

Pinduoduo did not appear on any of the lists.

They’re supposed to check Pinduoduo, and the fact that they didn’t find (anything) is embarrassing for the regulator.

CNN has reached out to the Ministry of Industry and Information Technology and the Cyberspace Administration of China for comment.

On Chinese social media, some cybersecurity experts questioned why regulators haven’t taken any action.

“Probably none of our regulators can understand coding and programming, nor do they understand technology. You can’t even understand the malicious code when it’s shoved right in front of your face,” a cybersecurity expert with 1.8 million followers wrote last week in a viral post on Weibo, a Twitter-like platform.

CNN’s Kristie Lu Stout and Sean Lyngaas contributed reporting.

#164. What is the US permitting bill and would it actually help the climate?

Sweeping US legislation to speed up energy projects has found rare bipartisan support. Supporters say it would unlock clean energy and lower energy bills, but critics say it's a gift to fossil fuels.

The dust has barely settled on the Democrats' big midterm showing, but lawmakers still have business to get done before a new US Congress takes office in 2027: namely, a permitting reform bill with rare cross-party support that could accelerate clean energy or fossil fuels — or both, depending on who's asked.

Permitting reform might sound like a snooze. But the Bipartisan American Affordability and Jobs Act (BAAJA) could have far-reaching consequences for the country's energy mix, household utility bills and climate goals.

BAAJA, which is backed by US President Donald Trump, has already cleared the Senate and now faces a vote in the House of Representatives. DW breaks down what's in the bill, the political fight playing out on the sidelines and what it could mean for consumers' wallets and the US energy transition.

Before many new solar farms, wind projects or power plants can connect to the electricity grid, they must first pass through an arduous and complex permitting process.

Getting the green light for an energy project can often take years and requires permits from multiple government agencies. If it manages to overcome those hurdles, a legal challenge can easily stop new infrastructure in its tracks.

The nearly 500-page bill aims to overhaul that process. It would speed up environmental reviews, simplify the planning and construction of power transmission lines, make permits harder to revoke and require the growing number of data centers to pay for any needed grid upgrades.

The bill is mostly technology agnostic. So, the same reforms that would make it easier to build a lot of green energy infrastructure quickly could, in theory, be used to speed up oil and gas projects whose carbon emissions are warming the planet.

That's what's made BAAJA controversial, with some Democrats saying permitting reform is needed to get energy bills down and others saying they support provisions, like building more transmission lines required for transporting green energy, but not the whole package.

It's hard to estimate, and there's a lot of contradictory information out there.

The Rhodium Group, an independent research firm, found the bill would lower US greenhouse gas emissions in every scenario it ran, by up to 4% in 2040 compared with no bill. This is a fraction of the emissions reductions targets the US dropped when it withdrew from Paris climate accord this year.

Purdue University engineer Kevin Kircher reached the opposite conclusion. His analysis, which has not been peer-reviewed, estimates that domestic emissions would rise by about 2%, mostly because he assumes the bill would boost US fossil fuel production.

To view this video please enable JavaScript, and consider upgrading to a web browser that supports HTML5 video

The difference comes down to a bet about what the bill ends up accelerating more: renewables and the power lines to carry them, or fossil fuels. That depends on which administration is issuing the permits.

Under the administration of former President Joe Biden, for instance, renewables got a boost. But since Trump took office in January 2025, his administration has favored fossil fuels and spent billions paying developers to give up their offshore wind leases.

The White House Council of Economic Advisers, a team of economists that advises the president, has estimated the measure would eventually cut the average household electricity bill by $178 to $251 (€162 to €229) a year.

The logic is that long permitting delays discourage investment in infrastructure, which drives up prices. But that estimate considers only one part of the bill, faster environmental reviews, and doesn't say when the savings would arrive.

Independent analyses also found a drop in electricity prices for consumers, though a more modest one. The Rhodium Group estimated the bill's passage would save the average household $41 to $66 a year by 2035. That's largely because new transmission lines would let cheaper power reach customers, and the costs of expanding the grid for data centers would no longer be passed on to households.

Critics have pointed to the fact that those savings are relative. Electricity demand from data centers is expected to push up prices regardless, and permitting reform would only slow that rise rather than reverse it.

To view this video please enable JavaScript, and consider upgrading to a web browser that supports HTML5 video

Oil Change International, an advocacy group that opposes the bill, argues households will still end up paying more for energy than they do now, especially if the bill mainly speeds up gas projects, since US gas prices are expected to rise sharply over the next decade.

Most major US environmental groups oppose the bill as written, including the Natural Resources Defense Council, League of Conservation Voters and Earthjustice. The Sierra Club called it "fundamentally flawed."

Much of the criticism focuses on provisions making federal permits harder to revoke.

"It would prevent a future administration from reconsidering a prior administration's flawed decisions to authorize activities such as Arctic drilling, deep seabed mining, or development of data centers on public lands," Earthjustice wrote in a critique.

The reverse is also true. That provision also makes it harder for an administration to come in and halt clean energy projects, as Trump did with offshore wind.

Opponents have warned the bill could weaken protection for water and habitats, and limit environmental lawsuits. One area that environmental groups have praised is the overhaul of how transmission lines are planned and approved.

That matters, because the windiest and sunniest places in the US are often far from the cities that need power, and building long-distance power lines to connect them can take a decade or more.

Still, many critics say they would rather wait until Democrats control Congress in January and pass a version more focused on clean energy. Though that approach is risky, any bill would still require Trump's signature to become law.

→ Baca artikel asli

#163. From minimalist to maximalist: Ars staffers show off their home offices

Our home offices range from tasteful to “please explain yourself.”

Ars Technica is a place that has always leaned into remote working. Since its beginnings in 1998, Ars has never had a single, central office—a setup that makes for a great commute to work, with little risk of getting stuck in traffic.

As Ars staffers step away from their desks for a few days to spend some time with family and friends, we wanted to give you a look inside our home offices. We’ve got all sorts of setups, tucked into basements, dens, spare bedrooms, apartment corners, and lofts. They run the gamut from minimalist “here’s my laptop and monitor” setups to “I really like collecting things.” Without any further ado, let’s dig in:

As an apartment dweller in New York City, I’ve usually set up my home work station in a small corner of my residence along with sometimes decamping for local cafes or coworking spaces. My current hodgepodge of equipment is probably on the minimal side compared to many members of the Ars team.

I’m usually multitasking on my basic monitor display and work laptop configuration, with the monitor sitting on a stack of several books (apologies in particular to Mary Beard’s excellent history of the Roman Republic, titled SPQR).

Accessories plugged into the work laptop include a simple Lekvey Ergonomic Mouse and a Logitech K380 keyboard that I’ve often brought along with me to public workspaces. Sometimes I’ll use the built-in Macbook Air keyboard, but I like having the laptop display elevated, whether I’m working at home or elsewhere.

My personal Apple M4 Macbook Air usually sits on the side, where I primarily use my old, battered pair of Sennheiser HD 518 headphones to listen to music during the work day.

I have a simple standing desk, but it’s not the easiest to change positions, so I wouldn’t recommend it for anyone who wants to adjust frequently. Whenever I’m sitting, I sometimes switch between a simple wooden chair from Ikea and a more ergonomic chair.

Aside from an assortment of coasters and a tea container turned pen holder, my desk also holds a painted dinosaur sculpture (courtesy of my young son) and two versions of the Spot It! Matching game (also for my son’s entertainment).

Last but not least, I have a pair of Powerblock dumbbells tucked under my work desk. I wouldn’t say they feature heavily in my work day, but they’re easily accessible if I have 15-20 minutes to spare.

Greetings from my basement hobbit hole! My desk sits across from a workbench area that was already here when we got the house. I have labeled drawers full of PC parts, tools, and cables accumulated over decades. The drawer organization was quite the upgrade over my previous solution of “chuck everything in unlabeled storage tubs.”

My current PC setup is an AceMagic W1 mini PC running Fedora, and a Blackview Intel N150 I keep on Windows for audio recording (PipeWire is fussy). The two are connected to my inputs and monitors via a KVM so I can switch back and forth at the press of a button.

The monitors are mismatched thrift finds. One is an HP, the other Sansui. The TV is a similar story—an old Samsung LCD that set me back $10 at the thrift store. I use it as a third monitor for watching PC streams. An Onn Android box is also connected, though it rarely earns its keep.

Below the TV sits a Lenovo ThinkSmart View, a repurposed B2B video-conference gadget modded to run LineageOS. It handles smart-home controls and tracks my solar production during the day. There’s also a Flashforge 5M printer in the corner, and the pegboard wall holds tools, Mega Man figures, and about 10 years’ worth of convention badges.

The back closet is the scary part: old consoles, spare drives, boards, and parts for every project I’ll get to “eventually.” The chair is a Dowinx gaming chair I grabbed on a Slickdeals whim—it’s fine, but I wouldn’t recommend it to anyone.

The rug-patterned Dude mousepad really ties the desk together, man.

I’m something of an outlier at Ars, in that I use Windows most days. I’m no great lover of Windows 11, but I still use it thanks to years of computing inertia. My machine is custom, built in early 2024 with a Raptor Lake Core i7 (that thankfully did not fall victim to the overvolting bug) and an RTX 4070 Ti. It has long been my preference to build my own PC for gaming and work to ensure easier repairs and upgrades. Although with the prices of components being what they are, I hope all these parts last a very long time.

The primary monitor on the bottom is an Alienware ultrawide OLED. I got it for gaming, but it’s also a nice productivity display that gives me lots of room to keep windows visible. Above that, I have an Asus ProArt LCD calibrated in sRGB for photography work. I’ll get to the keyboard, but I do want to point out my preferred mouse, a Logitech MX Vertical. I have carpal tunnel issues, and this thing has been a lifesaver.

I believe organizing my desk before taking the photo would be a form of lying, so rest assured this is what it looks like on an average day. I have a few organizers to keep phones and various accessories from getting scattered around my Uplift sit-stand desk (seen here in sitting mode), which I quite like. The chair is a Herman Miller Aeron, which I don’t like as much. It’s fine and has plenty of adjustments, but the mesh is very hard. You should only buy one on sale, and if you do, get a cushion.

Above the desk are some Star Trek cross-stitches, courtesy of my talented wife. The dangling ’90s-era computer components are a mobile I put together many years ago, featuring a neat Intel i486 DEC socket board and some SDRAM.

I’m not a particularly neat person. My natural state is to have two or three projects half-exploded all over every surface: piles of 3D-printed prototypes, spools of wire, a soldering iron, laser-cut acrylic for a pinball project, the packaging from something I recently opened and need to sort through. Those aren’t random examples; that’s the contents of my workbench behind me on the opposite wall of my office as I type this. It’s a bit of a mess.

To combat that tendency, I make a concerted effort to keep the desk where I spend so many hours of the day calm and a place where I can focus. It doesn’t always last! But I’m always at least striving for it, and what I find helps is having a very intentional design where everything has a place.

My desk surface is made from rubberwood butcher block, on Flexispot motorized legs. I recently picked up another rubberwood slab to build the cantilevered shelf. The supports neatly tuck away my M1 Max Mac Studio, which is still a great little workhorse with 64 gigs of RAM, and my Arturia audio interface, which I use to record line-in sources like synthesizers.

On the left is a task lamp and a pen holder with six discrete holes. This fits in with the intentional design part: I have to pick the six things I want handy, and I can’t just pack it with more and more stuff. Currently that’s my Apple Pencil, a Pentel mechanical pencil, an Olfa mini snap-off blade knife, and a selection of three Pigma Micron pens in different line widths.

The right side holds an Aiyima 10th anniversary hi-fi stack: A20 amp, D20 DAC, and T20 pre-amplifier. The speakers are SVS Ultra Evolution Nanos. They’re sitting on the latest version of some 3D-printed stands I’ve been designing. I print them with hollow legs and then fill them with concrete for mass and stability.

Since I know Ryan is going to flex on everyone with his keyboard collection, I have to try my best to keep up with my daily driver. It’s a Percent Canoe v2 case, running Holy Panda switches modified with 63 gram SPRiT springs for a slightly lighter touch, and GMK Bento keycaps. This was a pandemic build project, and it has served me well since.

I used to run multiple monitors, but I’ve found that I don’t focus as well with them and prefer a singular spot to pay attention to. I’m using an Apple Studio Display because I’m very picky about pixel density. I opted to order it without the stand so I can run it on an ergo arm.

When I built the shelf for my desk, I fabricated a mounting bracket for the arm out of schedule 40 steel pipe and a 3D-printed sleeve that sits in a pocket CNC’d out. Since it sits flush on the underside, I don’t have to use a clamp that would extend down into the space where I keep my Mac and audio interface.

When I need a secondary display, or I want to use my Apple Pencil in Photoshop or Illustrator, I use an iPad Pro. I used to have a dedicated Wacom monitor, but I find this much more convenient both in terms of space efficiency and ergonomics. If I’m not really leaning into hand drawing or lettering, I do most of my graphic work with a mouse, which is currently a Logitech MX Master 3S.

To wrap up the final items, the chair is an Aeron I bought new in 2003. I’ve had to repair it a few times over the years, but it’s either been things I consider wear items (wheels, armrests, gas cylinder) or Herman Miller has replaced under warranty. It was a big purchase for me at the time, but it’s ended up being a great investment. That’s Sophie draped over it, one of our four cats.

Tucked next to my desk, behind the subwoofer under the little side table, is my home server and networking gear. The server runs Unraid and is primarily used for my Jellyfin media collection and Home Assistant. Networking is a combination of Unifi and Eero, but at some point I’d like to ditch the Amazon gear and go fully native Unifi.

I spend a lot of time in my office. It’s not just a place to edit, write, and do other Ars-y things. It’s also my rehearsal space and a place to relax and unwind when I need some me-time. Long-time readers won’t be surprised to see Apple hardware front and center. I’ve been using Macs since college, and they’ve done alright by me ever since. My daily driver is a first-gen Mac Studio (M1 Max) with 64GB of RAM, which drives two Apple Studio Displays. As a long-time mouse hater, I use a Magic Trackpad along with my extended Apple keyboard. Underneath the desk is a UPS and a Thunderbolt disk enclosure that is one of my two backups.

The bookshelves are where my home networking gear lives. That includes a Synology DS920+ that backs up all of the computers in the house, serves up media, and runs my instance of Mealie. The Eero 6+ handles home networking, with everything in my office except my MacBook Pro connected via Ethernet. As much as I’d like to live a paperless existence, the HP printer-scanner-copier combo still gets some use. They’re difficult to see, but to the right of the G4 Cube (sadly inoperable) on top of the bookshelf sit an Apple TV and M2 Mac mini, which hook up to the 43-inch TV. My graphite iBook and eMate are now resigned to propping up bibs from races and other cycling events.

This is my office, which is the place where I am much of the time. The chair is a Herman Miller Embody and is one of the best things I’ve ever spent money on. The desk is a standy desk that I don’t stand at nearly often enough; the work PC is a Mac Studio; and the gaming PC is a frankenbox with a cockpit running Windows 10 LTSC (from my cold dead hands, etc., etc.). The Linux boxen are headless and are mostly banished to the closet.

The paintings (including the awesome Apollo 12 SCE-to-aux one) are all originals by the inimitable Stephen Quick, who does a hell of a job with commissions. What started as a funny idea about a Warhol-esque take on the many faces of Kurt Russel kinda turned over a couple of years into somewhat of a “these are some actors whose work I like” series. My buddies and I went to a lot of movies when we were teenagers, so the imagery sparks good memories for me.

The rest of the office has space stuff in it, especially the part behind where I was standing to do the pano sot. I’ve heard it said that when a man hits his 40s, he must choose between becoming obsessed with World War 2 history or smoking meats; I did kind of a variation on “World War 2” and fell deeply into historic human spaceflight. Apollo is kind of my jam, and some weekends you can even catch me docenting at Space Center Houston’s Saturn V exhibit! (Yeah, it’s maybe not as pretty as the exhibits at Marshall or KSC, but the JSC Saturn V is the only one made of flight stages end to end!)

Welcome to the mess of wires, monitors, and handheld fidgets that is my loft desk. Let’s take a quick tour along with the rest of my gaming cave/mid-life crisis Mario collection that lives right by it.

My office setup is probably a lot simpler—basic?—than most of my Ars colleagues. I use an adjustable wood-top desk that allows me to stand or sit while I work. I do almost all of my writing and editing on a MacBook Air, and I have a spare available in case I need it. Sometimes I travel with both MacBooks so I can use one for writing and the other for monitoring a rocket launch, press conference, or other event. I also work a lot from my porch, where I can enjoy the fresh air and quiet mornings on our ranch in rural Texas.

An iMac and a second monitor stand behind the laptops. I generally use them to watch conferences, rocket launch livestreams, weather radar, or live news. It just depends on what’s happening any given day. One of the things I love about the space beat is there’s something happening nearly every day, and it’s possible to cover much of it from home. There are often multiple things happening the same day, so the dual-screen setup becomes especially handy then. For example, I can use one monitor to watch what SpaceX is doing at Starbase, Texas, and another to watch a rocket launch in Florida. I use a ring light behind my desk for TV interviews or podcast appearances.

Absent from my desk is my large collection of space swag stowed away in storage boxes. Perhaps I will bring it out to add to my workspace for a future Ars Staff Source. For now, I use a mouse pad from the European Space Agency that I’ve had for at least 15 years. The mouse pad features ESA’s astronomy missions that were in development or operations at the time. All of the missions have now launched, and some have long been decommissioned. It’s a good reminder of how long it takes to design, develop, build, and launch science missions. Anyone who says this stuff is easy is being dishonest.

This image is a bit misleading, in that it implies I work on the pictured PC (RTX 4070, Ryzen 5 3600X, 32GB RAM) and sit at my desk while doing so. That’s occasionally true, but more often, I can be found wandering around my apartment with my M4 MacBook Air during work hours. I don’t even sit at my desk to game much anymore—on the off chance that I’m playing a game the Steam Deck can’t handle natively, I’ll stream it from my PC to my Steam Deck using Moonlight and Apollo (previously Moonlight and Sunshine).

You may also notice that my desk, from a company called Uplift, is adjustable. As it turns out, I don’t enjoy standing while working, so the ability to loft my tabletop skyward has been chiefly deployed in the service of making it easier to retrieve something I’ve dropped down there.

At night, my desk often moonlights as a craft area; I swap out my keyboard (a Wobkey Rainy75) for a cutting mat, and I’m ready to build some Gundam, assemble a book nook, or tackle a crochet pattern (a few amigurumi are scattered around my desk to keep me company). You can see some of my supplies on the left, but I have other stuff stashed in drawers and various containers that I can bring to my desk as needed.

My wife Anna and I rent an apartment in Chicago, and we’ve chosen the city life over, well, space. As such, my 10-by-10-foot office space serves multiple functions. I use it primarily for work, but since Anna and I are both big gamers and she is often camped out with the PlayStation 5 in the living room, I have my own gaming setup in the office. It’s cramped, but it works!

I do editing, news hits, and most other work during the day, but I’m a night owl at heart and find that I can only do heavy writing—like on longer feature articles—at night, so I’m picturing both the daytime and nighttime view here. I have a collection of Govee lights (Govee Hexa panels, several RGB Edison bulbs, and a Star Light Aurora galaxy projector) to max out the vibes for those nighttime focus (and gaming) sessions.

We moved into this place in the dead of the pandemic, and I bought most of the stuff in this office during that year or shortly afterward, so it’s a very 2020–2021-coded array of hardware.

The desk is a Secretlab Magnus Pro, and the chair is a Steelcase Gesture. I love the desk, but I have mixed feelings about the chair. I don’t like the material of the Herman Miller Aeron, but people kept telling me to buy an ergonomic chair for long-term back health, so I bought the other one everyone recommends. To be frank, I’m never fully comfortable in it, though, and wish I had just said “life is short” and bought something comfy and bad for me!

The main machine is a 14-inch MacBook Pro with an M1 Max, 64GB of RAM, and a 2TB hard drive—still holding strong after five years. I also have an Alienware Aurora R10—hear me out—prebuilt gaming desktop with a Ryzen 9 5900X and an RTX 3080. When I bought it, it was during the COVID-times GPU shortage, and there was absolutely no other way to get the GPU I wanted. The system is loud, hot, and hard to work with; I want very much to replace it with something custom, but the need has not yet arisen. I tend to hold on to stuff until it literally stops working.

As for monitors, the center monitor is a 27-inch, 1440p, 240 Hz OLED Asus XG27AQDMGZ. The left and right monitors are both 27-inch 4K, 60 Hz IPS Dell U2720Qs. I’ve got a pair of Audioengine A2+ speakers, plus a Blue Yeti X, a Logitech Lighta Glow, and the good ol’ Logitech C920 for video and voice calls.

Then there’s input: a Keychron K1 Pro keyboard, a Razer Orochi V2 mouse, and a Sony DualSense controller. I’ve got a Belkin charging station for my iPhone 17 Pro, AirPods Pro, and Apple Watch Series 7, plus AirPods Max on a headphone hanger on the front of the desk. The CalDigit E5 Thunderbolt hub is doing a lot of work here!

I feel fortunate to have a job that allows me to occasionally apply my lifelong love affair with video games. My favorite game of all time is The Elder Scrolls V: Skyrim (Morrowind is a close second), and I like Bethesda games in general. I’ve got a working (but mostly useless) Fallout Pip-Boy, which ought to count for something! I also have a few other Skyrim knickknacks hanging about. And if you’re a Star Trek: The Next Generation viewer, you might have already noticed the desk mat.

I do enjoy playing games at my desk sometimes, but after I sit at the computer all day, I often want to be somewhere else when winding down. So pictured here is the other half of the room. The leather armchair I use for console gaming is paired with a leather ottoman (the colors don’t match because, again, COVID-era supply limitations).

I have an LG C1 OLED TV with a PlayStation 5 Pro, a PlayStation 3 that is backward-compatible with PlayStation 1 and 2 games, a Nintendo Switch I barely touch, and three Analogue FPGA retro consoles: the Super Nt, Mega Sg, and 3D.

It’s a tiny room to spend so much time in, but after almost seven years in this apartment, it certainly feels like home!

#160. Crew member dies after diesel tanker sinks off Ibiza coast

Authorities rescued 17 crew members but one later died in hospital after a diesel tanker sank off the Spanish island of Ibiza. A contingency plan against possible pollution damage is now in place.

A tanker carrying up to 7,000 metric tonnes (7,716 short tons) of diesel sank off the Spanish island of Ibiza, local authorities said on Thursday.

The ship's 17 crew members were rescued but one later died in hospital and another six suffered from hypothermia when the Maltese-flagged Med Emre capsized overnight, the government of Spain's Balearic Islands, said in a statement.

According to emergency services, the five further crew members were taken to Valencia and the remainig five were in good condition on a ‌rescue ​vessel.

The chemical tanker was sailing from Algeciras to Barcelona when it sank about 30 nautical miles (56 kilometers) off Ibiza's northwestern coast.

The nationalities of the crew or the cause of the sinking was not immediately clear.

The Transport Ministry said the maritime rescue vessel Guardamar Talia had reported no wreckage, floating debris or signs of pollution at the site as of Thursday morning. It added that another rescue ship was heading to the area to continue monitoring the situation.

Spain's maritime rescue service deployed an aircraft and the Clara Campoamor, a vessel equipped to respond to oil spills, to inspect the area.

Spain's merchant marine authority ​has activated the national maritime pollution contingency plan at level 2, the second-highest.

The Balearic regional government said it was assessing the incident and its potential impact, including the risk of fuel pollution, before deciding on further measures.

The waters surrounding Ibiza are a critical zone for biodiversity as they host sea turtles, dolphins and the ‌Balearic shearwater, one of Europe's most threatened seabirds.

The area also hosts vast Posidonia oceanica seagrass meadows, which are essential for maintaining marine biodiversity.

Emilio Rodriguez Diaz, a maritime security expert at the University of Cadiz, told Cadena SER radio that the area affected was "particularly vulnerable."

But he said the Med Emre was a modern ship, built in Turkey in 2019, with a double hull to ensure that, "in the event of a leak (or) a collision, that intermediate part between the cargo hold and the outer layer would flood."

"It's a kind of buffer that prevents the cargo... from being released into the environment," he said.

Spain's worst maritime environmental disaster happened in 2022, when the tanker Prestige sank off the northwestern Galicia region. It spilled tens of thousands of heavy fuel oil and polluted beaches across Spain and France.

#159. Harry and Meghan’s Life as ‘Nonroyal Royals’ in the UK

Six years after leaving the country, the Sussexes are back and arranging a careful program of public appearances. Will they complement the royal family, or compete with it?

#158. Draining peatlands is responsible for a few percent of our global warming

Detailed estimate going back to 1800 shows how much carbon is being liberated.

Much of the focus for human-caused climate change is on fossil fuels, which are creating most of our problems. But land use change is also a large component of the story, accounting for about one-third of total historical CO2 emissions. Deforestation plays a major role here, but practices like draining wetlands also factor in. Exactly how much of our history of eliminating wetlands has contributed to warming is challenging to figure out. But thanks to new sources of data that measure emissions from today’s surviving wetlands, it’s possible to put a better number on it.

A new study led by Jevan Yu at MIT focuses on peatlands—environments like bogs and fens where organic matter accumulates into thick layers of material. Some 600 billion tons of carbon are held in these vast stores of peat, which is more than you can find in all the world’s living vegetation, the researchers note.

Because these areas are permanently inundated, any oxygen in the water is quickly used up, leaving the job of decaying all that organic matter to anaerobic bacteria that work much more slowly and don’t digest everything. So instead of seeing organic carbon turning into the CO2 gas released by aerobic bacteria, in peatlands, a subset of it gradually turns into methane gas. Methane is an even more potent greenhouse gas than CO2 itself and ultimately gets oxidized into CO2. That means there’s a little trade-off from peatlands—most of the carbon they have is kept out of the atmosphere, but the carbon that does escape does so as methane.

Many peatlands have been drained by digging ditches that let water flow out to repurpose the land for agriculture or other uses. That lowers the water level, supplying oxygen to the exposed peat and ringing the aerobic bacteria dinner bell. Methane emissions get turned down, and CO2 emissions get turned way up.

The researchers used recent studies that measure gas emissions from peatlands to build a global model that estimated CO2 and methane release as the water level drops. The two gases respond differently to a given drop in water level, and CO2 in particular responds differently depending on where in the world the bog is located. Using reconstructions of water-level changes due to historical peatland drainage, the team ran their model starting in 1800.

The simplest way to represent the climate impact of both CO2 and methane changes was to calculate the change in Earth’s energy budget—the strength of the greenhouse effect, in other words. They calculated the total effect in 2020 at about +83 milliwatts per square meter of the Earth’s surface from the CO2 emissions, and about -9 milliwatts per square meter from the reduced methane emissions, for a total effect of +73 mW. That is 2.7 percent of the total human-caused change in Earth’s energy budget. (With the error bars on all these numbers, it’s 1.8–3.6 percent.)

Or to look at it another way, it means that around 4 percent of all the carbon that was buried in peatlands is now in the atmosphere.

Regionally, over half of the total CO2 came from Europe, almost a quarter came from Southeast Asia, and only 14 percent came from North America. The timing is quite different, as well, with the contribution from Southeast Asia only jumping up in the last few decades.

Draining a peatland only starts the process of releasing carbon, though. So that means past land-use change will still be producing emissions in the future. To examine this, the researchers also ran their model forward to 2100.

If peatland water levels were simply held constant from 2020 on, the total change in Earth’s energy budget would nearly double, from 73 to 138 milliwatts per square meter by 2100. Moderate restoration of peatland water levels could reduce that growth, and ambitious restoration could even cause the effect to shrink slightly below its 2020 potency in their analysis. This is a process we can halt if we really want to.

But since rolling all the way back to the landscape of 1800 is unlikely, elevated emissions will continue for a long time. That makes this another part of the climate system where change plays out slowly, with long-term consequences.

Nature, 2026. DOI: 10.1038/s41586-026-11339-0 (About DOIs).

#156. Joshua Kimmich critcizes Bayern bosses over Musiala health comments

Joshua Kimmich has slammed Bayern Munich's leadership over comments about Jamal Musiala's health issues. The bosses have questioned whether the player can return to previous form – and is still worth his high salary.

Bayern Munich's disappointing 2-1 Champions League defeat at Lille on Wednesday night – in which Jamal Musiala came on for the last quarter of an hour – was overshadowed by comments the club's captain made afterwards.

"It must never happen that something like this is played out at a player's expense. Of course, the club always comes first and is bigger than the player, but it's not bigger than the person," Kimmich told reporters in the mixed zone.

"That's an absolute no-go and not Bayern-like," he added.

Kimmich was responding to recent comments made by Bayern supervisory board members Uli Hoeness and Karl-Heinz Rummenigge.

"If he (Musiala) doesn't return to being the Jamal of old, then we need to have a conversation with his family, with the player, and, above all, with his agency – because, frankly, the current financial figures would no longer be justifiable," Karl-Heinz Rummenigge told Sky on Friday.

Honorary President Uli Hoeness has described the situation as a "dilemma" for the club, while CEO Jan-Christian Dreesen raised the prospect of talks with Musiala's management and family.

While theGermany player has struggled with a number of injuries over the past couple of seasons, his health came into sharper focus in August, after he collapsed on the pitch in a couple of preseason matches. He subsequently revealed that he had been diagnosed with a neurological condition which causes him to suffer from occasional "absence seizures."

Kimmich added that criticism of a players' performances was legitimate, but that concerns about Musiala's situation should have been dealt with behind closed doors.

"That's something you should sort out internally, around a table, rather than trying to resolve it publicly," he said.

"It's not that he has problems because he's unprofessional. He's there every day trying to get the maximum out of himself."

Meanwhile, Bayern bosses met with Musiala's agents on Tuesday, and clarified the timeline of events, dismissing speculation that the player could have concealed the condition when he signed his last contract extension.

Bayern said the two sides agreed that he was first examined for seizure symptoms in April 2025, two months after he had signed his contract extension, which makes him one of the club's top earners and is to keep him in Munich until 2030. Discussions between Bayern management and Musiala's representatives are reportedly ongoing.

#155. New UK-EU treaty has 10-year termination clause

The government and the EU have published the texts of two treaties agreed last week as part of a post-Brexit reset.

The treaties were published on Thursday afternoon, alongside legislation designed to enact the bulk of the deal.

The deal covering food exports has a 10-year termination clause that would mean if one party ended the agreement, they would have to pay "reasonable and proportionate costs" to rebuild border checks infrastructure, which will be significantly scaled back as part of the new deal.

Opposition leader Kemi Badenoch has said she would scrap the deal while Reform UK's Nigel Farage has suggested he would refuse to pay.

The Brexit reset deal has already sparked debate about its benefits, its costs, and a wider discussion on the future of the UK-EU relationship.

The deal covers British food exports, emissions trading and a youth experience scheme.

Two of the three legal texts were released on Thursday, covering the food trade and emissions trading scheme deals, alongside the government's cost-cutting bill to enact the deal.

A new assessment of the impact of the deal on different sectors and regions of the country was also released.

EU offers UK fresh deal on 'Made in Europe' scheme

Badenoch challenges Burnham over university fees for EU students

Farage helps launch new campaign against the UK rejoining the EU

Under the deal, food exporters will be significant beneficiaries from the scrapping of considerable red tape and checks, and dismantling of border facilities. An internal analysis forecasts a 16% boost the export of major agricultural commodities.

The industry and government hope the reduction in costs for food producers and sellers will be passed on to customers, but there may be no clear visible impact at the checkout.

Privately, industry insiders told the BBC earlier this week that there are too many moving parts in the world economy, in particular the energy price shock resulting from war in the Middle East.

Regarding the fine detail in the text of one of the treaties relating to the 10-year termination clause, European affairs minister Hamish Falconer said: "The Cutting Costs Bill allows us to implement two major trade agreements."

"It allows us to eliminate paperwork for our food and drink traders, protect billions of pounds worth of British exports from carbon taxes, and make doing business with our largest trading partner cheaper and easier."

A plan to link British and European emissions trading schemes will see more coverage across the UK's aviation and maritime industries.

The treaty text also contains finer detail of how the UK will observe, shape but not vote on relevant EU rules. The UK will be invited to attend European Commission meetings and expert groups as an observer, as well as taking part in emergency food security planning.

However, the UK will not be given voting rights.

Plans to use secondary legislation, including granting direct so-called "Henry VIII powers" to ministers to align UK and EU laws, are detailed in the legislation.

Such powers will enable ministers to make changes to bills with less Parliamentary scrutiny.

Outlining the agreement with the EU to MPs earlier this week, the prime minister said new agreements with the EU are "pragmatic" and "not a prelude to anything".

Andy Burnham also said it would be "unthinkable" for the UK to rejoin the bloc without a referendum.

Several former prime ministers have urged him not to reopen the Brexit debate. Burnham said they are "stuck in the past" and later claimed there would be "no banging on about Europe" from Labour.

Badenoch said Burnham has "oversold" the deal and was "trying to take Britain back to 2015".

#154. Finland's Prime Minister Sanna Marin concedes election

Finland’s left-wing Prime Minister Sanna Marin conceded defeat on Sunday in the Nordic country’s parliamentary election as the opposition right-wing National Coalition Party (NCP) claimed victory in a tightly fought contest.

The pro-business NCP was expected to win 48 of the 200 seats in parliament, narrowly ahead of the nationalist Finns Party with 46 seats and Marin’s Social Democrats on 43 seats, justice ministry election data showed with all ballots counted.

“We got the biggest mandate,” NCP leader Petteri Orpo said in a speech to followers, vowing to “fix Finland” and its economy.

He will get the first chance at forming a coalition to obtain majority in parliament as Marin’s era as prime minister was expected to end.

Popular abroad, at home Marin faces battle to keep her job

“We have gained support, we have gained more seats (in parliament). That’s an excellent achievement, even if we did not finish first today,” the prime minister said in a speech to party members.

Marin, 37, the world’s youngest prime minister when she took office in 2019, is considered by fans around the globe as a millennial role model for progressive new leaders, but at home she has faced criticism for her partying and her government’s public spending.

Finnish Prime Minister Sanna Marin takes drug test following partying backlash

While she remains very popular among many Finns, particularly young moderates, she antagonized some conservatives with lavish spending on pensions and education they see as not frugal enough.

The NCP has led in polls for almost two years although its lead had melted away in recent months. It has promised to curb spending and stop the rise of public debt, which has reached just over 70% of GDP since Marin took office in 2019.

Orpo accused Marin of eroding Finland’s economic resilience at a time when Europe’s energy crisis, driven by Russia’s war in Ukraine, has hit the country hard and the cost of living has increased.

Orpo has said he will negotiate with all groups to obtain a majority in parliament, while Marin has said her Social Democrats may govern with the NCP but will not go into government with the Finns Party.

Marin called the Finns Party “openly racist” during a debate in January – an accusation the nationalist group rejected.

The Finns Party’s main goal is to reduce what leader Riikka Purra has called “harmful” immigration from developing countries outside the European Union. It also calls for austerity policies to curb deficit spending, a stance it shares with the NCP.

Most notable of Marin’s foreign policy actions has been her push, along with President Sauli Niinisto, for the country to make a watershed policy U-turn by seeking NATO membership in the wake of Russia’s invasion of Ukraine.

That process is now almost complete, with Helsinki expected to join within days after all the Western defense alliance’s 30 members approved the accession.

#152. EU-Indonesia trade deal opens new markets, but challenges remain

The agreement offers new export and investment opportunities for Jakarta and Brussels. But can Indonesia turn Europe's growing interest in its markets and critical minerals into sustainable gains at home?

As the European Union seeks to deepen its presence in Asia and diversify its trade relationships, Indonesia is emerging as an increasingly important partner.

At a signing ceremony in Brussels last week, European Commission President Ursula von der Leyen described the bloc's trade agreement with Jakarta as one of its "most ambitious deals" in the region, linking markets of 730 million people.

The deal, the culmination of nearly a decade of negotiations, reflects the EU's efforts to diversify its trade partnerships, secure access to critical raw materials and reduce strategic dependencies on China.

For Indonesia, it offers opportunities to attract investment, expand exports and strengthen its position in global supply chains.

The agreement will gradually scrap tariffs on more than 98% of product categories, covering almost the entire value of bilateral trade over 15 years.

For the EU, gaining better access to critical raw materials, particularly nickel, and diversifying its supply chains are central to its strategic partnership with Indonesia, said Philip Schleifer, an associate professor of political science at the University of Amsterdam.

To view this video please enable JavaScript, and consider upgrading to a web browser that supports HTML5 video

"The main strategic interests are opening up a large and growing market for European companies, diversifying and deepening its economic partnerships, reducing strategic dependencies on China, and securing access to critical raw materials," Schleifer told DW.

Indonesia's mineral resources are particularly important for Europe's green and digital transitions. Nickel, for instance, is a key material for batteries and electric vehicles.

The agreement also creates opportunities for European investment in strategic sectors, including electric vehicles, electronics and pharmaceuticals.

But Indonesia has its own industrial ambitions. The government wants to move beyond exporting raw materials by developing domestic processing industries and retaining more value from its natural resources.

"The challenge for the EU is to reconcile its own interest in securing raw material supply with Indonesia's industrial development objectives," said Eline Blot, a senior policy analyst at the Institute for European Environmental Policy (IEEP).

Yose Rizal Damuri, an economist at the Center for Strategic and International Studies, said his institution's study estimates Indonesian exports could increase by around 60% in the coming years.

"Not only can Indonesia increase exports to Europe, but it can also become more strongly integrated into the global supply chains of European companies," Yose told DW.

To view this video please enable JavaScript, and consider upgrading to a web browser that supports HTML5 video

Potential beneficiaries include labor-intensive manufacturing sectors such as textiles, garments, footwear and furniture, alongside other Indonesian commodities and manufactured products.

The agreement could also encourage European investment and create employment opportunities. However, Yose stressed that access to the European market does not automatically translate into higher exports.

He identified three priorities for Indonesia: raising awareness of the agreement among domestic businesses, strengthening production capacity and improving the country's business climate.

"Various trade agreements signed by Indonesia have relatively low utilization rates. The value of exports using these facilities remains limited compared with overall export values," he said, emphasizing the need for better information and outreach to producers.

Syafruddin Karimi, an economist at Andalas University in West Sumatra, also highlighted the opportunity to diversify Indonesia's export markets and reduce dependence on major trading partners such as China, the United States and regional Asian economies.

However, he warned that Indonesian producers must improve their compliance with European standards.

"Products must be traceable, including whether their production involves environmental damage or labor practices that undermine human dignity," Karimi said.

The expansion of trade and investment could also increase environmental pressures, particularly in nickel mining, palm oil production and manufacturing.

IEEP analyst Blot warned that increased production without stronger environmental safeguards could worsen pollution, ecosystem degradation and industrial emissions. "Nickel mining and processing can contribute to pollution and ecosystem degradation, while palm oil remains closely associated with deforestation," she said.

To view this video please enable JavaScript, and consider upgrading to a web browser that supports HTML5 video

The EU Deforestation Regulation presents another challenge for Indonesian producers, particularly small-scale farmers who may lack the resources, technical capacity and documentation needed to demonstrate compliance.

Blot acknowledged that these requirements could create disproportionate costs for smaller producers and potentially favor larger companies.

However, she cautioned against using smallholders' livelihoods as an argument to weaken environmental regulations.

"Rather than weakening environmental requirements, the priority should be to ensure that smallholders receive adequate financial and technical support to meet them," she said.

Schleifer at the University of Amsterdam also warned that growing global demand for Indonesian natural resources could create environmental and social problems previously associated with commodities such as timber, palm oil and coal.

While Indonesia has considerable bargaining power because of its mineral wealth, the challenge is ensuring that increased demand translates into domestic industrial development rather than simply expanding resource extraction.

The agreement includes a dedicated Trade and Sustainable Development chapter covering climate change, biodiversity conservation, sustainable forest management and labor rights. It also provides mechanisms for cooperation and civil society participation.

However, there is no guarantee these commitments will be implemented. "Experience with other EU trade agreements suggests that there is often a big gap between sustainability commitments on paper and outcomes on the ground," Schleifer said.

To view this video please enable JavaScript, and consider upgrading to a web browser that supports HTML5 video

For Indonesia, the challenge also lies in preparing domestic industries to compete. Karimi stressed that the government, both at national and regional levels, must work with businesses to improve production standards, particularly among small and medium-sized enterprises.

Yose also emphasized the importance of a predictable business environment, warning that frequent policy changes and regulatory uncertainty could discourage investors.

"Without improvements in the domestic business climate, this will not become a reality. Everything will remain good on paper but will not turn into real momentum for change," he said.

Ultimately, the trade agreement offers Indonesia an opportunity to strengthen its position in international trade and attract investment from Europe.

But whether the agreement delivers broader economic benefits will depend on the country's ability to develop competitive industries, move into higher-value production, and ensure that environmental protection and workers' rights are not overlooked.

DW's Muhammad Athif Aiman contributed to this article.

#150. Ford unveils the GT41 as it hopes to win Le Mans again

Ford enters the World Endurance Championship in 2027 with the new prototype.

Sixty years ago, after two failed attempts, Ford won the 24 Hours of Le Mans with its GT40 race car. In the years since, the Blue Oval has been back to France more than a few times, with varying success. It could never replicate the GT40’s success winning overall, but it celebrated the 40th anniversary with a class win. Now it wants another shot at the really big trophy, and it debuted the car during the Detroit Lions-Chicago Bears football game today: meet the GT41.

“Revealing the Ford GT41 is a significant moment for everyone involved in this program,” said Dan Sayers, manager of Ford Racing’s Hypercar program. “It’s the result of an enormous amount of work across our engineering, design, and development teams and an important milestone on our journey towards 2027. We know the scale of the challenge ahead—the level of competition in Hypercar is incredibly high, and there are no shortcuts to success. Our focus has been on building the right foundations, bringing together the right people and making sure we learn as much as possible from every stage of development.”

Like many other entrants in the World Endurance Championship’s Hypercar class, Ford has chosen the rear-wheel-drive LMDh technical approach rather than building the GT41 entirely from scratch, as Ferrari and Aston Martin do with their cars. LMDh cars start with one of four approved carbon fiber chassis—in this case, Ford is working with Oreca, which has also partnered with Genesis and Acura.

The hybrid system and transmission are also standard parts. But Ford supplies the engine—a 5.4 L V8 based on the Mustang’s Coyote engine—as well as software to control the hybrid powertrain. A non-hybridized version of the Coyote has already been racing effectively under the hood of the Ford Mustang GT3.

The technical regulations also allow for more styling freedom than we often see in racing. The absolute amount of aerodynamic downforce is capped, as is the minimum drag, so OEMs are encouraged to use road car styling details to create a closer link between prototype and road car. I’ll probably need to spend more time looking at it to spot those road car cues, but I do see quite a few superficial similarities with the Genesis LMDh car, as well as the Ferrari Hypercar.

Despite being an American car brand, Ford doesn’t have plans as yet to enter IMSA’s WeatherTech championship, so don’t expect to see the GT41 next January racing at Daytona. Instead, it will take its first green flag in Qatar in March, the first of WEC’s nine races next season.

Ford should find it an extremely competitive championship. Although Peugeot and Alpine will not race next year, McLaren is also entering, alongside returning teams from Aston Martin, BMW, Cadillac, Genesis, Ferrari, and Toyota, which this year won Le Mans and the overall title, following three years of Ferrari domination.

#149. DR Congo demands answers over Ugandan general's threats

Gen Muhoozi Kainerugaba's social media posts have previously sparked diplomatic tensions

The Democratic Republic of Congo has summoned Uganda's top diplomat in Kinshasa over social media posts by Uganda's military chief, Gen Muhoozi Kainerugaba, who is also the president's son.

In a series of X posts, Gen Kainerugaba appeared to support a rebel takeover of Walikale, a mineral-rich territory in eastern DR Congo. He insisted that Uganda's military involvement was purely driven by security concerns.

The DR Congo's foreign ministry summoned Uganda's envoy to explain the remarks, describing them as "particularly serious" and damaging to diplomatic relations.

The Ugandan government has yet to respond.

The diplomatic row comes despite close military ties between the neighbours.

Their forces have been conducting joint operations against the Islamic State-linked Allied Democratic Forces (ADF) in eastern DR Congo since 2021.

Uganda has also recently been involved in efforts to mediate between the Congolese government and an armed group led by former warlord Thomas Lubanga in Ituri province.

Arrested generals and a threatened VP: President's son fuels Ugandan succession fever

The Ugandan general who lobs grenades on Twitter

In his posts, Gen Kainerugaba accused the Democratic Forces for the Liberation of Rwanda (FDLR), an armed group operating in eastern DR Congo, of targeting members of the Bahima ethnic community.

The Bahema are a traditionally cattle-herding community mainly based in DR Congo's north-eastern Ituri province.

They have historical and cultural ties to the Bahima people of neighbouring Uganda.

He also threatened a rebel takeover of Walikale, in North Kivu province, while defending Uganda's military presence in DR Congo.

Walikale is rich in mineral resources , including some of the country's largest tin deposits and several major gold mines.

Gen Kainerugaba's X account, which had nearly 1.4 million followers, has since become inaccessible.

It is unclear whether he deactivated it, as he has done in the past, or whether the platform had suspended it.

During the same week he declared: "My tweets in Uganda are directives that must be obeyed."

The posts followed reports that a DR Congo military aircraft had allegedly been shot down by rebels, with foreign mercenaries reportedly among those on board.

Gen Kainerugaba criticised the reported presence of foreign mercenaries in DR Congo.

"These remarks, which are particularly serious, have caused a major media incident and undermine the necessary restraint that must prevail in relations between sovereign states," the DR Congo foreign ministry said in a statement on Wednesday.

"The Congolese government expects the Republic of Uganda to clarify its official position and take the necessary measures to prevent such transgressions from recurring in the future".

The ministry added that DR Congo remained committed to regional peace and stability but would defend its dignity and institutions against "any form of provocation or interference".

It is not the first time Gen Kainerugaba's social media posts have strained relations between the two countries.

In February last year, he threatened to seize the eastern DR Congo city of Bunia, claiming that members of the Bahima ethnic community were being attacked.

In 2024, DR Congo also summoned Uganda's diplomatic representative after the general made threats against foreign mercenaries operating in eastern DR Congo.

The 52-year-old general, widely seen as a potential successor to his father Yoweri Museveni, has become known for his provocative social media posts.

In 2022, he sparked a diplomatic row with neighbouring Kenya after threatening to invade the country and capture its capital, Nairobi, prompting his father to apologise forn the remarks.

#146. Britain's pound is beating every other major currency this year

The British pound crashed to a record low last fall as investors rebelled against budget plans by former Prime Minister Liz Truss. Now, it’s enjoying a comeback.

Sterling hit its highest level against the US dollar in 10 months on Tuesday, topping $1.25 for the first time since June 2022. The pound, which has advanced about 3.3% versus the greenback since the start of 2023, is the best-performing currency among developed economies this year.

The UK currency has been boosted by indications the country’s economy is holding up better than expected. Activity is now thought to have expanded 0.1% in the final three months of last year, up from a previous estimate of no growth at all. Gross domestic product growth in January has been estimated at 0.3% after dropping 0.5% in December.

This resilience is bolstering expectations the Bank of England will maintain aggressive interest rate hikes despite concerns about the health of the global banking sector. Rising rates can boost the domestic currency because they help attract foreign investors searching for higher returns.

Inflation in the United Kingdom also jumped to an annual rate of 10.4% in February, underscoring the need for the Bank of England to maintain its tough approach.

The pound plunged close to $1.03 in September 2022 after the Truss government unveiled plans to boost borrowing while slashing taxes, unleashing panic in financial markets that fueled fears of a recession in the United Kingdom.

The International Monetary Fund predicted in January that the UK economy would contract by 0.6% this year, while all other advanced economies would grow, if only slightly.

“There was a lot of pessimism being priced into the pound,” said Francesco Pesole, a currency strategist at ING.

But the sharp pullback in energy prices and China’s reopening have provided some relief about the economic outlook since the start of the year.

“There was a big re-rating of growth expectations around Europe, and that impacted the UK,” Pesole said.

The euro has also been lifted by these dynamics, rising 2.3% against the US dollar in 2023. The pound’s rally has been sharper in large part because its 2022 declines were more severe, according to Pesole.

Both currencies have been aided by the greenback’s sharp drop from highs reached last September as recession fears have percolated in the United States.

A lack of clarity around the Federal Reserve’s next steps has also restrained the dollar in recent weeks. Investor speculation has increased that the Fed could pause or stop rate hikes due to concerns about the economy following the failure of Silicon Valley Bank last month.

Jordan Rochester, a currency strategist at Nomura, said he thinks the pound could rise to $1.30 this year and “potentially higher.” But he still sees risks given the uncertainty surrounding the Bank of England’s plans and how rate rises will feed back through the country’s economy. And Pesole cautioned that currency fluctuations are often overdone when markets are choppy, as they are now.

“In a volatile market environment, moves are exacerbated,” he said.

#144. FIFA says a cyberattack against it targeted a 'limited set of personal and business information'

World football's governing body says the cyberattack targeted personal and business information. They struck at a sensitive time for the under-fire organization and its president, Gianni Infantino.

FIFA told news agencies on Thursday that it had been targeted by a person or group seeking access to its data.

"FIFA can confirm that it has been the victim of a criminal cyberattack resulting in unauthorized access to a limited set of personal and business information," a FIFA spokesperson told the Reuters news agency, issuing similar comments to other outlets.

Reuters cited a person "with direct knowledge of the matter" as saying that less than 0.1% of FIFA's total data had been compromised.

This person also said that no files from the controversial, abandoned FIFA Forward Enterprise project that looked to sell a stake in the World Cup to private investors had been accessed.

FIFA said that it was working closely with the relevant authorities, including law enforcement, in the aftermath of the event.

It did not say when the attack took place, list the precise nature of the compromised data or specify how unauthorized access to FIFA data had been sought, amid reports of people having posed as FIFA officials rather than actually hacking the organization or otherwise compromising its cybersecurity systems.

Last week, a group calling itself the Handsome Boy Hacking Club posted a screenshot of what it said were FIFA files. Three days later, it appeared to take credit for the cyberattack when responding to a post saying that more than 12 gigabytes of internal documents had been posted.

FIFA did not explicitly state whether it was talking about the same supposed incident.

The breach comes at a sensitive time for the organization. Still reeling from the scandal following this summer's World Cup of its now-aborted plans to sell off World Cup commercial rights shares, it's now trying to secure another term for its embattled president, Gianni Infantino.

Infantino is the favorite for an election on March 18, when he is expected toface challenges from Kohzo Tashima of Japan and Philippe Diallo of France. But until the turbulence of the last six months, it seemed most likely he would simply stand unopposed and extend his 11-year tenure without a fight.
