#143. Thirteen dead as mine disaster highlights illegal hunt for gold

The flooded mine is in Bogor in West Java

It's been three days since Rani last saw her father as he left for work on Monday. Early on Tuesday, she received news that he was trapped underground.

Rais, 50, had been working as an illegal gold miner in Bogor, Indonesia's West Java province. On Monday night, he and at least 25 other miners were digging in an open-pit mine about 100m (328ft) deep, when torrential rain caused a nearby river to overflow and flood the two shafts.

Authorities on Thursday confirmed that 13 people had died and their bodies were recovered from the site. At least 13 more are thought to still be missing.

Search-and-rescue operations are ongoing, with personnel using ropes to access the depths of the dark, narrow and slippery mine shafts, which are continually being flooded from below.

Above ground, ambulances and medical personnel are ready and waiting to transport victims.

"We are coordinating with all search and rescue personnel involved in the operation to monitor water conditions and assess access to the mine shaft," Desiana Kartika Bahari, head of the Jakarta Search and Rescue Office, said in a statement on Wednesday.

"The depth of the shaft, narrow access and limited lighting are key considerations in determining the search strategy so that personnel can operate safely."

Rescuers have been working day and night to find survivors in the flooded mine shaft

Small-scale gold mining is banned in Indonesia, but remains widespread in rural parts of the mineral-rich archipelago.

Multiple studies show that over the past ten years the illegal industry has rapidly flourished, often in correlation with rising gold prices. In early 2026, those prices reached an all-time high â spurring a gold rush that prompted mine operators across the country to expand their operations.

This included state-owned mining company Aneka Tambang (Antam), which owns the business permit for the area in which this week's flooded mine is located. Antam have said the mine was not theirs and was operating without their authorisation.

On Wednesday, while rescue operations there were underway, West Java Regional Police separately announced that they had uncovered 24 cases of illegal mining across the province between October and November alone.

But Bisman Bakhtiar, Executive Director of the Center for Energy and Mining Law Studies, told the BBC earlier this year that the actual number of illegal mines in Indonesia is higher than the government's figures.

"[The government figures are] what's been recorded. That means there must be more than that," he said, noting that law enforcement operations have had little impact on the sector.

"It's still very massive and very big because this has been going on for a very long time."

Others allege that the illegal industry is underpinned by corruption.

Laode M Syarif, former deputy chairman of the Corruption Eradication Committee, said that unofficial mining is difficult to eradicate because every illegal mine has "backing", usually from "police and military or high-ranking officials in local and provincial governments".

"As long as these backers are not dealt with firmly, illegal mining will continue," he said.

In many cases, though, the allure of these mines is simple: they offer an economic lifeline to people with few other viable prospects.

Despite the value of gold having dipped in recent months, countless illegal miners throughout Indonesia are still eagerly digging for paydirt.

But the lack of regulation and poor construction in the pits also means accidents are relatively common â whether from landslides, floods, or exposure to hazardous gases, all of which have claimed lives in recent years.

Relatives of the missing Bogor miners have been waiting near the site for news of their loved ones

Earlier this year, in the same area as this week's flooded mine, 11 illegal gold miners reportedly died after a carbon monoxide leak inside a shaft.

Elsewhere, in West Sumatra, nine people were killed at an illegal gold mine in May after heavy rain triggered the collapse of a 30-metre cliff, burying 12 miners in total.

There are numerous reports of other such incidents across the Indonesian archipelago. But the illegal nature of the small-scale gold mining sector obfuscates the true number of casualties.

Even this week, when asked how many miners were in the pits in Bogor when they flooded, M Adam Hamdani, head of emergency and logistics at the Bogor Regency Regional Disaster Management Agency, said the total casualty figure "is still unclear".

"Some say 20 people, others say 29 people," Hamdani told Indonesian news outlet Detik. "The problem is that no one reported it and no one wants to report it."

The Indonesian Ombudsman on Thursday urged law enforcement officials to conduct a thorough investigation of the incident and to address the larger systemic issue behind it.

"This should be a momentum for all parties to resolve the [illegal gold mining] problem comprehensively, not just taking action after the incident or closing the holes discovered," said Abdul Ghoffar, a member of the Indonesian Ombudsman.

"We need to uncover the entire [illegal gold mining] chain," he continued. "We must not allow only the miners in the field to be seen and prosecuted, while those who control the activities, provide capital and facilities, store, and market the mined products remain untouched.

"Law enforcement must target the actors and networks that keep this illegal activity going."

As rescue operations continue and more bodies are recovered, the picture is becoming less murky. But even if there are survivors among those who remain missing, time may be running out.

More than three days since the shafts flooded, the relatives of the anonymous men in the mines are desperately waiting to hear whether their loved ones survived.

As she waits for news of her father, Rani says: "I hope he's found soon."

#140. Freigang banned — Germany star to miss 2027 World Cup

Germany forward Laura Freigang has been suspended until October 2027 after missing three doping tests, ruling her out of next year's Women's World Cup in Brazil. Freigang has denied any intention to evade controls.

Laura Freigang is set to miss next year's women's World Cup in Brazil after being suspended for missing doping tests, the German Football Association (DFB) said in a statement on Thursday.

Freigang, who has played 45 matches for Germany and scored 17 goals, will be suspended until October 7, 2027 after reaching a settlement with the DFB, the World Anti-Doping Agency and Germany's National Anti-Doping Agency. The suspension is for the next 16 months as it began retroactively on June 8, 2026, when she was notified by the DFB, and prohibits Freigang from playing for club or country.

"It's important to me to emphasize once again that I was never suspected of doping," Freigang said in a statement on Instagram.

"My agreement to a settlement does not mean that I share all the assessments of my case. The past few months have been extremely difficult for me. Another legal process would have entailed significant financial, mental, and emotional strain. This is why I decided to take this path, in the hope of finding some peace, clarity and perspective for myself," she added.

"It was never my intention to let it get this far. Sports have defined my life since I was little. It breaks my heart that, for now, I won't be able to step onto the field, help my team, or live the daily life that I love so much."

This brings to a close a case that has been ongoing for months. The 28-year-old had been accused of not being where she said she would be for a doping control on three occasions between March 2025 and February 2026.

In June, Freigang explained missing the tests "were not deliberately avoided appointments but inconsistencies or misunderstandings in the comprehensive details that have to be updated daily and which we as national team players have to update in the system on a daily and hourly basis."

The ban ends her World Cup hopes, something the DFB has also acknowledged.

"We are simply so sorry for Laura on a personal level because of this situation. The past few weeks have been very stressful for her," said DFB Sporting Director Nia Künzer. "We hold her in high regard both as a player and as a person, and — as we have done in recent weeks — we will continue to stay in touch with her and support her as best we can."

The situation also puts her future at her club, Eintracht Frankfurt, into question. Freigang's contract runs until June next year.

"The agreement that has now been reached provides clarity and planning certainty through a process that is unique to Eintracht Frankfurt — for us as a club, but above all for Laura," said Katharina Kiel, Frankfurt's director of women's football.

The club and Freigang, who has played 180 Bundesliga games in her career, are now set to enter talks to determine the shape of her contract moving forward.

#139. Here’s where you can save (a little) on the Ocarina of Time remake

#136. Why GTA VI’s version of Florida feels so real

Posts from this topic will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Posts from this topic will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Posts from this topic will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Virtual city expert Emma Fraser explains what makes Rockstar’s latest open world work.

Posts from this author will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Posts from this author will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

There are plenty of reasons why Grand Theft Auto VI is so popular: the 13 years of anticipation, the relatable lead characters and romantic storyline, the sheer level of depth. But another major factor is the sense of place. The GTA series is unmatched when it comes to re-creating the feeling of a bustling, real-world city, and GTA VI’s fictional rendition of Florida is the studio’s most lifelike setting yet. It has functioning buses and trains, plentiful buildings you can go into and explore, residents you can chat with, and huge areas where you can interact with wildlife far away from the bright lights of the Miami-like Vice City. For Emma Fraser, an assistant professor at UC Berkeley who studies urban ruins and virtual cities, the sense of realism has created a unique sensation while playing.

“There’s something about this particular game that is really discombobulating and distracting in a sense,” she says. “Because you have these moments where you think, ‘I’m in a place, I’m experiencing a location.’ You comprehend intellectually that it’s not real or that it’s at best a representation, but you still have this feeling in your brain and you’ll still dream about being in that place.”

Fraser teaches courses on topics including urban geography and virtual worlds and has written papers exploring topics like “mapping in the digital age” and “the virtual spectacle of the end of the city.” She says she started researching video game cities in part because, despite their influence and reach, they’re an underexplored space in academia. “I’m a geographer, and geographers are obsessed with cities,” she explains. “So much of geography is thinking about: How do we imagine cities? How do we navigate cities? How do we build cities? How do we map cities? And video games have all of that, but they’re not taken that seriously as an academic interest.”

Like much of the world, she’s been looking forward to GTA VI for years, particularly because of Rockstar’s on-the-ground approach to research. Before development on the game began, developers from the studio traveled to Florida to get an accurate picture. As GTA VI producer Rob Nelson told me, the starting point for the game was the setting, rather than the story or characters. “We just went around to feel it out,” he said. “Go to the Everglades, go to the Keys, go to the city, as many places as we can, and start thinking about what aspects of the place we want to incorporate.”

While there are plenty of open-world games, many of the most successful — from Elden Ring to The Witcher — are sent in fantastical spaces. GTA stands apart in how its various settings look and feel true to the real world. And Fraser believes that this is one of the key reasons the series has such universal appeal. “You know immediately how to function in a city,” she explains. “You know there’s going to be law enforcement, there are going to be city blocks, there are going to be pedestrians, cars, and buses. It’s this really interesting environment that gives you a lot of possibilities to get a player more immersed in your world, because they already know a lot about a city as a general concept.”

“You can’t hold a whole city in your mind at one time.”

Fraser describes GTA VI’s world as having “implied expansiveness.” It’s not a one-to-one re-creation of the real location. There are plenty of things missing; you don’t see schools or daycares, and parking spaces are relatively sparse. But the game gets enough details right to be recognizable. Fraser explains that as long as certain parts of the city look correct — the lighting, the coastline, the decoration of street corners, the major arterial roads — that’s enough to convince people that they’re in something resembling Miami.

“You can’t hold a whole city in your mind at one time,” she says. “Instead it’s these highlights. What [Rockstar] do very successfully in the game is those snapshots. They’ve got far more content that is recognizable as Florida or Miami than they’ve been able to fit into previous games. So it’s a much more overwhelming sense of being in that place.” The “implied expansiveness” also comes in the form of dialogue, radio programs, and TV shows that suggest a much bigger world by discussing places and moments that exist outside of the game you can actually explore.

This level of verisimilitude — combined with the fact that players can explore the city from multiple perspectives, everything from walking to flying a plane — has led to an experience that Fraser likens to virtual tourism, which can result in all kinds of different playstyles. “Some tourists are going to blow a lot of money on taxis and get to wherever they’re going as fast as they can,” she explains, “and some people just like sitting on a bus or a train and getting to know a place.” Similarly, there are certain players who will just experience the story and play through the main missions, and others who will get lost in side activities like bird watching.

“Some people just like sitting on a bus or a train and getting to know a place”

What’s also notable about GTA VI is how its relationship-focused premise is motivating some players to experience the world in a much less violent way. And that includes how they move through the city. Some people are driving more carefully to avoid killing civilians, while others are exploring spaces while both characters hang out together. “In the first few games, you’re this empty vessel. You’re a criminal, you’re doing criminal things,” says Fraser. “Whereas with GTA VI your motivation and the way you think about your character is really important.”

Now that the game is out and millions of players are venturing through Vice City, Fraser is hoping to use it to research the ways that real and virtual worlds intersect when it comes to how we navigate cities. GTA VI offers a lot of choice in this regard; you can still steal a Lambo and speed through the streets, but you can also hop on a train, put on some headphones, and listen to a podcast while getting where you need to be. “Because this world is so fully furnished, and you have so many options, you might start to import your own real-world practices into the game,” she says.

But at this point, what has struck her most is the attention to detail that separates the universe of GTA VI from its contemporaries. “You can’t just generate a generic city and stick icons in, which is what a lot of games do,” she explains. “You aren’t going to get a sense of a real place if you do it that way.”

#134. Africa's opposition voices face growing pressure

Opposition politicians and government critics say arrests, prosecutions and restrictions on public activity are making it harder to challenge those in power. Is a broader pattern emerging?

From Cameroon and Tanzania to Mozambique, Nigeria and Zambia, opposition leaders have accused authorities of limiting political freedoms through legal action and restrictions on public activity.

In each case, officials have said the measures are justified under national laws and security provisions.

"Cameroon is very sensitive, and it is important that we know when to criticize the government and when not to criticize the government openly," said Ngam Glory Ani Mbom, a citizen of the central African country.

She said fear of arrest and violence discourage political participation. "You cannot talk about democracy when opposition supporters cannot freely express their minds to the public," she told DW.

For Ndah Grimbald, deputy secretary-general of Cameroon's opposition Social Democratic Front, one of the biggest obstacles is organizing public meetings.

"The first major challenge we face as members of the opposition is the non-authorization of public rallies, public meetings," he said, noting that opposition proposals receive little attention in parliament. "Private members' bills are not even listened to."

Cameroonian law guarantees freedom of assembly but allows authorities to restrict gatherings on grounds of public order or national security. Organizers are also required to notify officials in advance.

In Tanzania, opposition leader Tundu Lissu was arrested in April 2025 and charged with treason. In November 2026, the High Court acquitted Lissu of treason but convicted him of incitement and sentenced him to four years in prison.

Prosecutors argued that Lissu's remarks were intended to disrupt Tanzania's 2025 general election. In its ruling, the court said it was satisfied that his statements were intended to "disrupt elections and instigate violence."

Lissu rejected the verdict, calling the case "political persecution." He told the judges: "I was not charged with incitement but you judges are convicting me with it just to impress the person who gives you the titles." He added: "Excuses were created to imprison me."

To view this video please enable JavaScript, and consider upgrading to a web browser that supports HTML5 video

Gerson Msigwa, Tanzania's chief government spokesperson, directly rejected allegations of political repression and human rights violations. "Concerns of diminishing civic space and restrictions on political parties to engage in political processes in the lead-up to the general election are unfounded and misleading," Msigwa said in response to Amnesty International's accusations of a "wave of terror" sweeping Tanzania ahead of the 2025 vote.

In Mozambique, opposition figure Venancio Mondlane faces five charges linked to protests after the disputed 2024 election, including incitement to terrorism and public advocacy of crime. Prosecutors have accused him of encouraging violence and civil disobedience. Mondlane has denied the allegations.

To view this video please enable JavaScript, and consider upgrading to a web browser that supports HTML5 video

"I have more than enough evidence. There's plenty to go around. Many of my statements were precisely in the opposite direction," he told DW, adding that the protests were intended to prevent violence.

Pedro Sinai Nhatitima, a judge counselor and spokesperson for Mozambique's Supreme Court, defended the court's decision not to allow live broadcasts of Mondlane's trial. He denied that the proceedings are politically influenced, saying the court will decide the case according to the law.

In Nigeria, police in Borno State recently arrested five men wearing T-shirts bearing the slogan "Tinubu Must Go!"

The police said the men were not arrested for their political views, but were found with suspected drugs and other items. The men face allegations including public disturbance and thuggery.

In Kano, police blocked access to a venue for a planned meeting, citing public safety concerns.

In Zambia, opposition leader Brian Mundubile has criticized what he describes as a retreat from democratic rights. The courts were closed on August 24, the final day for filing a presidential election petition. The judiciary cited security reasons, while police said intelligence-led security assessments were underway at several government institutions.

State House presidential aide Clayson Hamasaka denied that President Hakainde Hichilema had ordered the closure to prevent an election challenge.

To view this video please enable JavaScript, and consider upgrading to a web browser that supports HTML5 video

The same month, former opposition lawmaker and cabinet minister Mutotwe Kafwaya was shot dead during a security operation at Mundubile's residence. Authorities said the operation followed intelligence about individuals considered a national security threat, and that officers came under gunfire and returned fire.

Mundubile, who faces treason and sedition charges with other opposition figures, has called for fairness in the proceedings. "We expect an open court process. We are innocent of all the charges against us," he said.

Zambian constitutional lawyer Linda Kasonde has warned: "As you can see, our public institutions are on trial. Democracy in Zambia is on trial."

Cameroonian political analyst Kinang Derick Fai said that despite the setbacks, democratic development across the continent remains a work in progress.

"We are far from getting to the best level," he said. "It is a democracy still struggling to find its way."

DW correspondent Moki Edwin Kindzeka contributed reporting.

#132. GTA VI developers vote against unionization

Posts from this topic will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Posts from this topic will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Posts from this topic will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Workers voted 75 percent against the IWGB recognition agreement.

Workers voted 75 percent against the IWGB recognition agreement.

Posts from this author will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Posts from this author will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Workers at Grand Theft Auto VI developer Rockstar Games won’t unionize, after the vote for a formal recognition agreement failed, with only 25 percent of votes in favor of unionization. The Independent Workers Union of Great Britain (IWGB) said that Rockstar workers in the union will continue organizing even without formal recognition.

Voting ran from November 12th through 26th, covering the launch of GTA VI. If successful, the vote would have established a formal framework for collective negotiation with the studio over pay, overtime, bonuses, leave, and pensions, along with a right to consultation over future changes including AI implementation and layoffs. Without it, Rockstar won’t have an obligation to meet union representatives, though workers and union members can still lobby for change informally.

“For anyone at Rockstar who believes in a fairer workplace, a fairer sector and a fairer society - this isn’t a sign to retreat, it’s a sign to double down on what we stand for,” IWGB said in a statement sent to The Verge. “Hundreds of our coworkers voted in favour of our union at Rockstar, yet sadly we recognise we have more work to do to build understanding and win the trust of more of our colleagues. We’re ready to put in the effort to make that happen.”

When Rockstar fired 31 IWGB members last year, the union accused it of “the most blatant and ruthless act of union busting in the history of the games industry.” That case has gone to a UK employment tribunal, with a judgment still pending.

“We are extraordinarily proud of our team, the culture we’ve built together and the launch of Grand Theft Auto VI,” Rockstar head of publishing Jennifer Kolbe told Bloomberg News. “This result reflects our team’s strong support for continuing the direct relationships, collaboration and open dialogue that have helped make Rockstar Games so innovative and strong. As we look ahead, we’re excited to build on all we’ve accomplished together.”

#130. Hamas political official and wife among eight Palestinians killed in Israeli strikes on Gaza

Dozens of Palestinians have been killed in Gaza in near daily air strikes over the past month

A Hamas political official and his wife were among eight Palestinians killed in three Israeli air strikes across the Gaza Strip on Thursday.

Local sources told the BBC a residential apartment in Deir al-Balah was targeted at dawn, killing Khaled Abu Nada - the deputy education minister - and his wife, Abeer Mohammed Ismail Abu Nada.

A Hamas Civil Defence spokesman said that rescue teams and medical personnel had transferred eight bodies to Al-Aqsa Hospital in central Gaza and Al-Shifa Hospital in Gaza City.

In a joint statement, Israel's prime minister and defence minister said the operation was "part of a series of targeted killings" against Hamas officials.

Thursday's strikes also reportedly killed three Palestinians on al-Nafaq Street, east of Gaza City. Their identities have not yet been established, eyewitnesses said.

In a separate strike on Shati refugee camp, west of Gaza City, three Palestinians working for Hamas's security services were killed. They were identified as Yasser al-Lahham, Iyad Ahmed and Ibrahim al-Mashal.

Khaled Abu Nada and his wife Abeer Mohammed Ismail Abu Nada were killed in the air strike

In a post X, Israeli Prime Minister Benjamin Netanyahu confirmed the death of Khaled Abu Nada and said about 80 Hamas officials have been killed in the last month.

"No one in Hamas's leadership will succeed in evading us," he said, adding that the military would "continue to pursue our enemies and protect the security of Israeli civilians".

The Israel Defense Forces (IDF) said it had carried out a strike on a "senior" Hamas operative, and would provide further details later.

The attacks came a day after Mohammed Abu al-Ouf, 47, the head of Hamas's internal security service in northern Gaza, his wife and their two teenage daughters were killed in Israeli air strike on their apartment in Shati refugee camp in Gaza City.

On Tuesday, the overall commander of Hamas's internal security service was among 10 Palestinians reportedly killed in Israeli strikes.

Violence has continued in Gaza since Israel and Hamas agreed to a US-brokered ceasefire in October last year. Israeli strikes take place almost daily in areas under Hamas's control, which are home to 2.1 million people, most of them displaced.

At least 1,705 people have been killed in Gaza over the past 13 months, according to the territory's Hamas-run health ministry, whose figures are considered reliable by the UN.

Israel says six of its soldiers have been killed in Gaza during the same period.

The Gaza war was triggered by the Hamas-led attack on southern Israel on 7 October 2023, when about 1,200 people were killed and 251 others were taken hostage.

Since then, more than 74,772 people have been killed in Israeli attacks in Gaza, the health ministry says.

#128. French minister under fire for Playboy magazine cover

French government minister Marlene Schiappa has come under fire from members of her own party after appearing on the front cover of Playboy magazine.

Schiappa, who has been a government minister since 2017, appeared on the cover of the magazine to accompany a 12-page interview she did on women’s and LGBT rights. Schiappa, who is the current Minister for the Social Economy and French Associations, was photographed for the cover wearing a white dress.

Schiappa has been a long-time advocate for women’s rights and was appointed as the country’s first ever Gender Equality Minister in 2017. In this role, she successfully spearheaded a new sexual harassment law which allows for on the spot fines to be issued to men who catcall, harass or follow women on the street.

Her appearance has drawn criticism from political colleagues including French Prime Minister Elisabeth Borne.

Borne pulled up Schiappa over the cover, telling her it “wasn’t appropriate, especially during this period,” CNN affiliate BFMTV reported Saturday, citing a source close to the prime minister.

France is currently in the throes of a political and social crisis triggered by French President Emmanuel Macron’s move to push ahead with controversial pension reforms despite widespread public opposition.

“We are in the middle of a social crisis, there is the issue of policing, there are people between life and death, and I have the impression of being behind a smoke screen,” Sandrine Rousseau, Green Party politician and fellow women’s rights activist, told BFMTV Friday.

France wracked by more pension protests amid rising violence on the streets

French politician, Jean Luc Mélenchon who came third in the 2022 presidential elections criticized both Schiappa’s appearance and the decision by French President Emmanuel Macron to give an interview to children’s magazine, Pif Gadget, this week.

“In a country where the President expresses himself in Pif and his minister in Playboy, the problem would be the opposition. France is going off the rails,” Mélenchon tweeted Saturday.

Schiappa responded to her critics in a tweet on Saturday, saying: “Defending the right of women to have control of their bodies, that’s everywhere and all the time. In France, women are free. With all due respect to the detractors and hypocrites.”

French Interior Minister, Gérald Darmanin came to Schiappa’s defense during an interview with French news channel CNews on Sunday calling her a “woman of character.”

“I wanted to say that Marlene Schiappa is a courageous female politician who has her character and who has her style which is not mine, but I respect,” he remarked.

#126. Iranian drone kills 2 Italian workers in Kuwait, Meloni says

The Italians were employed as technicians for the Italian branch of German arms manufacturer Rheinmetall. One Kuwaiti and one Pakistani national were also injured in the attack. Iran has denied involvement.

An Iranian drone strike on Kuwait left two Italians dead on Thursday, the Italian Foreign Ministry said.

The Italians were working for the Italian branch of German arms manufacturer Rheinmetall. Italian news agency ANSA said the victims were two men aged 36 and 65.

It said they had been carrying out maintenance work on detection antennas of the Skyguard air-defense system.

"An explosion caused by a drone attack... occurred at a site where two Italian technicians from the company Rheinmetall Italia were working," the statement from the Foreign Ministry said.

Kuwait's Defense Ministry said a Kuwaiti and Pakistani national were injured in the "criminal attack" on a "vital site" and taken to the hospital.

Iran denied that it had attacked Kuwaiti airspace.

To view this video please enable JavaScript, and consider upgrading to a web browser that supports HTML5 video

Italian Prime Minister Giorgia Meloni said she learned of the "tragic" deaths of two Italian citizens in Kuwait during a European Council meeting.

"On behalf of myself and the government, I express my deepest sorrow and extend my heartfelt condolences to the victims' families," Meloni posted on X. "My thoughts are with all those affected by the attack and their families."

European Commission President Ursula von der Leyen also said she was "saddened" by the news.

"We must step up pressure on Iran to put an end to these unacceptable attacks against its neighbors and our partners in the Gulf," von der Leyen said. "Today's events are yet another reminder that security in the Gulf is a matter of security for us all."

Kuwait, a US ally in the Middle East, has been frequently targeted by Iranian attacks since the start of the US-Israeli war against Iran in February.

#124. Federal judge blocks the FCC from reviewing ABC’s licenses early

Posts from this topic will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Posts from this topic will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Posts from this topic will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

The judge has preliminarily sided with ABC, rejecting the FCC’s efforts to dismiss the case.﻿

The judge has preliminarily sided with ABC, rejecting the FCC’s efforts to dismiss the case.﻿

Posts from this author will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Posts from this author will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

US District Judge Loren AliKhan has blocked the Federal Communications Commission from conducting an early review of ABC’s broadcast licenses in a preliminary injunction, and rejected its efforts to dismiss ABC’s First Amendment lawsuit. ABC has accused the FCC of violating its free speech rights and claims the agency “waged a retaliatory campaign” against the network over content it broadcast. In her ruling the judge noted that the network was “likely to succeed on their retaliation claim.”

Although Disney, ABC’s parent company, wasn’t set to renew its broadcast licenses until 2028, last April the FCC ordered the network to file for an early license renewal claiming it was part of an investigation into Disney’s policies on diversity, equity, and inclusivity. The FCC’s move came a day after President Donald Trump demanded ABC fire late-night talk show host Jimmy Kimmel following a skit about Melania Trump.

In response to the FCC’s order, Disney and ABC sued the agency in August and accused the FCC of “punishing ABC for its speech” by threatening its broadcast licenses. The preliminary injunction issued by the judge on Wednesday prohibits the FCC from taking any further action in the early review of ABC’s broadcast licenses and noted that the network had shown that the court’s intervention in the agency’s actions was in the public interest.

”No one doubts that the Commission may enforce the law in the manner it sees fit,” the judge wrote. “But in doing so, Defendants may not retaliate against or punish Plaintiffs and other broadcast license holders for disfavored speech.”

#123. Is authoritarian Vietnam ready to phase out the death penalty?

Vietnam has voted to abolish the death penalty for six more crimes, continuing a decades-long trend of restricting capital punishment.

Vietnam, a country once considered one of the world's most prolific executioners, is steadily reducing the number of crimes punishable by death.

On November 20, the country's National Assembly approved a revised penal code that abolishes capital punishment for six offenses, including drug trafficking, rape of a person under the age of 16 and crimes against humanity.

When the legislation takes effect next March, just four offenses — treason, murder, terrorism and illegal drug production — will remain punishable by death, down from 44 in 1997.

Although Hanoi has given little indication that it intends to do away with capital punishment entirely, few countries that still regularly execute prisoners have narrowed its legal application so considerably.

The reforms are particularly striking given that they come as Vietnam's ruling Communist Party has intensified its crackdown on political dissent and independent civil society.

General Luong Tam Quang, the minister of public security, said the changes reflect humanitarian considerations, the need for more proportionate sentencing and Vietnam's international integration, while maintaining a tough approach to crime, according to the state-run Vietnam News Agency.

Vietnam's gradual reduction in the number of offenses punishable by death reflects an evolution in its criminal justice philosophy, said Tran Dai Lam, a lawyer at the ANVI law firm in Hanoi.

"There is a growing recognition that the effectiveness of criminal law depends not necessarily on the severity of punishment, but on the certainty that criminal offenses are detected, prosecuted and punished," he told DW.

Vietnam's latest reforms come barely a year after the National Assembly abolished the death penalty for eight other offenses, including embezzlement, bribery, espionage and the illegal transportation of drugs.

The removal of capital punishment for drug trafficking is particularly significant given the prominence of narcotics-related offenses in Vietnam's criminal justice system and the country's proximity to the Golden Triangle, one of the world's largest illicit drug-producing regions.

The new legislation also provides for existing death sentences to be commuted to life imprisonment for offenses that will no longer carry the death penalty.

To view this video please enable JavaScript, and consider upgrading to a web browser that supports HTML5 video

Hai Luong, a lecturer in criminology at Griffith University in Australia, told DW that Vietnam has been taking a "retentionist-but-reductionist" approach.

"Vietnam is reforming the death penalty rather than abolishing it," he said. "The trend is towards a more limited and selective use of capital punishment, but not yet towards its complete removal."

According to Luong, the gradual reduction in capital offenses reflects Hanoi's efforts to modernize its legal system without relinquishing its authority to severely punish what it considers the most serious crimes.

Growing international recognition that capital punishment has little additional deterrent effect is also informing Vietnam's approach, he explained.

Decades of harsh penalties for drug-related offenses have done little to stem the international narcotics trade, while the continued expansion of drug trafficking networks in Southeast Asia has raised questions about the effectiveness of executions as a crime prevention measure.

Vietnam's growing integration into the international community, including its participation in international drug control conventions and closer diplomatic ties with Western countries, has also given Hanoi greater incentives to align its criminal justice system with international standards, Luong added.

Vietnam's gradual retreat from capital punishment could also bring it closer to the European Union on human rights.

Brussels has repeatedly called on Hanoi to abolish capital punishment, a subject regularly raised during bilateral human rights discussions. The EU, one of the world's most vocal advocates of abolition, has long opposed capital punishment as part of its foreign policy.

An EU spokesperson told DW that Brussels welcomes "Vietnam's latest reduction in crimes punishable by capital punishment. This is a positive step and shows a disposition to consider gradual reform, leading towards a moratorium first and finally to the abolition of the death penalty."

"Any concrete step towards this ultimate goal would be very much welcomed by the EU," they added.

However, Vietnam's reforms still fall short of international human rights standards, particularly since the death penalty remains available for drug-related offenses that do not involve intentional killing.

Nor is there much evidence that progress on capital punishment is accompanied by improvements in other areas of Vietnam's human rights record, notably freedom of expression and association.

It is also difficult to establish whether Vietnam's legislative reforms have resulted in fewer executions.

The authorities classify execution statistics as state secrets. Amnesty International believes Vietnam continues to make extensive use of capital punishment, but it cannot provide reliable annual figures.

To view this video please enable JavaScript, and consider upgrading to a web browser that supports HTML5 video

Vietnam's capital punishment reform allows the state to continue executing people to fulfil its political and public security goals, "while also managing, or massaging, its international reputation through highly publicized 'reductionist' reforms," Daniel Pascoe, an associate professor of law at the City University of Hong Kong and an expert on capital punishment, told DW

For example, he added, although the recent abolition of the death penalty for drug trafficking is welcome, "it doesn't tell us whether the Vietnamese authorities will now simply charge a higher number of suspects with the drug production offense, which remains death-eligible."

Vietnam's reforms are part of a broader, if uneven, shift away from capital punishment in Southeast Asia.

Cambodia, the Philippines and East Timor have abolished the death penalty altogether. Malaysia abolished mandatory death sentences in 2023, while Indonesia's new penal code, which took effect this year, allows for death sentences to be commuted under certain conditions following a 10-year probationary period.

However, Singapore has increased its use of capital punishment in recent years, particularly for drug-related offenses, while Myanmar's military authorities resumed executions in 2022 after a hiatus of more than three decades.

Pascoe told DW that although the impact of Vietnam's reforms on execution numbers remains unclear, there is symbolic significance in one of Southeast Asia's most authoritarian governments visibly restricting the death penalty.

"If a one-party state like Vietnam can reduce the scope of capital punishment, why shouldn't Singapore, Indonesia, Thailand, Malaysia, Laos, Brunei and Myanmar also do so?" Pascoe added.

The number of countries actively executing people is close to being at an all-time low, but those that are actively executing, such as Iran and Singapore, seem to be increasing their use, Sara Kowal, co-director of Eleos Justice, a research and advocacy initiative against the death penalty at Australia's Monash University, told DW.

"Vietnam's dramatic reduction of death-eligible offenses is another really positive sign that the global trend towards abolishing the death penalty is continuing," Kowal said.

"It would be great to see Vietnam take the next step to establish an official moratorium on all executions," she added.

According to Amnesty International, 17 countries carried out executions in 2025, among them China, Saudi Arabia, Yemen and the US.

#121. The best Black Friday deals we’ve already found

Black Friday is nearly here, bringing with it steep discounts on Apple gear, games, and more.

Black Friday is nearly here, bringing with it steep discounts on Apple gear, games, and more.

Posts from this author will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

If you buy something from a link, The Verge may earn a commission. See our ethics statement.

The countdown to Black Friday is on, and many discounts have already arrived. Although the shopping holiday isn’t officially until tomorrow, November 27th, retailers are already dropping early deals on some of our favorite tech. Of course, with prices rising across the board, a good discount doesn’t necessarily mean you’re getting a historically low price. But we’re still seeing worthwhile discounts on a wide variety of tried-and-tested gear, with more expected later this weekend.

Below, we’ve rounded up the best early Black Friday deals we’ve found so far. Prices could drop further as Black Friday approaches, as is always the case, but these deals are worth considering if you’d rather shop early and beat the holiday rush. We’ll continue to update this guide as more retailers launch their holiday sales, too, so be sure to check back regularly as we get closer to the main event.

Apple’s AirPods Pro 3 deliver the best noise cancellation and sound quality of any AirPods yet, along with a more comfortable, secure fit and improved water resistance. They also feature built-in heart rate tracking for workouts and support live translations for a handful of languages. Read our review.

You can get an ad-supported Disney Plus and Hulu bundle for $4.99 a month ($8 off) for an entire year. Both streaming services feature a deep catalog of new and classic TV shows and movies, including The Simpsons, Andor, Toy Story 5, and The Fantastic Four: First Steps.

The Nintendo Switch 2 features a 7.9-inch LCD display, magnetically attached Joy-Con 2 controllers, backward compatibility with most original Switch games, and exclusive titles like Donkey Kong Bananza and The Legend of Zelda: Ocarina of Time. Read our review.

Do you ever wish you could turn on your coffee maker or hit the light switch without getting up? The SwitchBot Bot lets you do that. All you need to do is stick the inexpensive contraption on a standard button or rocker switch, which will allow you to control or schedule your pet feeder, garage door, and a wide range of other devices from your smartphone.

An HBO Max Basic subscription provides access to shows like A Knight of the Seven Kingdoms, The Last of Us, and The Pitt, as well as films like Superman and Weapons. You can get a year of the monthly, ad-supported subscription for $5.49 a month (about $6 off) until December 1st.

Apple’s latest AirTags feature an upgraded ultra-wideband chip for more precise location tracking, a noticeably louder speaker, and an expanded Bluetooth range. Read our review.

Amazon’s last-gen Fire TV Stick 4K Plus streams in 4K and supports Dolby Atmos, Dolby Vision, and Alexa Plus. It also ships with the redesigned Fire OS and, unlike more recent models, still lets you sideload Android TV apps.

Sony’s XM4 noise-canceling headphones were so good, the company brought them back with a few minor improvements. They still offer great ANC, a timeless foldable design, and delivering performance nearly on par with Sony’s newer flagships at a fraction of the cost.

Lego’s miniature bonsai trees can bring a sense of calm to even the most cluttered home. Building each tree — whether it be the black pine, the ginkgo, or the lilac-adorned wisteria — is designed to be a mindfulness practice in itself, centering your giftee as they go. They also make for a great piece of decor once finished, adding a touch of zen to any desk or shelf.

Apple’s most affordable laptop uses the same A18 Pro chip found in the iPhone 16 Pro. It features a vibrant 13-inch display, surprisingly good speakers, and 8GB of RAM, making it a solid option for students and anyone who needs a laptop for everyday tasks. Read our review.

Peacock is a great way to catch up on a wealth of content from NBC’s various networks, whether you plan to rewatch The Office or catch up on all things Bravo. A Premium subscription also grants access to films from studios like Universal Pictures and Dreamworks, along with live sports and a host of Peacock Originals.

Rechargeable AA batteries are handy until it’s time to charge them. Most wall chargers let you refill four, maybe six, at once. Zepath’s clever solution is to ship them in an easy-to-use USB-C charging case that charges eight simultaneously. The batteries themselves are long-lasting, too.

Sony’s DualSense Wireless Controller for the PlayStation 5 features immersive haptic feedback and resistance triggers, which feel great to use. It offers a built-in mic, a 3.5mm headphone jack, and a mute button; it also works with PC, macOS, and mobile devices via Bluetooth or USB-C.

Apple’s 11th-gen iPad is the successor to the previous 10-gen model. It has a new A16 chip and few other changes — and is the only Apple tablet to not support Apple Intelligence.

With 12.4mm drivers, active noise cancellation, and an IP54 rating, the CMF Buds 2A offer high-end features at an affordable price. You can also customize the sound, adjust ANC levels, and find the earbuds using the Nothing X app.

Anker’s durable travel adapter delivers up to 100W of power and works in over 200 countries. It doesn’t support AC plugs, but it does offer a trio of USB-C ports and a nifty LCD display to keep tabs on the charge percentage.

The Apple Watch Series 12 introduces AI-powered Audio Intelligence features, including sound recognition alerts, Live Rewind, and Siri Recap. It can also track your heart rate every five seconds, even when you’re not exercising, and offers faster charging than prior models. Read our review.

007 First Light, a third-person stealth action game from the studio behind Hitman, is arguably the best action game we’ve played this year. It features a wide variety of missions that have you sneaking through enemy territory, engaging in hand-to-hand combat, and taking part in high-speed car chases — you know, classic Bond stuff. Read our review.

Fanttik’s rechargeable electric screwdriver features three adjustable torque settings, a built-in LED light, and 16 S2 hardened steel bits for tackling everything from furniture assembly to small electronics repairs. It also comes with a magnetic storage case and supports USB-C.

No one should have to deal with a rat’s nest behind their desk when devices like Anker’s 2-in-1 USB-C cable exist. The handy USB-C cable splits at the end, giving you the ability to charge up to two devices from a single port. That means you can split power between a phone and tablet or juice a 16-inch MacBook Pro to 50 percent in just 20 minutes using a single port.

The Nintendo Switch 2 has 256GB of storage, but that won’t get you very far if you’ve built up a medium-sized digital game library. Samsung’s P9 256GB microSD express card doubles the console’s available storage.

You likely won’t find wireless earbuds with a more striking appearance than Nothing’s Ear 3A. They also sound fantastic, boast active noise cancellation, and offer robust EQ settings, making them a great pick for the price. Read our review.

Ember’s latest smart mug is designed to keep your coffee at the ideal temperature for up to three hours, with five temp settings between 120 and 145 degrees Fahrenheit. The third-gen version is similar to prior models; however, you can now adjust the heat settings directly on the mug and throw it in the dishwasher, which wasn’t the case before.

Despite being an entry-level variant of the B6 OLED, this model features four HDMI 2.1 ports, a 120Hz refresh rate, a capable processor, and pixel-perfect image quality and contrast for any content you want to watch. Lower brightness is likely the biggest compromise with getting a cheaper OLED.

JBL’s Tour One M3 noise-canceling headphones come with a touchscreen-equipped wireless transmitter that lets you wirelessly listen to audio from in-flight entertainment systems and other devices without Bluetooth. They also offer solid active noise cancellation and last up to 70 hours on a charge.

Hisense’s 2026 CanvasTV is a 4K QLED TV that doubles as wall art when you’re not watching. It features a matte, anti-glare display and runs on Google’s excellent TV software; it’s also a great pick for gamers thanks to its snappy 144Hz variable refresh rate and a pair of HDMI 2.1 ports.

The Elegoo Centauri Carbon 2 Combo is a beginner-friendly 3D printer that supports four filament spools. It features an enclosed build chamber, automatic bed leveling, and a large touchscreen, making it easy to produce reliable prints.

Moft’s Trackable Tripod Wallet is a MagSafe-compatible wallet that holds two cards and folds into an adjustable phone stand or small tripod. It also supports both Apple’s Find My network and Google’s Find Hub, offers Qi wireless charging, and includes a detachable shutter remote for taking photos.

Govee’s Table Lamp 2 is way more fun than your average table lamp. The color-changing device boasts built-in scheduling and more than 60 dynamic lighting effects, including preset scenes for various holidays. You can even create custom color combos for when “Dandelion” and other presets just aren’t cutting it.

Birdfy’s metal smart bird feeder features a wide-angle 4K camera that lets you get an up-close look at visiting birds, along with a 1.8-liter seed capacity and a built-in solar panel for charging. It also offers free AI-powered recognition of more than 6,000 bird species.

Bambu Lab’s P2S Combo is a 3D printer that can print in multiple colors and automatically dry its materials to help improve print quality. It also features a 5-inch touchscreen, a built-in camera for monitoring prints remotely, and AI-powered tools that can detect printing problems.

Headspace makes it easier to build mindfulness into your everyday life, whether you’re looking to manage stress, improve focus, or get a better night’s sleep. An annual subscription gives you access to hundreds of guided meditations, breathing exercises, and other tools for beginners and experienced users alike.

The Dreame X60 Ultra is a powerful robot vacuum and mop that does a great job of cleaning carpets thanks to its dual roller brushes and 35,000Pa of suction. It can climb over tall thresholds, squeeze under low furniture, and automatically detach its mop pads when vacuuming carpets.

Bose’s second-gen QuietComfort Headphones build on the original with better sound quality and support for lossless audio over USB-C. They also offer improved noise cancellation and are more comfortable thanks to their lack of “eardrum suck.”

Garmin’s midrange Forerunner watch has an OLED display, a week’s worth of battery life, and dual-frequency GPS for better accuracy. Read our review.

The Matic robot vacuum and mop is ideal for cluttered homes with pets, multiple floors, and a mixture of carpets and hardwood. It offers almost humanlike navigation, tackling high-pile rugs and mopping up wet spills. The quiet bot also requires less maintenance than many rivals thanks to its onboard water tank and dust bag. Read our review.

Azul is a game in which you arrange patterned tiles in order to create the most beautiful tapestry possible. Arranging tiles in a strategic sequence will help you build your score while limiting your opponents’ ability to grow theirs.

The Eufy Omni C28 combines excellent mopping performance with a small self-cleaning dock that’s well suited for apartments and other smaller spaces. It navigates around furniture and into tight areas with ease, while its roller mop does a great job cleaning hard floors.

#118. The four astronauts NASA picked for the first crewed moon mission in 50 years

Sign up for CNN’s Wonder Theory science newsletter. Explore the universe with news on fascinating discoveries, scientific advancements and more.

Astronauts who will helm the first crewed moon mission in five decades were revealed on Monday, queuing up the quartet to begin training for the historic Artemis II lunar flyby that is set to take off in November 2024.

The astronauts are NASA’s Reid Wiseman, Victor Glover and Christina Koch, and Jeremy Hansen of the Canadian Space Agency.

Wiseman is a 47-year-old decorated naval aviator and test pilot who was first selected to be a NASA astronaut in 2009. A native of Baltimore, Maryland, he’s completed one prior spaceflight, a 165-day trip to the International Space Station that had launched aboard a Russian Soyuz rocket in 2014. Most recently, Wiseman served as chief of the astronaut office before stepping down in November 2022, making him eligible for a flight assignment.

Wiseman will serve as commander of the Artemis II mission.

Hansen, 47, is a fighter pilot who was selected by the Canadian Space Agency for astronaut training in 2009. From London, Ontario, Hansen is one of only four active Canadian astronauts, and he recently became the first Canadian to be put in charge of training for a new class of NASA astronauts.

He will be the first Canadian ever to travel to deep space.

Glover is a 46-year-old naval aviator who returned to Earth from his first spaceflight in 2021 after piloting the second crewed flight of SpaceX’s Crew Dragon spacecraft and spending nearly six months aboard the International Space Station.

“It’s so much more than the four names that have been announced,” Glover said during the Monday announcement at NASA Johnson Space Center in Houston. “We need to celebrate this moment in human history. … It is the next step in the journey that will get humanity to Mars.”

Glover, born in Pomona, California, served in several military squadrons in the United States and Japan in the 2000s, and he completed test pilot training with the US Air Force. When he was selected for the NASA astronaut corps in 2013, he was working in the US Senate as a legislative fellow. All told, Glover logged 3,000 flight hours in more than 40 aircraft, over 400 carrier arrested landings and 24 combat missions.

Glover’s first mission to space was as part of the SpaceX Crew-1 team, which launched to the International Space Station in November 2020 for a six-month stay on the orbiting laboratory.

Koch, 44, is a veteran of six spacewalks — including the first all-female spacewalk in 2019. She holds the record for the longest single spaceflight by a woman, with a total of 328 days in space. Koch is also an an electrical engineer who helped develop scientific instruments for multiple NASA mission. Koch, a native of Grand Rapids, Michigan, also spent a year at the South Pole, an arduous stay that could well prepare her for the intensity of a moon mission.

The Artemis II mission will build on Artemis I, an uncrewed test mission that sent NASA’s Orion capsule on a 1.4 million-mile voyage to lap the moon that concluded in December. The space agency deemed that mission a success and is still working to review all the data collected.

If all goes to plan, Artemis II will take off around November 2024. The crew members, strapped inside the Orion spacecraft, will launch atop a NASA-developed Space Launch System rocket from NASA’s Kennedy Space Center in Florida.

The journey is expected to last about 10 days and will send the crew out beyond the moon, potentially further than any human has traveled in history, though the exact distance is yet to be determined.

The “exact distance beyond the Moon will depend on the day of liftoff and the relative distance of the Moon from the Earth at the time of the mission,” NASA spokesperson Kathryn Hambleton said via email.

After circling the moon, the spacecraft will return to Earth for a splashdown landing in the Pacific Ocean.

Artemis II is expected to pave the way for the Artemis III mission later this decade, which NASA has vowed will put the first woman and person of color on the lunar surface. It will also mark the first time humans have touched down on the moon since the Apollo program ended in 1972.

The Artemis III mission is expected to take off later this decade. But much of the technology the mission will require, including spacesuits for walking on the moon and a lunar lander to ferry the astronauts to the moon’s surface, is still in development.

NASA is targeting a 2025 launch date for Artemis III, though the space agency’s inspector general has already said delays will likely push the mission to 2026 or later.

The space agency has been seeking to return people to the moon for more than a decade. The Artemis program was designed to pave the way to establishing a permanent lunar outpost, allowing astronauts to live and work deeper into space long term as NASA and its partners map a path to sending the first humans to Mars.

Vanessa Wyche, the director of NASA’s Johnson Space Center, declined to provide details to CNN about the selection process. But she emphasized the diversity of the Artemis II crew, which includes men and women rather than only a staff of White male test pilots as has been the case for historic missions of the past.

“I can tell you, they still all have the right stuff,” Wyche said. “We have requirements different than we did (when we) just had test pilots” on inaugural missions.

Koch said in an interview with CNN’s Ed Lavandera that the group found out they were selected a few weeks ago.

“We were all sent to a meeting that was on our calendars under a different pretext that didn’t sound as lofty as the one it was going to be,” Koch said. “And accidentally two of us were very late to that meeting.”

She said the offer rendered her “speechless.”

“It truly is an honor,” she added. “It’s an honor — not to get myself in the space — but because it’s amazing to be a part of this team that’s going back to the moon and on to Mars.”

An interview with the four astronauts will air on “CNN This Morning” on Tuesday, which starts at 6 am ET.

CNN’s Kristin Fisher contributed to this story.

#116. Germany: IG Metall union says deal reached with employers

IG Metall, representing millions of workers in Germany's metal and electrical industry, is the country's largest trade union. The deal came after more than 24 hours of talks with employers.

Germany's powerful IG Metall trade union said Thursday it has reached a pay deal with employers after more than 24 hours of the fourth round of talks.

IG Metall and employers agreed on the following points in their agreement:

IG Metall represents 3.7 million workers in the German metal and electrical industry in total. It is Germany's largest trade union.

"The result is a compromise under difficult conditions," Baden-Württemberg IG Metall boss Barbara Resch said.

The talks come amid job cuts across multiple sectors in the German economy, such as the automotive sector.

IG Metall represents workers in major German companies such as Volkswagen, Mercedes-Benz, Siemens, ThyssenKrupp, and Salzgitter AG, among others. All of those aforementioned companies have announced job-cutting plans within the last two years.

German companies are attempting to slash labor costs and cut benefits as the German economy faces increasing competition from China and pressure from US tariffs. This also means that labor unions are being presented with less desirable packages for employees.

#115. Android app developers will soon have to share their address publicly

Posts from this topic will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Posts from this topic will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Posts from this topic will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Posts from this author will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Posts from this author will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Developers have until February 1st 2027 to declare whether or not they’re a “trader” under UK and European law. Broadly speaking, Google says the definition applies if you earn revenue from your apps, especially if that revenue is regular or at high volume. Developers are exempt if their apps are only a hobby or non-commercial, or if they simply don’t publish them in the UK or Europe.

If they self-declare as a trader, developers’ contact details will be shared publicly starting March 1st, including a phone number and address. Google doesn’t accept PO boxes for developer payment profile addresses, meaning many independent developers without an office will either have to share their home address online or pay for a registered agent or other service that provides a business address. One independent developer told The Elec they were considering renting a private office solely to comply with the new rules. We’ve asked Google to confirm why developers aren’t permitted to publish a PO box address, and why details are being shared globally, and will update this article when we hear back.

Apple is subject to the same regulations, but is handling them differently to Google. It will only display developer addresses within the relevant European countries, rather than publishing them worldwide. It’s also allowing developers to supply PO box addresses, so long as they provide Apple with a receipt or bill that links the PO box to their regular address.

Update, November 26th: Added mention of developers’ option to use a registered agent service for a business address.

#113. Nine injured, three critically, after Bangkok oil refinery explosion

Watch: Fire and smoke billows out of refinery owned by energy company Bangchak Corporation

Nine people have been injured after an explosion at an oil refinery in Bangkok, authorities have said.

Three of them are in critical condition, according to news agency AFP, which said the local hospital had revised its earlier assessment of four.

A fire broke out after the blast at the Phra Khanong facility run by energy giant Bangchak Corporation in the east of Thailand's capital at around 14:45 (07:45 GMT).

The company said the fire was brought under control and put out within 30 minutes, adding that the cause of the incident was being investigated.

Black smoke could be seen across Bangkok's skyline after the blast, as local officials told residents to leave the area.

An alert sent to mobile phones by Bangkok authorities confirmed that the fire was under control, but urged people to avoid the area.

Eyewitnesses shared video clips on social media of flames and smoke rising above the refinery.

On a visit to the scene, Thailand's energy minister Akanat Promphan said it appeared the impact of the explosion was limited because the fire started in a distillation tower about 100m (330ft) from where oil was being stored.

He said the incident was concerning as the refinery was in a residential area and the government was suspending production until it could be confident that operations would resume without any issues.

Bangchak Corporation said air quality monitoring in the immediate area around the refinery was within normal ranges.

It confirmed that production at the site, which can process up to 120,000 barrels of oil a day had been shut down. The company has a second refinery in Chonburi province, about 50 miles (80km) from Bangkok, handling a daily capacity of about 180,000 barrels.

An explosion occurred at the Bangkok refinery in July 2012, sparking a fire but no reported injuries.

If you’re looking for a new laptop or a gift for the student in your life, you can save $100 on the MacBook Neo at several retailers, in both storage sizes and all four colors. The 256GB model is currently discounted to $599.99 at Amazon and Costco, while the 512GB model with Touch ID is available for $699.99 via the same retailers (Amazon / Costco). None of the retailers currently list an end date for the discount, which is the biggest price drop we’ve seen since Apple raised the laptop’s MSRP by $100 in July in response to the ongoing RAM crisis.

Apple’s budget-friendly MacBook leverages the A18 Pro processor, a chip originally found in the iPhone 16 Pro, to keep costs down. Both the 256GB and 512GB models feature 8GB of memory, and my colleague Antonio G. Di Benedetto was impressed by the vivid 13-inch screen and speakers, the latter of which are better than those in many cheap (and even midrange) Windows laptops. The Neo isn’t super performant, especially in terms of storage speed and total memory, but it’s great for everyday tasks, casual use, and students.

#110. Protests in Iraqi Kurdistan at plans for government in Baghdad to take over oil, border crossings

Thousands have gathered in the capital of northern Iraq's semiautonomous Kurdish region, waving flags and voicing opposition to plans for the federal government to take control of oil operations and border crossings.

Protesters took to the streets of Irbil, also often written Erbil, in Iraqi Kurdistan early on Thursday in large numbers, waving massive flags of the semiautonomous region in northern Iraq.

They were responding to federal government plans announced earlier in the week for Baghdad to take control of oil operations and border crossings previously run by Kurdish authorities.

Iraqi Prime Minister Ali al-Zaidi had said the measures were part of a bid to stop oil smuggling when announcing the changes.

The plans marked an escalation in the long-running power struggle between the central government and Kurdish authorities that have largely governed themselves since 1991, albeit with varying levels of recognition and authority.

Rival Kurdish parties joined forces for the large protests in the regional capital.

Protests were also reported to have spread to several other towns and cities beyond the capital.

Iraq's 2005 constitution, written following the US-led invasion that toppled Saddam Hussein, formally recognized the Kurdish region and its government. But authorities in Erbil and Baghdad have remained in a tug of war over the control of land, oil and money ever since.

Tuesday's decision, which received backing from Iraq's Federal Supreme Court a day later, brings border crossing in the Kurdish region under federal government management. It places approval for oil exports from the region under the authority of the federal oil ministry and transfers the management of oil fields in four contested provinces — Kirkuk, Diyala, Salahuddin and Nineveh.

To view this video please enable JavaScript, and consider upgrading to a web browser that supports HTML5 video

The Kurdish regional government's Cabinet said on Wednesday that the decision from Baghdad "violates both the Constitution and the constitutional rights of the Kurdistan region," saying it represented "an attempt to return Iraq to a centralized regime."

Prime Minister al-Zaidi, who took office earlier this year with the backing of US President Donald Trump, also dismissed Kirkuk's operations commander and police chief and the head of the Border Ports Commission. He said the measures reflected Iraq's "independent national will."

Regional Prime Minister Masrour Barzani said his government was "surprised" by al-Zaidi's decisions and called them "completely unacceptable."

Iraq's Supreme Court ruled on Wednesday that the authority of the Kurdish Regional Government (KRG) only covered the land it administered on March 19, 2003, the day before the US-led invasion that unseated Saddam Hussein.

Kurdish peshmerga forces moved into many disputed areas after 2003, including the oil fields covered by this week's orders.

The 2005 constitution tried to solve the fate of some of the disputed territories in the lands between the borders to Syria and Iran that are home to a host of ethnicities and communities but with Kurds generally the majority group.

Article 140 called for reversing Saddam-era policies of expelling Kurds and moving Arabs in, and called for a census and a referendum on whether the disputed regions should join the Kurdistan region. Neither was held.

Kurdish forces then gained more of the disputed territory after Iraqi forces were forced out by the so-called "Islamic State," with Kurdish pershmerga for subsequently reclaiming some of the land with some international assistance.

The oil-rich region is highly important to both administrations, in a country that relies on oil revenues for the vast majority of its public sector budget.

Internal political tensions within Iraqi Kurdistan have further complicated the issue in recent months, with the Federal Supreme Court hearing an ongoing challenge against the current government's legitimacy after a party withdrew from the coalition cabinet. But rival Kurdish political factions seem to have found common ground on this issue at least.

The Forerunner 265S may not be the newest or flashiest Garmin, but it’s one that I’ve always loved most in testing. For runners with smaller wrists, it fits nicely. The battery life is pretty good thanks in part to its OLED touchscreen. When I reviewed it a few years ago, I called it the Goldilocks of fitness watches at the time, and the only thing that bugged me was its price. But now that it’s down to $299.99 (a $150 discount off its MSRP) at Amazon and Best Buy for Black Friday, I can easily recommend it.

Garmin’s Forerunner 265S is identical to the standard 265, only it features a slightly better battery life and a smaller 42mm build for those with more petite wrists.

The neat thing about Garmins in general is that you really don’t need the latest models to get the most out of the company’s platform. Its watches tend to get firmware updates for several years after launch, which is how long the average Garmin user tends to hold on to theirs. This particular watch is best suited for folks who want something a bit slimmer and smaller, enjoy a pop of color, and want a little extra fitness tracking prowess — but don’t need the whole kit and caboodle that sends the price up by hundreds.

#106. Tributes for Duran Duran guitarist Andy Taylor after his death aged 65

Watch: Taylor on the 'overwhelming reaction' to Duran Duran's music

Simon Le Bon, Sir Rod Stewart and Gary Kemp are among the pop stars who have paid tribute to Duran Duran guitarist Andy Taylor following his death at the age of 65.

Taylor co-wrote some of the group's biggest hits including Save A Prayer, Rio and Girls On Film.

Le Bon and his bandmates paid tribute, saying "he brought an indelible fire and swagger to our music", while Sir Rod remembered his "dear pal" and Spandau Ballet's Kemp called him "a great player who set the tone for the 80s".

Taylor's wife Tracey called him "the rock of our family", adding: "We will miss him every single day." He died after "a long and courageous battle with prostate cancer", Duran Duran said in a statement on Wednesday.

Andy Taylor: Duran Duran's livewire guitarist who was a Wild Boy on stage and off

He died "peacefully at home, surrounded by his family, on Monday 23rd November", a statement said.

Paying tribute, his Duran Duran bandmates said: "From the moment he arrived in Birmingham from the North East with a guitar and an unshakeable belief in what we could become, he brought an indelible fire and swagger to our music.

"His playing drove the songs that took us around the world, and with a dry wit that was matched only by his wonderfully unsentimental, no-nonsense outlook on life he kept us grounded and gave the band its edge through the extraordinary years that followed."

They said their thoughts were with his wife, children and grandson.

Le Bon said there was a "a massive Andy Taylor shaped hole in my heart".

"Andy never took the easy musical route. His overdrive overdrove us into being the only funk/rock/punk band in the New Romantic movement, with songs that still survive, decades later."

Watch: Andy Taylor tells the BBC about his cancer diagnosis in 2023

Sir Rod, who wrote five songs with Taylor for the 1988 album Out of Order, recalled them making "some good music together" and having "some outrageous fun".

Kemp said Taylor "influenced many of us along the way with his sparse staccato style", adding: "He brought shameless rock to the mix."

In a band tribute, Spandau Ballet called him "a hugely talented musician and an important part of the British music scene".

US guitarist Nile Rodgers, the co-founder of Chic, recalled "countless great days and nights" with his "dear friend".

Belinda Carlisle said she was "blessed" to have worked with Taylor, who played the guitar solo and was in the video for her song Mad About You.

Former Led Zeppelin frontman Robert Plant called Taylor "a tough fighter", while Then Jerico's Mark Shaw remembered him as "one of the greatest musicians and songwriters I've ever heard and had the privilege to work with".

Taylor (second left) married his wife Tracey at the Chateau Marmont hotel in Los Angeles in 1982

Taylor's wife Tracey, a hairstylist he met during the early days of Duran Duran, and whom he married in 1982, said: "For all the incredible experiences music brought him, family always came first for Taylor, adding: "Nothing made him happier than having us all together."

She said he faced the last few years "with so much courage and determination" and said they were "grateful" for the "kindness and support shown to him by fans throughout his illness", saying it brought him "immense comfort and strength".

Andy Taylor's guitar riffs powered some of Duran Duran's best-known songs

The son of a fisherman, Taylor taught himself guitar, bass, and drums and began playing professionally at age 13.

He played with several bands around the UK before answering a music paper advertisement for a "live-wire guitarist", which led to his audition for Duran Duran in Birmingham in April 1980.

The group went on to achieve global fame through the New Romantic movement, which combined slick, melodic synth-pop with aspirational music videos and chiselled good looks.

Taylor's guitar lines - full of glassy, powerful riffs - were often a highlight of their songs, from the choppy opening of Girls on Film to the sleazy scuzz of Hungry Like The Wolf.

Duran Duran bridged the sounds of pop, funk, disco, punk and new wave with a sound that remains indelibly tied to the 1980s

Duran Duran were one of the biggest pop acts of the 1980s, and its five members became global hearththrobs.

Their indelible hooks and airbrushed music videos found a natural home on the newly launched MTV, giving their career a huge boost in the US.

They became so popular in the US that the press labeled them the leaders of a "second British invasion" of the charts - alongside groups like Spandau Ballet, Human League and Eurythmics.

During a hiatus in 1984, Andy and John Taylor enlisted Robert Palmer for their successful but short-lived side project the Power Station.

The following year, the guitarist decided to quit Duran Duran after a miserable experience performing at Live Aid.

"We'd reached rock bottom and we had absolutely nothing to say to each other," he wrote in his autobiography, Wild Boy.

"We drove to the venue through streets packed with excited rock fans, but inside the bus the atmosphere was as if we were on a way to a funeral."

He went on to play on Palmer's Addicted To Love, and released a solo album, Thunder, in 1987.

Taylor quit Duran Duran at the height of their fame in 1985, but rejoined the band several times in the 2000s and contributed to their most recent album, Danse Macabre

After a period writing and producing albums for other artists, he rejoined Duran Duran in 2000, lasting for just one album (2004's Astronaut) before parting ways again.

In 2022, when the band were inducted to the Rock and Roll Hall of Fame, Taylor was notably absent from the ceremony, despite public plans for a reunion.

When the group took the stage, Le Bon read a letter from the guitarist, in which he revealed his diagnosis with stage 4 metastatic prostate cancer.

Nonetheless, he contributed to the following year's Halloween-themed album Danse Macabre, marking his final collaboration with the band.

Duran Duran at the BBC, on BBC Four on Friday 27 November at 21:30 GMT

Sounds of the 80s, on Radio 2 on Saturday 28 November at at 18:00 GMT
