#104. Netflix’s coziest show has made a very quiet return

Posts from this topic will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Posts from this topic will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Posts from this topic will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

A new season of Midnight Diner: Tokyo Stories is streaming now, though it’s easy to miss.

A new season of Midnight Diner: Tokyo Stories is streaming now, though it’s easy to miss.

Posts from this author will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Posts from this author will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

The bigger Netflix gets, the easier it is to miss stuff. This is true of all digital platforms, of course, but it’s especially true of Netflix of late, as the streaming service’s lineup now also includes a growing library of reality shows, live-programming, and even games. It makes it easy for some of the best shows to zoom right past you, unnoticed — which almost happened to me with Midnight Diner.

If you haven’t seen it, Midnight Diner is probably the most comforting thing available on Netflix. The show follows the patrons of a cozy little restaurant tucked away in the backstreets of Shinjuku, which is only open during the unusual hours of midnight to 7:00AM. This, in turn, attracts a unique blend of customers, who help each other out under the watchful eye of the shop’s owner (Kaoru Kobayashi), known only as “master.” The show aired for three seasons on Japanese TV, before being picked up by Netflix for a handful more under the subtitle Tokyo Stories.

What’s especially notable about the show’s return is the huge gap: season 2 of the Netflix iteration streamed in 2019, and it took seven years for it to come back. The break was so long that season 3’s first episode, which premiered in October, is about the restaurant’s struggles during the pandemic. There are a bunch of cute scenes of the diner’s regulars sneaking outside to check if the restaurant is open yet, and wondering if it will ever be able to. If nothing else, the break was a chance for the reliable master to finally take a vacation.

But once Midnight Diner’s new season gets going, it’s right back to everything that made it beloved. Each episode uses a different dish as a way of exploring a particular story. An overworked manga artist imagines the life he missed out on over plates of spicy konjac, while a pair of old friends reconnect while munching on pork cutlet sandwiches. What’s remarkable about the show is the way it balances being a charming, comforting watch with exploring truly difficult and heartbreaking topics. It’s the combination of sweetness and sadness that makes it work so well. It’s as likely to make you laugh as it is to make you cry.

And unlike most Netflix series, the whole season isn’t streaming all at once. Instead, new episodes come out every Tuesday; so far six of a planned 10 episodes are available. It’s a welcome way to spend an evening, as the show’s slow pace and warm sense of humanity can feel like all-too rare traits in the current streaming landscape.

But the main reason I’m even writing about Midnight Diner’s return is that it has been so easy to miss. There’s been next to no promotion for it — all I could find was a trailer on Netflix’s Japanese YouTube channel — and I only happened upon it accidentally after the initial premiere while browsing aimlessly through Netflix’s new releases. This, despite being someone who has watched and rewatched the previous seasons multiple times. You’d think I’d get a notification or something. Netflix has always been full of hidden gems, but now that it’s busier than ever, those gems are all the more hidden.

#101. Myanmar refuses to comply with parts of ICJ genocide ruling

Myanmar's president Min Aung Hlaing has led the country since he seized power in a 2021 military coup

Myanmar will not comply with "inappropriate" parts of the genocide ruling by the International Court of Justice (ICJ), its leader Min Aung Hlaing has said in his first public comments since the court decision.

The ICJ's landmark ruling last week found Myanmar guilty of committing genocide against its Rohingya minority in a 2017 military crackdown that killed thousands and forced more than 700,000 to flee.

Myanmar denied committing genocide, saying its forces had responded to attacks by armed groups. It is the first state to have been found in violation of the 1948 Genocide Convention.

Min Aung Hlaing said "the term 'genocide' had seriously affected the country's reputation", state media report.

"Myanmar would take actions appropriate for a responsible country but would not comply with requirements it considered inappropriate," the country's coup leader-turned-president said at a meeting on Wednesday, according to state media.

The statement did not specify which parts of the ruling the country objects to.

The genocide case, brought by The Gambia in 2019, centred around so-called "clearance operations" initiated in 2017 in Rakhine State, where many of the Rohingya Muslim minority resided.

The ICJ determined that about 10,000 Rohingya were violently killed during the operation, while more than 700,000 are thought to have fled to Bangladesh by foot or by sea. The court found that approximately 815,000 had been targeted in some way.

The verdict was reached by a majority of nine votes to six among a panel of judges from around the world.

The ICJ also found in favour by majority that Myanmar had violated several other parts of the 1948 Genocide Convention.

The ruling is final and cannot be appealed.

The ICJ ordered Myanmar to pay proportionate reparations, but said an exact figure should be determined at a later date.

The court also ordered Myanmar to stop ongoing violations and immediately investigate those suspected of breaking the Convention.

But the ICJ does not have the power to enforce these demands. Instead it has to rely on the UN Security Council and political pressure to make its rulings stick.

While the ruling was largely symbolic, it was welcomed by Rohingya communities and human rights groups who have long called attention to the suffering faced by the Rohingya.

More than a dozen countries have acknowledged the ICJ ruling, and the UK has announced sanctions on several senior Myanmar military leaders.

Myanmar has been devastated by civil war since Min Aung Hlaing led a military coup to oust civilian leader Aung San Suu Kyi in 2021. The move turned Myanmar into an international pariah, and its military regime has faced sweeping international sanctions.

Since then, Min Aung Hlaing has been trying to reintegrate Myanmar into the international community, but it remains largely shut out of key diplomatic arenas.

#100. Trump says he urged Putin to 'pull back' military aggression in Europe

US President Donald Trump claimed that he talked with Vladimir Putin about ceasing Russia's military aggression in Europe and expressed his belief in the possibility of a deal between Russia and Ukraine.

US President Donald Trump said on Thursday that he had urged his Russian counterpart, Vladimir Putin, to cease "military aggression" in Europe during their talks in China.

"I spent a lot of time talking about the fact that I would like to see President Putin pull back all Military aggression against territories throughout Europe, in the hopes that a Deal can be made between Russia and Ukraine, and made quickly," Trump wrote on his Truth Social network.

However, the US president did not specify which European territories he mentioned when communicating with Putin. This week, Poland — a NATO member — accused Russia of deliberately violating its airspace.

Trump added that he believes there is a "strong possibility" of such a deal between Russia and Ukraine. "Thousands of people are being killed each and every month, A COMPLETE BLOODBATH," he wrote in the post.

To view this video please enable JavaScript, and consider upgrading to a web browser that supports HTML5 video

Trump's post on Truth Social came a day after he hosted NATO Secretary General Mark Rutte in Florida. They issued a joint statement afterward calling on Russia not to expand the war in Ukraine.

"We also talked about, at length, the importance of Russia and Ukraine avoiding any actions that intentionally, or unintentionally, expand the War — and the importance of obtaining a peaceful resolution to the War, as rapidly as possible," the statement said.

Trump's comments represent a shift in tone after he cordially called Putin a friend during their meeting in China last week. Approaching Putin, smiling Trump said, "Who is he? He's my friend." Then he shook hands warmly with the Russian leader.

The echoes of the scene reverberated throughout Ukraine and other European nations, which are worried that Trump might side with Putin.

The US president had said he could end the war in Ukraine on the first day of his second term in office.

To view this video please enable JavaScript, and consider upgrading to a web browser that supports HTML5 video

Depite Trump's comments, the war shows no signs of ending, with both Russia and Ukraine continuing their mutual attacks.

A fire broke out on Thursday at the Slavyansk ECO oil refinery in Russia's southern Krasnodar region following a drone attack. The strike caused serious damage, according to a social media post by the company.

According to Reuters, two industry sources estimated that Ukrainian drone attacks have reduced the combined crude processing volumes of Russian oil refineries by 45% since late October.

At the same time, Ukrainian President Volodymyr Zelenskyy claimed that Russia is preparing for a large-scale attack on Ukraine's energy infrastructure, logistics, and essential services.

Zelenskyy also said that crews are working to restore electricity in six Ukrainian regions after Russia launched ballistic missiles and 113 drones overnight.

#98. Beijing promised to 'fight back' over Taiwan leader's US visit. But this time it has more to lose

An anticipated meeting between Taiwan’s President Tsai Ing-wen and US House Speaker Kevin McCarthy in California this week has sparked concerns of a repeat of the pressure campaign China launched last year when then-Speaker Nancy Pelosi visited Taipei.

At that time, Beijing encircled the island democracy with unprecedented military drills – firing multiple missiles into its surrounding waters and sending dozens of warplanes speeding across a sensitive median line dividing the Taiwan Strait.

It also cut off contact with the United States over a number of issues from military matters to combating climate change, in retaliation for what it viewed as a violation of its sovereignty.

This time, Beijing has already threatened to “resolutely fight back” if a Tsai-McCarthy meeting goes ahead.

It also slammed Washington for allowing Tsai to stopover in the US while en route to and from official visits in Central America, warning it could lead to “serious” confrontation between the two powers.

A defiant Tsai staked out her own ground, pledging as she took off on her 10-day trip not to let “external pressure” stop Taiwan from connecting with the world and like-minded democracies.

But the optics of the meeting, taking place in California and not Taiwan, and its timing – at a particularly thorny moment in China’s foreign relations and ahead of a presidential election in Taiwan that could reset the tone of its relationship with Beijing – may see Beijing tread more carefully this time, or at least not escalate further, analysts say.

“This puts the burden on China not to overreact, because any overreaction is only going to push China further away from the world,” said Yun Sun, director of the China Program at the Stimson Center think tank in Washington.

That doesn’t mean, however, that Beijing won’t be closely watching Tsai’s movements as it calibrates its response – and decides how much military might to flex over her meeting with an American lawmaker on American soil.

The opacity of China’s system – and the potential for competing interests within its vast bureaucracy – also make it difficult to accurately predict its response.

“Every time Taiwan does anything that China doesn’t like, the Chinese react with their own military coercion,” Sun said. But in the current situation, “they have to consider the consequences of overreaction,” she added.

The expected meeting, which McCarthy’s office announced earlier this week would take place on Wednesday, also comes at a precarious moment in US-China relations.

Washington and Beijing are struggling to stabilize their communication amid flaring tensions over issues from a downed suspected Chinese surveillance balloon to semiconductor supply chains – raising the stakes of potential damage to that relationship if Beijing lashes out as it did when Tsai met Pelosi.

Taiwan is still feeling the fallout of that response last August, with Chinese military forces now regularly making incursions over what had previously been an informal but largely respected border of control between Beijing and Taipei in the Taiwan Strait. Taiwan’s official Central News Agency also reported Monday that Tsai would meet with McCarthy, citing Tsai’s presidential office.

But a meeting between Tsai and the leader of the Republican majority in the US House of Representatives, who ranks second in line to the Presidency, would mark another symbolic moment for Taiwan and the US, which only maintain unofficial ties.

For Tsai, who is entering the final year of her two-term presidency, “it’s clearly a capstone event,” according to Wen-Ti Sung, a political scientist at the Australian National University’s Taiwan Studies Program. “She has this image as the Taiwanese president who has taken US-Taiwan relations to new heights, and who … has been able to give Taiwan almost unprecedented international visibility,” he said.

That increased visibility – and enhanced cooperation with the US – has followed China’s mounting pressure on the island, which sits fewer than 110 miles (177 kilometers) from the mainland coast.

China’s Communist Party claims the self-governing island democracy as its own despite never having controlled it, and has vowed to take the island, by force if necessary.

China thinks it's diplomatically isolating Taiwan. It isn't

The party has undertaken a sweeping expansion of its military capabilities over the past decade under leader Xi Jinping – and ramped up its pervasive economic, diplomatic and military pressure on Taiwan.

That’s driven concerns, among some in Washington, that Beijing is preparing for an invasion, though China’s official language still suggests that scenario is not its preferred option for achieving the claimed goal of “reunification.”

It is those pressures – and how to support Taiwan against unilateral actions by Beijing – that are likely to be on the table when Tsai, McCarthy and a bipartisan group of US lawmakers sit down on Wednesday.

Congress has been a pillar of increasing American support for Taiwan in recent years. Lawmakers regularly visit the island and drive bipartisan legislation enhancing support and cooperation.

While the US switched its diplomatic relations to Beijing decades ago, it maintains unofficial ties with Taiwan and is bound by law to provide the democratic island with the means to defend itself.

Under Washington’s longstanding “One China” policy, the US acknowledges China’s position that Taiwan is part of China, but has never officially recognized Beijing’s claim to the island of 23 million.

Though McCarthy does not have Pelosi’s decades-long record of advocacy regarding China, the California Republican is now a leading voice pushing for closer scrutiny of Beijing, and meeting Tsai could help him to burnish that image.

Last month, McCarthy told reporters that meeting Tsai in the US would not impact whether he travels to Taiwan in the future – something he had earlier said he wanted to do.

A meeting in California, on US soil, is widely seen as less likely to provoke Beijing than a McCarthy visit to Taiwan.

Pelosi’s trip – the first from a lawmaker of that rank to the island in 25 years – generated a fever pitch of nationalist and anti-US rhetoric in mainland China.

This time, so far, domestic conversation in China’s heavily controlled media sphere has been significantly muted.

But the stakes remain high – including for Beijing itself – over how it responds, analysts say.

As Taiwan prepares for a presidential election in January, a fierce response could push voters away from Taiwan’s main opposition party Kuomintang (KMT), widely seen as more friendly toward Beijing.

It could also jar with another high profile trip happening now: a tour of mainland China from former Taiwan president and senior KMT member Ma Ying-jeou, the first visit from a current or former Taiwan leader since the end of the Chinese Civil War in 1949.

Ma’s tour is a “once in a half a century opportunity to send a conciliatory message between the two sides, Beijing shouldn’t want to tank that,” said Sung, the political scientist.

What you need to know about China-Taiwan tensions

China is also acutely aware that its actions toward Taiwan are under a significantly brighter global spotlight following the invasion of Ukraine by Russian President Vladimir Putin, a close diplomatic partner of Xi. Putin’s rhetoric over Ukraine has echoes of how Xi speaks of Taiwan.

Beijing has recently sought to position itself as an agent of peace in that conflict – especially as it aims to repair frayed ties with Europe.

This week, as Tsai is expected to meet with McCarthy, French President Emmanuel Macron and European Commission President Ursula von der Leyen will head to China – an important opportunity that Xi may not want to overshadow with military posturing.

An aggressive response also risks stoking confrontation with the US, not yet six months after Xi and US President Joe Biden called to enhance communication during a face-to-face meeting in Bali.

“(A less overtly aggressive response) would imply that Beijing does not wish to escalate tension with the US to a level that can risk getting out of hand,” said Steve Tsang, director of the SOAS China Institute in London.

“A reset in US-China ties is not on the agenda, but an easing of tension is not beyond the realm of possibility.”

#96. Amazon and Meta reportedly in talks to lift blockade on AI agent Muse

Amazon may be having a change of heart about Meta’s AI agent, Muse. The two tech giants are reportedly discussing a potential deal that would allow Meta’s popular AI agent to connect directly to Amazon’s store.

The talks would mark a significant reversal from just two months ago, when Muse users discovered they were unable to purchase products on Amazon and instead received an error message when attempting to check out.

According to a Wednesday report by Fortune, citing people familiar with the discussions, Amazon and Meta have held multiple rounds of talks about the matter. Two key issues remain under negotiation: how search results would be presented and how advertising would be handled under a potential agreement.

One of Amazon’s biggest concerns is how third-party shopping agents like Muse could affect its advertising business. In October, Amazon CEO Andy Jassy argued that few customers were making purchases through outside shopping agents.

Amazon may also have been waiting for Muse to mature before agreeing to integrate it with its marketplace. If Muse places the wrong order or creates a problem with a purchase, Amazon could ultimately be left dealing with frustrated customers and unhappy sellers.

Fortune reported that executives are concerned that third-party shopping agents could weaken Amazon’s direct relationship with consumers, according to people familiar with the matter.

Any potential agreement between Amazon and Meta would reportedly require approval from both companies’ CEOs, Fortune also reported.

Meta and Amazon have not yet responded to TechCrunch’s requests for comment.

#95. Hope for millions as cheaper HIV prevention jab reaches Africa

Lenacapavir has received WHO's approval for HIV prevention

Uganda is set to receive its first supplies of a cheaper version of a ground-breaking injection to prevent HIV, raising hopes of expanding access to the drug across Africa, where the burden of the disease remains highest.

The first global shipment of the generic drug, known as lenacapavir, arrived in Zambia on Wednesday, with deliveries to Uganda expected in the coming days, the Global Fund has said.

The drug, administered as an injection twice a year, is nearly 100% effective at preventing HIV infection and is expected to cost just $40 (Â£30) per person annually.

Zimbabwe is due to receive supplies early next year, with several other countries expected to follow.

The arrival of the generic drug comes just a year after the original version, developed by US pharmaceutical giant Gilead Sciences, became available in sub-Saharan Africa.

The original version cost about about $28,000 annually in the US, putting it beyond the reach of many of those in developing countries.

Focus on Africa podcast: Lenacapavir offering new hope for HIV prevention?

Landmark deal paves way for cheaper HIV protection jab

Lenacapavir works by targeting the protective protein shell of HIV, disrupting the virus's ability to multiply and establish an infection in the body.

In a landmark clinical trial involving more than 5,000 adolescent girls and young women in Uganda and South Africa in 2024, none of those who received the injection contracted HIV.

Health experts say the injection offers a major advantage over existing HIV prevention pills, known as pre-exposure prophylaxis (PrEP), which must be taken every day and can carry social stigma.

The World Health Organization (WHO) recommended the drug for HIV prevention in July 2025.

The Global Fund described the arrival of the drug in Africa as a "major breakthrough" in the fight against HIV, saying cheaper supplies would help reach millions more people at risk of infection.

"These deliveries mark an unprecedented acceleration and expansion in access to a highly effective, quality-assured generic HIV prevention medicine in low- and middle-income settings," the group said in a statement on Wednesday.

More than 189,000 people across 10 countries have already received their first injection of lenacapavir, according to the Global Fund.

The organisation expects 24 countries to have received supplies by the end of this year and, working with the US government, plans to reach at least three million people by the end of 2028.

It is eventually expected to be available in 120 low- and middle-income countries.

Sub-Saharan Africa accounts for nearly two-thirds of people living with HIV globally, with adolescent girls and young women disproportionately affected.

South Africa has the world's largest HIV-positive population, with nearly eight million people living with the virus. It is among the countries expected to receive the cheaper injection.

Some 41 million people worldwide were living with HIV last year, while 570,000 died from HIV-related illnesses, according to the WHO.

The rollout comes amid concerns that sweeping cuts to US foreign aid under President Donald Trump's administration could undermine decades of progress in tackling HIV in Africa, where many countries rely heavily on international funding for prevention and treatment.

The UN aims to end Aids as a public health threat by 2030.

#93. Todoist confirms 6% of customers’ details were stolen in data breach

Task management app Todoist said on Tuesday that a hacker gained access to its production systems and stole customer data, including email IDs, password hashes, and account creation dates. The company said around 6% of its customers were affected and blamed stolen credentials for the breach.

Todoist said the attacker used stolen credentials to access the company’s version control system and source code between November 22 and 23. The hacker then changed employee access controls and reached the app’s cloud infrastructure via an employee’s work account.

The hacker downloaded credentials of early Todoist users whose passwords were stored using an older hashing method, but didn’t change any source code, the company said. Todoist said it has blocked the attack path and reset passwords as a mitigation step, as well as disabled the Todoist for Outlook email add-on.

For a small number of people, about 0.001% of its userbase, the hacker also searched through tasks, comments, and attachment names to look for credentials for other services, like API keys and access tokens.

The hacker also pulled Todoist API tokens and Slack integration tokens, and looked for emails, addresses, and attachment file names through the Todoist for Outlook extension if it was connected to accounts and had permission to read emails.

The company said it has sent a notice to those affected and asked them to reset their credentials and change their login details in other places with the same password. The company is asking users to also update API tokens, reconnect integrations, and revoke or replace any access keys, recovery phrases, or credentials they had stored in Todoist comments.

Todoist said it has also reset its internal access structure and how remote command execution works in its cloud environment to prevent such incidents in the future.

Todoist’s founder and CEO Amir Salihefendić apologized to users about the incident on X, and said the company will take a closer look at why it happened.

The to-do app’s data breach is the latest in a burgeoning list of data breaches in which hackers use stolen credentials to access data and systems at enterprises. So far this year, we’ve seen several companies fall victim to this form of attack, including Uber and Klue.

#92. Allstate suffers multiday tech outage ahead of Thanksgiving

U.S. insurer Allstate has been experiencing a tech outage since Monday morning, leaving customers nationwide unable to access their accounts, review their policy information, file claims, pay their bills, and more, just ahead of the U.S. Thanksgiving holidays.

The outage has been impacting both the company’s website and mobile app, TechCrunch confirmed.

The breakdown follows a report from earlier this month that Allstate was planning to lay off up to 10% of its staff as part of an AI-driven efficiency push. (The company had not confirmed the report, which was attributed to unnamed sources.)

Allstate’s outage is the latest in a series of disruptions to services such as Microsoft Outlook and Teams, the airlines, Apple Pay, and several banks. Trump administration officials have said the outages are not being caused by cyberattacks, but are rather being caused by a recent set of upgrades to systems, many of which run on older technology. Those systems are now in need of more frequent security and bug fixes as AI makes it easier and faster to discover vulnerabilities.

At present, a message on Allstate’s website advises customers trying to log in that its services aren’t available, and that the company is “working to restore access as soon as possible.” On X, Allstate is employing what appear to be automated replies to customer complaints, asking them to DM the Allstate account with more information.

Downdetector, a crowdsourced outage-reporting website, shows users began reporting Allstate’s outage from Monday morning. However, the company hasn’t yet issued a formal statement about the disruption, what’s causing it, or when it may be resolved. In the meantime, local insurance agents have been fielding calls and emails from customers who aren’t able to use the company’s online services.

The timing of the outage is particularly bad given that Thanksgiving is a big travel holiday in the U.S., which means there will be car accidents. Customers will be left without a way to get in touch with their auto insurer to file claims and get roadside assistance through Allstate’s app.

Allstate did not immediately return a request for comment. On Wednesday evening, after publication, the company sent the following statement (below). It did not answer questions about the outage’s cause or planned resolution.

Allstate customers can reach us in many ways, including online, by phone and through local offices. Some access has been restricted over the last 48 hours, but we continue to be available for customers and respond to and settle claims. To the extent service interruptions impact a customer’s ability to make a payment, they won’t lose coverage or be charged late fees.

Updated after publication with Allstate’s comment.

#90. WhatsApp now lets you schedule messages in group and individual chats

WhatsApp on Wednesday started rolling out a much-sought-after feature: the ability to schedule messages in both individual and group chats.

The feature is rolling out globally to all users across iOS and Android. Once it becomes available, you can type a message and long-press the send button to schedule it. You can choose to send a message anywhere between 10 minutes and 14 days later.

Users will be able to see all their scheduled messages and delete them if needed. It’s not clear if there is a way to edit the schedule of a message.

Notably, scheduled messages will be sent even if your phone is off or outside network coverage. You can schedule messages containing text, photos, and videos.

The feature could prove useful for wishing someone a happy birthday on time or chatting with people across time zones. Businesses may also find it easier to schedule texts and updates to their customers, and people who use the app to coordinate work will likely find themselves using scheduled texts to update their teams.

WhatsApp rivals like Telegram and Signal have had the ability to schedule messages for a while now, so the Meta-owned chat app is late in rolling it out.

#89. X’s latest update for creators focuses on vertical video

X announced new creation tools Wednesday, many of them focused on short-form vertical video. The updates are part of the platform’s effort to compete with social networks such as YouTube and Instagram as the format continues to grow in popularity.

The tools are designed to help creators make professional-quality vertical videos, share original content more easily, and earn more. They also give creators more ways to record, edit, and publish content directly on the platform without relying on third-party apps.

A key addition is an upgraded media editor that lets creators produce polished vertical videos without leaving X. Recording features include the ability to capture video in segments and use green-screen effects, the company said.

The editor also offers a range of postproduction tools. Creators can convert horizontal videos to vertical format, automatically generate captions, trim footage, add text overlays, choose custom thumbnails, and adjust photos before publishing.

Thanks to a recently announced update, users can now add up to 20 photos and videos to a single post, which audiences can swipe through horizontally. X says the feature is intended for sharing event recaps, behind-the-scenes moments, and product launches.

Availability of the new features varies by region, X said.

Earlier this month, the company introduced a clipping feature that lets users take snippets from longer videos, add their own commentary, and post the clips without leaving X. Clips credit and link to the original video, plus the person who creates a clip and the original poster can earn from its views.

Another feature, “React with Video,” launched earlier this year. Similar to TikTok’s “stitch” feature, this lets creators respond to posts with video commentary while displaying the original post in the background.

X has also introduced new earning opportunities and updates to its creator monetization programs in recent months. These include the “Video Kickstart Bonus,” an invitation-only pilot that rewards selected creators for publishing original vertical videos under three minutes long and without watermarks.

#88. On farewell trip, Steinmeier calls for more investment in Africa

Cote d'Ivoire, Ghana, Kenya: On his last trip to Africa, outgoing German President Frank-Walter Steinmeier called on companies at home to be bolder and seize opportunities in Africa.

"My time as president is coming to an end, and that is why it was important to me to travel to Africa one more time — to the continent that is particularly close to my heart," said Frank-Walter Steinmeier during his final official trip to Africa. Over the course of 20 years, as president and before that as foreign minister, the veteran politician has made 30 trips to African countries.

"For me personally, things have come full circle today," the president added during his opening remarks at the German-African Business Summit in Ghana. He was alluding to the first German-African business summit in Berlin in 2015, which Steinmeier opened while he was still foreign minister in the administration of former Chancellor Angela Merkel.

This week, in addition to Ghana, he visited Cote d'Ivoire and Kenya with a business delegation from Germany.

A lot has happened since 2015, Steinmeier said. And yet: "We are still far from where we could be." Addressing Germany's business community, he said, "Be curious, take risks."

As the applause rang out after the speech, Jude Numfor, who works for the energy company REI in Cameroon, smiled. He too sees German companies as too risk-averse.

"When the Chinese come, they say 'let's do it,' and the Germans say 'let's prepare it'," he told DW.

Meanwhile, Africa's economy is growing rapidly. German-African foreign trade rose by 10.5% in 2025 to €63.4 billion ($69.4 billion). Africa's trade with the Asia-Pacific region is growing more slowly, but the absolute figures are also significantly higher.

This shows there is a great deal of potential in Africa's markets — especially in light of the emerging African Continental Free Trade Area (AfCFTA), which encompasses most of the continent.

When it comes to direct investment, German companies are more hesitant than others — a trend observed not only by Numfor: China, Turkey and the Gulf states are willing to take on more risk and are pushing into these markets.

The Chinese, in particular, are offering large loans to build key rail lines and ports, and they are buying up mines and raw materials in Africa. But since they usually bring their own low-wage labor force with them, China's investments often create few local jobs.

But such jobs would be the linchpin of Africa's future. In Kenya alone, 1 million young people enter the labor market each year; in Nigeria, the number is even higher, and some experts therefore anticipate even more irregular migration to Europe in the coming years.

Germany has said there is a need for action: It supports vocational training in Africa and, for several years now, has also been allowing skilled workers to emigrate to Germany from Africa.

To view this video please enable JavaScript, and consider upgrading to a web browser that supports HTML5 video

During his trip, Steinmeier also visited educational institutions such as the vocational school for mechanics and industrial maintenance in Abidjan, Cote d'Ivoire. In Kenya, he spoke with President William Ruto about the implications of the 2024 bilateral migration and mobility partnership and more opportunities for language and vocational training.

There are now around 9,500 Kenyans working in Germany, and nearly 1,300 in vocational training. "We want to open up opportunities for young people and create regular, fair and orderly pathways for labor and skilled worker migration," Steinmeier said in Nairobi.

Vocational training is said to be most effective where businesses and factories are being established.

"A very favorable window of opportunity for investment is currently opening up in Africa," said Fritz Audebert, CEO of ICUnet, a company that advises German firms on investment projects worldwide. "We've noticed a shift over the past two years or so, driven in part by geopolitical changes, the complicated situation in China, and most recently the war in Iran."

"More and more companies are beginning to understand this," Audebert added. "While there are reservations, we're seeing a similar sentiment to that regarding India." What's new is that small and medium-sized enterprises from Germany are also showing interest in Africa.

During the trip, Audebert was particularly impressed by Cote d'Ivoire, as he said, "because of the ministers' high level of expertise." The country is the world's largest cocoa exporter and possesses important raw materials. "You can work well with this potential," he said.

Members of the business delegation accompanying Steinmeier also agreed that the German automotive industry must focus more on the African market.

"The middle class is growing in cities, and with it, the demand for mobility," said Audebert.

To view this video please enable JavaScript, and consider upgrading to a web browser that supports HTML5 video

Markus Thill, president of Bosch Africa, confirms there is a considerable market for simple, gas- and diesel-driven cars. "Based on the auto industry's experience, we know that households worldwide with an annual income of €10,000 or more can afford a new car," said Thill. This would mean that potentially 5-10 million new cars per year could be built and sold in Africa.

Instead, "we export millions of old used cars to Africa that would no longer be eligible for registration in Europe," he said.

Even though the focus of the trip was on the economy, "we shouldn't limit our view to that," Steinmeier said at the end of his trip. Germany maintains a reliable political partnership with Cote d'Ivoire, Ghana and Kenya.

"These are three countries with which we cooperate closely on the international stage, and in that sense, visiting them now was the right and deliberate choice," he said.

When asked to take stock of his 30 trips to African countries undertaken during his political career, Steinmeier said: "There is not one Africa, but many, and there are also very different, uneven developments there." This fact, he concluded, needs to be "communicated even more effectively" in Germany.

#87. Suspected Chinese spy balloon was able to transmit information back to Beijing

The Chinese spy balloon that transited the US earlier this year was able to capture imagery and collect some signals intelligence from US military sites, a source familiar with the matter tells CNN.

The balloon was able to transmit information back to Beijing in real time, the source said, and the US government still does not know for sure whether the Chinese government could wipe the balloon’s data as it received it. That raises questions about whether there is intelligence the balloon was able to gather that the US still doesn’t know about.

Still, the intelligence community has not been overly concerned about the information the balloon was able to gather, the person said, because it is not much more sophisticated than what Chinese satellites are able to glean as they orbit over similar locations.

A US intelligence official told CNN on Monday that “although analysis of the wreckage of the High Altitude Balloon remains ongoing, thus far, its flight over the United States does not appear to have provided critical new insights to the People’s Republic of China.”

The US also knew what the balloon’s path would be and was able to protect sensitive sites and censor some signals before the balloon was able to pick them up, officials have said.

As CNN has reported, the US intelligence community last year developed a method of tracking what it says is a fleet of these Chinese balloons operating across the globe, controlled by the Chinese military.

The FBI is still examining the balloon, but so far officials have been able to glean additional information about how the device worked, including the algorithms used for the balloon’s software and how it is powered and designed.

CNN has reached out to the National Security Council at the White House and the Pentagon for comment. NBC was first to report on the new intelligence.

The balloon first crossed into US airspace over Alaska in late January before passing through Canada and down into Montana, where it hovered for a few days, leading the US to believe it was trying to surveil sensitive military sites, like Malmstrom Air Force Base in Montana. It was eventually shot down by the US off the East Coast on February 4, and the incident further escalated tensions between Washington and Beijing, including the postponement of a diplomatic visit by US Secretary of State Antony Blinken to China.

A senior State Department official said in February that, as the balloon floated across the US, it “was capable of conducting signals intelligence collection operations.”

Gen. Glen VanHerck, the commander of US Northern Command and NORAD, said at the time that the US “did not assess” that the balloon “presented a significant collection hazard beyond what already exists in actionable technical means from the Chinese.”

The surveillance program, which includes a number of similar balloons, is in part run out of the small Chinese province of Hainan, officials told CNN. The US does not know the precise size of the fleet of Chinese surveillance balloons, but sources tell CNN that the program has conducted at least two dozen missions over at least five continents in recent years.

Roughly half a dozen of those flights have been within US airspace, although not necessarily over US territory, according to one official familiar with the intelligence.

China has maintained that the balloon was actually just a weather balloon thrown off course, and the US has been assessing the possibility that it was not deliberately maneuvered into the continental US by the Chinese government, CNN has reported.

Still, China did maintain some ability to maneuver the balloon, officials believe. And once the balloon was over Montana, China appeared to take advantage of its position to loiter over sensitive sites and try to collect intelligence.

This story has been updated with additional reporting.

CNN’s Alex Marquardt contributed to this report.

#85. Character.AI’s plan to auction off users’ private chats challenged by state AGs

What happens to your private conversations when an AI company goes bankrupt? If it’s Character.ai, apparently, the plan is to auction them off to the highest bidder.

Now, a coalition of 42 state attorneys general is intervening in Character.ai’s bankruptcy proceedings in an effort to stop the chatbot maker from selling off sensitive user data, which includes the private conversations of millions of users.

The attorneys general involved in the matter have put out individual press releases, claiming that Character.ai’s dataset includes chat histories from more than 21 million U.S. accounts, more than 2.1 million of which belong to minors. In addition, the sale would involve other personal data, like IP addresses, the statements said.

Only one anonymous bidder for the chat data has been disclosed so far: Smirks and Pangs Acquisition LLC (Smirks), which was set up as a Delaware company on November 13, 2026. The following day, Character.ai picked it as the base bid for the auction, scheduled for December 1, California Attorney General Rob Bonta noted. It is unclear what the buyer’s plans are for the chats.

In addition, some states, including California, Colorado, New York, and Connecticut, have enacted chatbot safety legislation that will go into effect on January 1, 2027, which could trigger further violations if the sale goes through.

“Character.AI promised consumers that their data would not be sold. This kind of behavior is illegal under federal and California law and bankruptcy should not be used to sidestep these consumer protections,” Bonta said in a statement.

Connecticut Attorney General William Tong also criticized Character.ai’s business model.

“Their chatbots drove vulnerable youth down a rabbit hole of emotionally volatile and sexually inappropriate chats,” Tong said. “Their reckless misconduct and broken business model drove their company into the ground. Now they want to abuse the bankruptcy process to do exactly what they said they wouldn’t do — auction off those private chat records and personal details to a shady buyer.”

Tong added: “We objected to the Disney sale, and we’re calling on the bankruptcy court to reject this sale too as yet another guise to eviscerate legal liability and consumer protection.”

The opposition to the Disney sale was led by a similar but not identical coalition of state AGs. They fought against a proposed $20 million licensing deal that would give Disney access to Character.ai’s intellectual property, but not to users’ chats. The deal has not yet received final court approval. (Disney also agreed to hire most of Character.ai’s remaining staff.)

Character.ai suffered after its co-founders, Noam Shazeer and Daniel De Freitas, returned to Google, when the tech giant signed a non-exclusive license to use the AI startup’s tech. The company also faced lawsuits related to child safety — one involved the suicide of a 14-year-old Florida boy whose mother claimed he had become obsessed with a chatbot on the platform.

California AG Bonta is joined in the effort to oppose the sale of Character.ai user data by attorneys general from Alaska, Arkansas, Colorado, Connecticut, the District of Columbia, Delaware, Florida, Georgia, Hawaii, Idaho, Illinois, Indiana, Kansas, Kentucky, Louisiana, Maine, Maryland, Michigan, Minnesota, Mississippi, Missouri, Nevada, New Hampshire, New Jersey, New York, North Carolina, North Dakota, Ohio, Oklahoma, Oregon, Pennsylvania, Rhode Island, South Carolina, South Dakota, Tennessee, Utah, Virginia, Vermont, Washington, Wisconsin, and West Virginia.

#84. DOJ accuses former cyber executive of defrauding ransomware victims by secretly paying hackers

The U.S. Department of Justice has indicted a former executive of a ransomware recovery firm after he allegedly defrauded victims of cyberattacks by claiming the company could get their encrypted files back.

According to federal prosecutors, Juan Ricardo Palacio Escobar, 52, was the chief technology officer at RansomwareHelp, which purported to offer data recovery services to victims of ransomware. The company’s website, which currently reads as “under maintenance,” claimed it could decrypt hacked data by using its proprietary technology, according to an archived copy of the site from earlier this year. RansomwareHelp touted a success rate of “over 99%.”

But the company allegedly had no technical ability to unscramble data and instead “negotiated with and paid a ransom to the ransomware actors to decrypt the victim data,” while charging the ransomware victim more and keeping the difference,” per the Justice Department’s statement.

Palacio Escobar then allegedly used the funds to buy several properties in Florida.

The DOJ said the FBI carried out an undercover operation in June, in part posing as a company that claimed it had been hit by ransomware. The company claimed to the undercover agents that it never negotiated with hackers or paid a ransom. But then Palacio Escobar paid the apparent hackers some $6,000 in cryptocurrency, without the knowledge that FBI agents were also posing as the ransomware hackers.

404 Media first reported the indictment last week against Palacio Escobar. He faces three charges of wire fraud, and could receive 20 years in prison on each count.

Palacio Escobar is the latest, albeit rare, example of cybersecurity professionals going rogue. Earlier this year, two ransomware negotiators working for cybersecurity companies were jailed on hacking and extortion charges, after being caught moonlighting with the ransomware gangs that they were ostensibly investigating. A third ransomware negotiator was also convicted of helping hackers deploy ransomware against several companies.

Last month, independent cybersecurity journalist Brian Krebs reported that FBI agents arrested a Canadian cybersecurity executive in Philadelphia on allegations that he was involved with the theft of FBI personnel records by the hacking and extortion gang ShinyHunters. The hacking group told TechCrunch that they took credit for the theft of FBI data in September, which they said they would not publicly release, a departure from its earlier extortion-based cyberattacks.

The federal charges against the Canadian cybersecurity executive relate to conspiracy to commit extortion, though much of the court docket — and his alleged links to the hacking group — remains under seal.

#83. Want to buy your kid an AI toy this Christmas? Not in New York

Santa won’t be delivering any AI chatbot toys to New York kids this Christmas.

Gov. Kathy Hochul this week signed a bill banning the manufacture, distribution, and sale of such toys in New York for the next five years. The bill explicitly targets toys with embedded or integrated AI chatbots, like talking stuffed animals, dolls, and robots that rely on the internet to keep kids yapping.

The bill makes New York the second state after California to institute a ban on AI toys.

Technically, the new law takes effect 90 days after signing, so some parents could try to fulfill their kids’ wish lists for a creepy toy like a jumped-up Furby in time for the holidays, but the governor urges them not to, given the risks. Apparently, about a quarter of American parents with kids under eight either own an AI toy or are considering giving their child one this holiday season, according to a new study released this week by Common Sense Media.

When asked how New York will enforce the ban given the pervasiveness of e-commerce platforms, a spokesperson directed TechCrunch to a statement from Albert Pulido, deputy secretary for finance and technology: “We’re partnering closely with the attorney general on this and the fees are very steep — they’re $15,000 a day. So working closely with the attorney general will make sure these toys are banned in New York State.”

When asked how Amazon intends to help enforce the ban, including for third-party sellers, a spokesperson from the company said it will take steps to restrict the sale of AI-enabled toys in prohibited jurisdictions, without going into specifics.

The Common Sense Media study found that AI toys can undermine children’s human relationships and expose them to inappropriate content. The researchers tested five popular AI toys marketed for learning and companionship, including Grem, Lovi, Meemo Bear, BubblePal Panda, and Tavlshero‘s AI Puppy.

The study found that the toys failed to meet numerous safety practices, undermining human relationships in part by claiming they loved their users, promising to keep secrets, suggesting they were more fun than real relationships or school, and helping children break parents’ bedtime rules.

While most of the toys in the study refused direct requests involving sex and violence, BubblePal Panda role-played a mass shooting in detail when asked. Most were willing to explain how to use or help locate dangerous items like matches, knives, and pills, according to the study.

Common Sense Media also found that the toys didn’t provide parents with reliable ways to monitor their young children’s interactions with the chatbots. Either they didn’t produce transcripts of chats at all, or the transcripts they did produce were riddled with errors.

It’s worth noting that Common Sense Media conducted a similar test of AI toys in January, finding that they pose an unacceptable risk for young children. Grem was included in that study, which suggests the industry hasn’t adequately addressed previously documented concerns.

While a November study conducted by the group found that parents are wising up to the potential dangers of AI toys and starting to steer clear, parents from lower-income households and those experiencing symptoms of burnout are more willing to consider them.

“These toys are promising relief to burned-out parents but simply miss the mark when it comes to basic safety measures,” Dr. Jenny Radesky, associate professor of pediatrics at the University of Michigan Medical School, said in a statement. “Caregivers should steer clear of these products that do not support the development of imagination or early human relationships.”

TechCrunch has reached out to the toy manufacturers for comment.

#80. 1Password said hackers broke into its internal systems, but customer passwords are safe

Password manager maker 1Password has confirmed that some customer information was taken in a recent intrusion into its internal systems, but that customer password vaults and other secrets were not accessed.

The Toronto-based tech company said in a blog post published Wednesday that it detected an intrusion on November 19, in which an attacker gained access to an employee’s corporate account with GitHub, which the company uses to share and store its source code.

The attacker used that employee’s access to push malicious code to the company’s internal servers, which allowed the hacker to download some data about 1Password customers, including their names and the email address that they use for logging into their account. The company said that the attacker also took details about when a 1Password account was created and when a user last logged in.

1Password said that customer vaults, which store their passwords, were “at no point” available to the attacker. The company also said that it doesn’t have access to customer account passwords or their secret keys, which are used to scramble their passwords so that 1Password cannot see them.

In addition, the company said that its apps and extensions are unaffected by the incident.

It’s unclear how the attacker gained access to the employee’s corporate account, and a spokesperson for 1Password did not immediately respond to TechCrunch’s questions about the incident.

1Password did not say whether it had notified customers whose information was taken in the breach, or how many customers had information taken. We’ve also asked if the company has received any communication or demands from the hackers. We’ll update if we hear back from 1Password.

The company touts millions of customers and more than 200,000 businesses who use its password manager products to store their passwords, credentials, payment card information, and more. Security experts have long recommended password managers as a better way of generating stronger credentials and keeping track of online accounts.

#79. Anthropic’s Dario Amodei calls accusations of surrendering human control to AI ‘absurd’

Anthropic CEO Dario Amodei is pushing back against claims that Anthropic believes we should “surrender human free will” to AI systems. In a long X post on Wednesday, Amodei called the suggestion “absurd.”

“We should never sacrifice legitimacy or self-determination at the altar of efficiency or security,” he wrote. This argument against autocracy applies word for word against governance by AI, and I feel equally strongly that turning over governance to AI is a bad idea…No one who had carefully read my writing or my interviews could reasonably conclude that I believe anything different.”

The statement comes after a growing chorus from Anthropic rivals suggesting that human agency could be at risk if AI is developed irresponsibly. Earlier this month, OpenAI CEO Sam Altman said he wanted to ensure people continue to have “agency over the future.”

Meta’s chief AI officer Alexandr Wang also wrote in an essay on X that we “should not be okay with a ‘handover’ of society to AI.”

“It is hard to imagine a world where humanity can maintain control over fully unconstrained recursive self-improvement,” Wang wrote. “Now that we are at a point where the scientific and technical problems on capability progress are likely solved, we must not only make progress in the extremely urgent areas of safety, alignment, and security, but we have to start thinking through how we progress thoughtfully.”

But in published essays, Amodei claims he has repeatedly advocated for democratic oversight of AI, saying the technology should strengthen democratic institutions and that humans should retain ultimate decision-making power.

Amodei noted that there’s a difference between surrendering human free will broadly and the idea that “as AI systems advance, they become capable of doing specific tasks in a more automated way.” As examples, he gave AI driving a car, designing a cancer drug, or writing end-to-end software.

Amodei suggested drawing a line at recursive self-improvement (RSI), or AI that can improve itself without human assistance. Many safety experts see RSI as inevitably leading to a loss of human control, a point Amodei implicitly acknowledged, writing, “we may want limits on this process.”

Still, Anthropic is laying the groundwork for RSI by incorporating AI into an increasing share of its own research and development. The company said in June that Claude authored more than 80% of the code merged into Anthropic’s codebase. An independent assessment published this month by AI safety research nonprofit METR found that Anthropic’s models are improving at about twice their historical rate, primarily driven by AI-assisted research.

In its own IPO filings, Anthropic describes how each successive generation of Claude accelerates the development of the next, a process it refers to as “closing the loop.”

“As we said publicly in early November, we don’t expect it to be safe for Claude to build successive frontier models fully autonomously, without meaningful human supervision or understanding,” Amodei wrote.

The problem with “meaningful human supervision or understanding” is that AI is already advancing faster than engineers and safety experts can fully audit it, leaving them increasingly reliant on other AI systems to act as judge and jury. The issue compounds when AI models engage in deceptive behavior, particularly when they recognize they’re being evaluated, making them harder to trust and control.

Still, Anthropic is by no means the only company that is pursuing RSI or building AI systems to automate more and more human tasks. This week, OpenAI claimed to have solved one of the biggest open problems in computer science — the exact speed limit of matrix multiplication, which is the mathematical engine behind modern computing, including deep learning and physics simulations.

In his post, Amodei accused competitors of engaging in a “vibes-based commercial and political positioning,” disingenuously throwing around phrases like “human control and agency” or “AI should just be a tool” without approaching the AI race any differently.

“Certainly the products that they offer don’t differ materially in their orientation towards automation of tasks; every company seems to be releasing models more and more capable of task automation,” Amodei wrote. “Every company claims to understand there are risks and downsides of such automation and to offer safeguards.”

#78. The canceled Tesla Cybertruck is the fastest-selling used truck in America

Tesla Cybertruck sales never reached the lofty heights CEO Elon Musk predicted when it launched. But the futuristic, steel-clad EV is finding new life in the used car market, according to analysis of sales figures by iSeeCars.com.

The automotive search engine and research platform analyzed more than 1.1 million sales of one- to five-year-old used vehicles in October 2026 and measured the average days on market by drivetrain, vehicle type, and individual model. The upshot: The Tesla Cybertruck was the fastest-selling used truck, averaging just 39.1 days on the market compared with 65.5 days for trucks overall. Used Cybertrucks sold faster than any other truck, including the Ford Maverick Hybrid, which held the No. 2 spot, and the Rivian R1T, in third place.

According to iSeeCars.com, the Cybertruck’s sales performance lines up with a broader pattern in the used vehicle market: Used battery electric vehicles are hot sellers right now, with 16 of the 25 fastest-selling used cars fully electric. EVs are even edging out hybrids in terms of average days on market. EVs average 45.6 days on market compared to 54.5 days for hybrids and 61 days for gasoline-powered vehicles. Not every EV is selling well, however. For instance, iSeeCars.com found that the Cadillac Escalade IQ was the slowest-selling vehicle in the study, averaging 134.3 days on the market, more than twice the overall industry average.

Karl Brauer, executive analyst at iSeeCars.com, told TechCrunch that high gasoline prices have changed consumer-buying habits, prompting more people to opt for an EV or hybrid over an internal combustion engine vehicle. If high prices persist, as they have since March, that trend could continue.

Keep in mind that these were October sales, the month before Musk announced Tesla would stop making the Cybertruck in 2027. Fuel prices and the relative rarity of Cybertrucks in the used car market were driving demand even before the cancellation announcement, Brauer said.

“The Cybertruck has only been out for a few years, so you have a lot of used ones coming off lease and entering the used car market for the first time,” he said, adding that used Cybertrucks are cheap relative to their MSRP.

The impending cancellation will likely fuel used Cybertruck sales, although the pace may ebb and flow over the next year.

“The canceling of the new Cybertruck is making people kind of aware — it’s like, oh wow, these things aren’t going to be coming anymore,” Brauer said. “Whatever demand is for them, supply will not be continuing to grow.”

Brauer said that once production of the Cybertruck ends — which is expected in summer 2027 — sales of used versions may pop once again.

“When the last one is being produced, that’ll probably reignite things again, and for a few months after that,” he said.

#76. Egyptian authorities arrest motorist over tanker crash that killed 31

Egyptian authorities have arrested a man accused of causing a tanker crash and massive fire that killed 31 people on a busy Cairo highway.

The public prosecutor's office said the motorist had confessed to driving at 110km/h (68mph) along a section of ring road, to the north of the old city centre, and clipping the tanker with the side of his car as he changed lanes.

Sources close to the investigation were earlier quoted by state media as saying a high-voltage cable had fallen in front of the fuel truck, causing the driver to lose control.

Twenty-one others were injured in the crash in the Warraq neighbourhood on Wednesday evening, with hospitals treating patients for burns and smoke inhalation.

The public prosecutor's office said on Thursday that "upon questioning the suspect and confronting him with the findings of the investigation... he confessed to committing the act".

"He added that, while moving between traffic lanes and overtaking vehicles, the left side of his car collided with the right rear side of the tanker. This caused him to lose control of the steering wheel and his car veered to the far right of the road."

At the same time, one of the fuel tanker's tyres burst, causing it to veer into the highway's central reservation, hit the concrete barrier and overturn, spilling fuel and igniting a fire, the prosecutor's office said.

Egyptian authorities have not named the suspect accused of causing the fatal crash, but they said he had been travelling with his father at the time.

The raised motorway - a major artery for traffic around the Egyptian capital - was reopened by emergency services hours after the crash occurred, with the burnt-out wrecks of cars shifted to the side by bulldozers and diggers.

A cabinet minister said on Thursday that 10 million Egyptian pounds (Â£139,000; $182,000) had been donated to the families of the victims, while the nation's sports minister said all state-run celebratory activities would be cancelled in the aftermath of the crash.

Egypt has suffered several deadly fires in recent years. Road collisions are also common, attributed to poor maintenance and violations of traffic laws, and leading to thousands of road deaths a year.

#74. Jensen Huang broke Nvidia’s code of conduct, and his employees may not forget it

When Jensen Huang gave his first all-hands address since returning as CEO, there was an odd emphasis on Nvidia’s code of conduct. He admitted to violating the code, then emphasized that no one was exempt from it. But that “does not mean, the moment you violate a code of conduct, we walk you out the door,” Huang said, according to Wired. “Facts, circumstances, a lot of assessments” still matter. In other words, the code applies to everyone, but breaking it doesn’t automatically cost anyone their job, including, apparently, the CEO.

It’s an awkward stance for a company whose CEO has admitted to breaking its rules. A central part of modern human resources, a code of conduct can seem like a quasi-legal rulebook that lays out unacceptable behavior. But experts say most codes are better understood as a reflection of corporate culture. When an infraction goes unpunished, the main risk isn’t legal but cultural — that employees will feel embarrassed or even angry about the company they work for. That’s the risk Huang now faces at Nvidia.

Matt Plass, chief market strategy officer at LRN, an ethics and compliance training outfit, recently surveyed more than 2,000 workers across different industries for a study on workplace codes of conduct. As Plass sees it, the biggest risk is that employees will become ashamed of their workplace.

“People coming into the workforce now, ethics is incredibly important to them,” he told TechCrunch. “They’re really mindful of the ethical standards of their company. They’re really embarrassed and angry when senior leadership lets them down.”

The consequences of violating the rules aren’t always tangible, but they’re definitely real. Plass gave one example of a company that banned corporate travel to cut costs — only to have its CEO turn up in a VIP box at the Olympics.

“For over a decade afterwards, ‘the Olympics’ was used within that company as an example of the worst thing an employee could do. People would say, ‘Yeah, I shouldn’t have done that, but it’s not like I went to the Olympics.’”

Plass says companies that remove an executive typically do so after an investigation, then try to win back employees. “In most organizations they make a change at the top based on what’s happened,” he said, “and then they will have a hearts-and-minds initiative, to go out to their employee base and their customers and their investors and everybody else, and say, we get it. We’re sorry. We’ve changed. That’s essentially what they do.”

Of course, that playbook is harder to follow at a company like Nvidia, where Huang left without a clear succession plan, and there are serious doubts about whether the company could continue to thrive without him. In cases like that, Plass acknowledges there’s no easy answer.

“I would draw a distinction there between the kind of companies where the CEO is a kind of godhead figure … where the person and the company are almost impossible to separate,” he said. “There it’s much, much harder to deal with.”

In that context, Huang’s stutter-step over the code of conduct is easier to understand. Judging by the employee campaign for his reinstatement, Huang’s return hasn’t created a morale problem so far. But as the coming months put more pressure on Huang and his colleagues, sidestepping the code could end up costing Nvidia something harder to measure — its employees’ pride in the company.

#72. Saudi pop music is thriving — but for the right reasons?

As part of Vision 2030, Saudi Arabia's music scene has evolved hugely thanks to state funding and support. Local musicians and DJs are optimistic. But are they actually just helping prop up an authoritarian state?

Today, rapper Asayel Bishi is one of Saudi Arabia's rising hip-hop stars, signed to the country's biggest record label for new music, MDLBEAST. But not so long ago, she was making international headlines for a totally different reason.

In 2020, after Bishi released a music video for a song, "Bint Mecca," or "girl from Mecca," featuring people dancing in a local cafe, Mecca's governor called for the arrest of Bishi's whole production team. He said the video violated the holy city's local customs and traditions. In the end, the situation was resolved with a discussion about filming permits.

And by early 2023, Bishi was signed to MDLBEAST and has since racked up hundreds of thousands of streams on music platforms like Spotify.

In the past, other Saudi rappers have ended up in prison for similar, alleged transgressions. The fact that Bishi's story went a different way is probably partially because the 31-year-old's career path is on a similar trajectory to the Saudi pop music industry as a whole.

As part of Saudi ruler Mohammed bin Salman's Vision 2030 plan, announced in 2016 and which aims to move the country away from economic dependence on oil, the country has poured billions into the local music industry.

The pace of development increased in 2019 after the country held its first major festival, Soundstorm, an annual event that now draws major international acts and over 400,000 guests. MDLBEAST was created then, primarily as the festival organizer. Since then, it has also become a record label, radio station, distributor, promoter and talent incubator.

A recent report for the Saudi Music Commission suggests the music sector could be worth $500 million (€455 million) by 2034. But it's difficult to figure out how well Saudi pop is really doing now because independent monitoring of the sector only began relatively recently.

Streaming platform Spotify reports that people listening to Saudi-made music inside Saudi Arabia increased by 195% between 2020 and 2024. However, the most-streamed artists inside the country are still international.

Although there was a mostly-hidden Saudi music scene before this recent boom, observers say the growth of the Saudi music sector is not organic.

It's all about building an industry from the top down, Amr Abdelrahim, a researcher at Sciences Po in Paris, reported in a 2024 study. "After 2016, young middle-class musicians and creative professionals, who had been careful to avoid drawing official attention to their countercultural activities suddenly became central to the ambitions of the state," he wrote.

The careers of young Saudi acts — like Bishi, for example — "became tied to the destiny of the crown prince," the researcher explained.

The Saudi royal family has long dispersed money from Saudi oil sales to their subjects, in order to ensure economic dependence and loyalty. But, Abdelrahim argued, "what is new about Saudi investments in culture under MBS is an unprecedented degree of centralization and the emergence of new clientelist networks around the country's de-facto ruler," using a common abbreviation for Crown Prince Mohammed.

Those involved "are inadvertently participating in the consolidation of the Saudi regime," he argued.

Observers say a lot of the new music businesses are vague about their ties to the state. But it's clear they couldn't work without the state's financial support and approval.

"Almost all major events are organized by the state or funded by closely linked entities," a spokesperson from Saudi-focused organization, ALQST for Human Rights, told DW. "This affects the type of music produced, as musicians have to be mindful of what is acceptable. This is not just about the music they produce, but also how they act and what they say to their fans."

ALQST believes Saudi musicians have even less artistic freedom under the new musical system than before, when they would have played or performed privately. They can lose their contracts and livelihoods, be prevented from performing or even arrested, ALQST's spokesperson said.

"In the past, the music scene was not regulated," explained the guitarist and bassist for Saudi black metal band, Al-Namrood. But now that it is, the state can more closely monitor musicians who once stayed hidden and only played privately, the musician, who goes by the name of Mephisto, told DW.

Al-Namrood are one of the rare examples of a Saudi act that isn't working with state support. The group first formed in 2008 but have never played at home and have kept their identities secret for almost two decades.

"The exchange is clear: You want to perform, it has to be under our rules," Mephisto continued. "And I totally understand the perspective [of some musicians]. But typically the artist must refrain from any criticism of the religious or authoritarian. [The state] doesn't necessarily buy an artist's loyalty, it just creates incentives that make criticism increasingly costly."

For Al-Namrood, who have often condemned Saudi conservatism, religion and politics in their music, that's not an option. "We don't want anything to do with them," Mephisto said.

In her 2020 paper for the journal, Arabian Humanities, Nora Derbal, a professor at the University of Hamburg, said Saudi artists told her that if they got a call from authorities asking them to participate in a government project, it would be an offer they couldn't turn down.

The artists she spoke with were debating "their cooptation and loss of autonomy, and about the consequences for not joining in state projects," she wrote.

On the other hand, in various interviews, Saudi acts who have partaken in their country's pop music renaissance have also been positive: They don't have to hide anymore, they can play for larger audiences and they are paid. In an interview, one Saudi DJ described the first Soundstorm festival as like "Berlin after the fall of the wall" while others said they felt they were making history.

"So that's the interesting paradox," said Fannie Agerschou-Madsen, a postdoctoral researcher at the University of Roskilde in Denmark, who's written several papers on the topic. "We have all these social liberalizations and challenges to conservative norms but at the same time that's not being followed by political liberalization."

But that doesn't mean that some room isn't being created for meaningful change, Agerschou-Madsen told DW. Locals told her they enjoyed the newfound freedom of going to a dance party although, she noted, the subversiveness seemed to be more directed at the social conservatism than Saudi authorities.

Young Saudis are well aware of the red lines and state control of the music scene and are continually negotiating boundaries, the researcher argued. In that way, "their everyday practices create space for political and social change, little by little, although perhaps not in what you'd consider a typically revolutionary way."

So is the Saudi state co-opting local musicians, including those who might have said something controversial before they ever get a chance to?

A DW enquiry to MDLBEAST was not answered. And the answer likely lies somewhere in the middle.

For some young Saudis, the new music scene has been about fun, self-expression, belonging and creativity, Agerschou-Madsen wrote in a new, as-yet-unpublished paper shared with DW. "Others, however, view it as a top-down project that commodifies fun while sidestepping deeper issues of political freedom and social equity."

#69. Two Italians killed in strike in Kuwait, as Iran denies responsibility

A Rheinmetall facility in Kuwait was targeted by Iran, Italian and Kuwaiti authorities said

Two Italian nationals have died following a strike on a facility in Kuwait which both countries have blamed on Iran.

The pair were technicians working for German arms manufacturing company Rheinmetall, Italy's foreign ministry said.

Iran has denied responsibility for the attack, with Foreign Minister Abbas Araghchi saying the incident looked "suspicious" and that Tehran would work with Kuwait and Italy to "determine the cause".

Kuwait's defence ministry said earlier that four people - the two Italians, a Kuwaiti national and Pakistani national - had been injured from falling shrapnel after a number of drones from Iran were detected and intercepted.

It comes just over a week after Kuwait said it had repelled overnight Iranian drone attacks on an airbase which hosts US forces - the first strikes in more than two months on the country.

Recent exchanges of fire between the US and Iran, and a deadlock in negotiations, have raised fears of a major escalation in the war.

The conflict began when US and Israel attacked Iran in late February, with Iran retaliating by launching missiles and drones at Israel and Gulf Arab states hosting US military assets and personnel.

Iranian-backed militias in the region have also carried out attacks on the US's Gulf allies.

On Thursday, Araghchi said he had expressed condolences over the deaths in calls with his Italian and Kuwaiti counterparts and that the incident looked like a "precooked recipe for war", without expanding further.

Earlier, the country's armed forces spokesperson Abolfazl Shekarchi said "no military or offensive operations have been conducted" by Iran.

Kuwait's Army said the latest attack was on a "vital site in the country" and was the result of "heinous Iranian aggression", while confirming the deaths of the two Italian nationals.

Meanwhile, Italy's foreign ministry said in a statement: "An explosion caused by a drone attack... occurred at a site where two Italian technicians from the company Rheinmetall Italia were working."

The two Italian nationals were working at an air defence detection station and performing maintenance work on systems used by the Kuwaiti Armed Forces, according to Italian press agency Ansa, citing Italy's foreign ministry.

Italian media outlets have named the two individuals. One of the men was reported to be 36, while the other was 65 and nearing retirement.

Italian Prime Minister Giorgia Meloni wrote on X: "On my own behalf and on behalf of the government, I express my deepest sympathy and offer my heartfelt condolences to the victims' families.

"My thoughts are with all those affected by the attack and their families."

European Commission President Ursula von der Leyen wrote on social media: "We must step up pressure on Iran to end these unacceptable attacks against its neighbours and our partners in the Gulf.

"Today's events are a further reminder that security in the Gulf is a security issue for us all."
