#68. FCC approves SpaceX plan to put AI data centers in space

SpaceX was given conditional approval by the Federal Communications Commission on Wednesday to deploy and operate a constellation of up to 1 million satellites for its orbital data center business, a regulatory victory for Elon Musk and his bid to move AI data centers into space.

The FCC authorization was granted despite objections from Amazon, Viasat, and the American Astronomical Society, which flagged concerns over orbital debris and congestion, radiofrequency interference, and environmental consequences. Amazon, which is building a competitor to SpaceX’s Starlink service, argued in its petition that the application lacked basic details and described it as a “lofty ambition rather than a real plan.” Despite that lack of detail, Amazon also said the proposal “risks real harm.”

The FCC dismissed some of the key concerns raised by opponents, including potential threats to scientific research, because the agency determined they fell outside its regulatory authority.

FCC Chair Brendan Carr said in a statement that the action “opens a bold new frontier for the computing infrastructure needed to power America’s leadership and growth.”

Musk thanked Carr in a post on X, writing “Much appreciate. This will be a great asset to America and the world.”

This initial regulatory approval doesn’t mean orbital data centers are imminent.

SpaceX faces a number of technical and economic challenges, including how to dramatically reduce the cost of manufacturing and launching satellites, cool processors in space, and even how to secure financing for chips that are destined for orbit.

The company must also clear other regulatory hurdles. The FCC has not yet approved SpaceX’s plans to handle orbital debris for satellites that would operate above 600 kilometers, for instance. SpaceX requested a waiver of existing FCC rules, which requires satellites ending missions below 2,000 kilometers reenter Earth’s atmosphere within five years of completing those missions. The company is interested in moving some of these retired satellites into other orbits around Earth or even around the sun, according to the FCC authorization document.

SpaceX has said it plans to deploy the satellites, known as Starmind, at altitudes ranging from 500 to 1,002 kilometers. The FCC’s approval gives SpaceX the authority to use radiofrequency communications to maintain contact with and control its orbital data centers from Earth if their primary communications systems fail. The Starmind satellites would primarily communicate using lasers to connect with other satellites, including SpaceX’s Starlink.

The FCC has given SpaceX time to sort out these issues. Under the approval, SpaceX has seven years to get its first operational satellite into orbit and 14 years to deploy the entire constellation.

#67. S&P considers Nvidia credit downgrade over governance and ‘key man’ risks

S&P Global Ratings, one of the three major credit rating agencies, is considering downgrading Nvidia’s rating, citing concerns about the company’s management and governance practices.

Despite Nvidia’s strong financial performance, the ouster and reinstatement of CEO Jensen Huang has raised concerns about “weak executive oversight and lack of succession planning” and “key man risk,” meaning heavy reliance on Huang for its future success, S&P said in a statement.

The agency expects to finish its review within 90 days and could cut Nvidia’s rating from AA to AA-, the fourth-highest on its scale.

Although Nvidia is certain to keep its investment-grade rating, a downgrade could raise its borrowing costs. Because Nvidia backstops neoclouds and other infrastructure companies that lack strong credit on their own, a lower rating could make their debt costlier or force them to post more collateral.

In August, Nvidia announced plans to arrange $500 billion in financing for AI infrastructure with six financial institutions, including Goldman Sachs, Apollo, and Blackstone. Under the original terms, Nvidia would guarantee 25% of the loans. But even before Huang’s ouster and return, lenders had been demanding that Nvidia guarantee more, Bloomberg reported.

A downgrade would likely prompt lenders to demand even larger guarantees.

#66. Trial of Berlin woman accused of spying for Russia begins after delay

Despite delays due to health issues, the trial of the Berlin woman accused of spying for Russia has begun. Prosecutors say the defendant used her military contacts to provide information on German defense and Ukraine.

The trial of a German-Ukrainian woman on charges of spying for Russia started Thursday.

The start of the proceedings was delayed in the morning after the accused developed health problems that required treatment by emergency medical workers, a spokeswoman for a Berlin court said.

Later, however, she was brought into the courtroom in a wheelchair, wearing dark sunglasses. Her lawyers caused further delay when they said she would not confirm her personal information.

Several times throughout the afternoon, the defense attorneys said their client was feeling nauseous, and a bucket was brought in. A doctor was present in the courtroom.

The arrest of the 57-year-old Berlin businesswoman triggered a diplomatic clash between Berlin and Moscow.

The Russian ambassador was summoned regarding the incident, and Andrei Mayerov, the deputy military attache at the embassy, was expelled from Germany.

Ilona W., who holds German and Ukrainian citizenship, is accused of working for the intelligence service of a foreign power.

The trial is expected to last until May 2027.

She is accused of having contacts with current and former German Defense Ministry officials to provide information about defense industry locations, drone tests and planned drone deliveries to Ukraine.

She was also said to have been in contact with a man, her alleged handler, working for Russian intelligence at the Russian Embassy in Berlin since at least November 2023, prosecutors say.

On several occasions, she allegedly helped her contact at the embassy attend political events in Berlin under a false identity in order to establish contacts there. She is also suspected of having compiled background information on participants in high-level political events.

Berlin summoned the alleged handler, who was identified by Der Spiegel magazine as the embassy's deputy military attache, over the incident and expelled him from Germany.

Ilona W. led an advocacy group for years that called for international cooperation, focusing particularly on German, Russian, Ukrainian relations.

In addition to her advocacy work, she also ran a small marketing agency.

Both positions allowed her to be active in Berlin's political circles and provided her with connections within the military.

The Russian ambassador to Berlin, Sergei Nechayev, denied the allegations this week.

"The Russian Embassy has no connection whatsoever to this woman, and even if she happened to cross paths with one of our representatives, perhaps at some reception, that means absolutely nothing," Nechayev told news agency AFP.

#64. Grand Theft Auto VI proves why efficiency isn’t a virtue

It should have been impossible for “Grand Theft Auto VI” (GTA VI) to live up to the hype. Fans waited 13 years for the newest installment in the crime-adventure franchise, and Rockstar Games exceeded even those inconceivable expectations when the game finally came out last week.

GTA VI launched as the highest-rated game of all time on Metacritic and made $3.79 billion on release day to mark what could be the biggest entertainment event ever – more massive than “Avengers: Endgame” ($2.9 billion) or Taylor Swift’s Eras tour ($2 billion).

The 13-year wait certainly fueled the frenzy, and sales, around GTA VI, but some fans craved GTA VI because of what it represents in the emerging era of AI.

Even the biggest workaholics among us – startup founders – scheduled time off to play the game, including Spiry co-founder Dima Beseda, who told TechCrunch, “I reshuffled my workload to free up time around the weekends and even booked vitamin IV drips to keep my energy up.”

Yet some players felt a sense of anticipatory nostalgia as they wiped away a layer of dust on their Xbox to play the game they’d been looking forward to since they were kids.

“We do have a view that GTA is likely to be the last game of its kind to be made this way,” Ben Feder, managing partner at TIRTA Ventures and former CEO of Rockstar parent company Take-Two Interactive, told TechCrunch.

That’s because GTA VI was created entirely without generative AI.

“We’re kind of at this technological moment where this way of developing has sort of reached its natural frontier,” Feder said. “I don’t think you can say that games like this will always be around.”

As millions of people immersed themselves in Vice City, the tech industry witnessed another long-awaited moment: Anthropic became the first major AI lab to go public. At Nasdaq HQ, Anthropic executives gathered to celebrate the Claude maker’s $81.3 billion IPO. At virtually the same moment that Anthropic broke the record for the largest IPO ever, GTA VI was also breaking records with the highest-rated and best-selling game launch of all time.

But when the Anthropic team rang the bell on Thursday, the sound also signaled the end of an era in video game development, and perhaps even in the arts at large.

The AI industry operates with efficiency as its north star. The promise of this technology is that we will be able to automate so much work that we can expand the capabilities of humanity, avoiding needless time spent tinkering with ultimately fruitless ideas. Instead, a prompt goes into an LLM, and a response is spat out.

This technological revolution exploded into the world with some soul-crushing side effects: children are inundated with mind-numbing slop on YouTube, lawyers cite nonexistent cases in court, and tech companies like Meta lay off tens of thousands of people as part of a corporate-branded “Year of Efficiency.” But against the backdrop of the AI industry’s first major IPO, Rockstar has unveiled a game that about 5,000 people labored over for more than a decade.

The final product is breathtaking, looking so lifelike that it can be hard to distinguish gameplay footage from real life. These sprawling open-world games are usually made possible by procedurally generating new landscapes and objects, but Rockstar has taken the time to painstakingly craft every detail of the world, even developing 30,000 different nonplayable characters with unique appearances and personalities.

The video game industry is already grappling with the threat of AI. Google’s Project Genie, which launched earlier this year, allows users to create and wander around 3D worlds generated from prompts to an LLM. The stock prices of several video game companies faltered around the time of Project Genie’s release, perhaps because of investors’ uncertainty about the future relevance of video game developers.

“The notion that somehow new tools would allow an individual to push a button and generate a hit, market a hit, and bring it to many millions of consumers around the world, it’s a laughable notion,” Take-Two Interactive CEO Strauss Zelnick said at the time. “It’s just never been the case with entertainment.”

Zelnick’s fellow tech CEOs see different future, though. On a Meta earnings call this year, CEO Mark Zuckerberg promised that, “soon, you’ll have an AI that understands you and [can] even generate great personalized content for you.”

Elon Musk even went as far as proposing that a game like GTA VI could be made with AI.

“You won’t even have to ask,” Musk wrote on X. “AI will figure what video game you’d like best.”

This is the paradox of “Grandthropic” week. GTA VI is so impressively vast and captivating because its developers could make mistakes, experiment, and rework ideas, even if that meant delaying the game (twice). GTA VI was incubated with the exact inefficiencies that investors hope AI companies like Anthropic can help eradicate.

“Plenty of fantastic art — video games and elsewhere — almost by definition has to be created slowly, and there’s something to having that kind of inefficiency around it,” Feder said. “Creative output requires deletion, right? Getting to kind of a masterpiece oftentimes requires an enormous amount of deletion. And when you’re talking about a game of the kind of complexity we’re talking about, that kind of deletion ends up being very expensive, and it is by definition not efficient.”

GTA VI reeks of its own humanity – every small detail is loaded with intention, but it’s this impressive precision that keeps the world abuzz, triggering the kind of monocultural moment that has become increasingly rare in a media landscape that revolves around highly personalized social media algorithms.

The game forces the player to slow down, which is either frustrating or genius, depending on your sense of humor. People waited more than three presidential terms for the next installment in a high-octane action franchise, only to download a 212 GB behemoth of a game in which your earliest missions involve driving around doing errands and putting up drywall to make your girlfriend happy. Don’t worry — there are still car chases and shootouts — but in a moment when AI feels suffocating, GTA VI is such an artistic achievement that it is reaching audiences that don’t even like violent video games.

The ultimate fantasy of GTA VI is no longer to role-play as a criminal — the fantasy is just taking your time to relish the mundane and explore an expansive world that developers were given the time and space to perfect.

#63. A month with the iPhone Duo has changed how I use my phone

For years, the definition of a premium smartphone depended on its cameras, processors, and how light it felt when you stowed it in your pocket. The iPhone Duo challenges that concept by asking consumers to pay more for a fundamentally different smartphone experience, even if it means sacrificing what they’ve long thought of as high-end features.

In a little under a month of using the Duo, I’ve found that the trade-offs I was initially concerned about matter far less to me than I had expected. What’s been more interesting is how the Duo’s foldable form factor has changed my everyday habits, including how I interact with apps, which apps I use, and how I organize and personalize the phone itself.

I also easily forgot a number of my initial concerns about switching to the Duo. The apps, for the most part, are now optimized for the Duo; I’ve reacquainted myself with Touch ID; and I don’t miss the Pro Max camera on most days.

That’s not to say the Duo doesn’t have its shortcomings. I still miss that telephoto lens sometimes, and I’m still longing for Face ID — though not as much since Apple shipped a face unlock feature in the latest update. But after a month of using the Duo, I’ve found these compromises have had less of an impact on my day-to-day experience compared with the benefits that the new form factor brings.

I was surprised by how much I’ve grown to appreciate the Duo’s multitasking capabilities. Initially, I didn’t think I’d regularly use it to view two apps side by side, given the still fairly limited screen space even while the phone is unfolded, especially compared with an iPad. But I’ve found myself snapping apps to both halves of the screen more often than I had imagined.

For starters, Apple has made this side-by-side mode incredibly intuitive and easy to use — you simply drag the app over to the left or right side of the screen and drop it into the target box that appears.

I have regularly taken advantage of this feature to do all sorts of things.

I really enjoy keeping X open as I read through the news, which allows me to switch back and forth between the article I’m reading and real-time reactions to it. Sometimes I’ll keep email or Slack open on the side while I’m working on something else so I don’t miss an important message or notification.

And much like the overstimulated members of Gen Z (which I am not), I sometimes like scrolling social media while engaging with some other media off to the side.

Case in point: I passively watched Netflix’s “Love is Blind” reunion while reading the gossipy tweets and Reddit posts about the main characters on the other side of the screen. This sort of TV watching behavior used to be called second-screen viewing, but now it’s morphing into something new. Everything is happening on one screen, so should we call it “Duo screen viewing,” perhaps? It makes me long for even more interactive TV experiences where commentary and content are combined.

I’ve noticed another shift in my behavior since adopting the Duo as my main device. When I’m out and about, the Duo feels more like an (arguably heavier) iPhone mini. For those who still miss smaller iPhones, the Duo is the next best thing. Despite its weight, the phone feels small. You can cover it with one hand; you can type with a thumb; you can reach the top and bottom easily; and you can even fit it into your front and back pockets. The Pro Max now feels unbelievably strange in my hand — how was I ever comfortable wrangling this giant rectangle?

The Duo’s small screen works well when you’re on the go, especially for short interactions like responding to a quick text or pulling up directions to a destination. It’s not as great for mindless scrolling, which I appreciate, as it forces me to engage with the real world instead of killing every moment of boredom or unease by opening up apps.

But when you’re home or somewhere with time to kill, you can unfold the screen and enjoy your apps super-sized. It’s the best of both worlds.

I find that I instinctively unfold the phone when I’m preparing to watch videos, because the larger screen is best for that. It’s also unfolded if I’m shopping, as it’s more convenient for looking at products, zooming in to examine the details, or complete checkout forms.

Generally, I also unfold the Duo when I’m actively using apps. This setup has also allowed me to rethink my Home Screen layout: With the Duo closed, I prefer to see just widgets on the small screen with key information like weather, time, battery life, and so on. Meanwhile, the only app icons on the Home Screen are those in the dock.

In the weeks since its launch, most of the apps that weren’t yet optimized for the Duo have been upgraded to support its foldable form factor. In mid-November, Apple said there were over 200,000 Duo-ready apps, and in my experience, that certainly feels true. A majority of my so-called “daily drivers” — with the glaring exception of key Google apps like Gmail and Drive — have dropped the black band on the side of the screen that was frustrating early testers of the phone.

Most apps now take advantage of the available space, whether that’s on the Duo’s small front screen or the wider, unfolded one.

I’ve also found it interesting to see the different interpretations developers have had for how to best optimize their app for Duo.

Some have used the form factor as a way to bring in some sort of novelty: there’s a note-taking app that opens and closes like a notebook, with pages you can write on with the Apple Pencil; a retro Walkman-like music player; a tiny typewriter; a gaming handheld; a game where you close the phone to make grilled cheese.

As much as I love the whimsy of this group of apps, I appreciate how my everyday apps feel better to use on the unfolded screen. Spotify, at last, feels less cluttered when it has more screen real estate. A number of note-taking, list-making, and calendar apps also feel somehow more usable when spread out.

The Netflix app is actually fantastic with its support for multiple viewing modes, which include playing video on the closed outer display, the “laptop” orientation with its big player controls, and the “tent” mode.

Other developers have similarly reimagined how their app would work if it had originally been designed for the Duo’s small and larger screens.

One good example of this is Sofa, a handy utility for organizing the books, movies, TV shows, podcasts, albums, and games you want to check out. When the Duo is closed, its navigation is tucked off to the right side in a vertical bar, while the main screen focuses on the items you’ve saved. But when the Duo is unfolded, the number of items displayed on the screen also expands into a two-column layout, with more of your items featured on the screen and your lists on the left side. It doesn’t feel like two different apps, but like you’ve opened up the app itself and given it more space to spread out.

In terms of your daily social media scroll, apps like TikTok and Instagram have been updated to be perfectly usable on the Duo, whether the phone is open or closed. When closed, TikTok now stretches the video to cover the screen, instead of showing the ugly black bar. Scrolling short-form videos on the Duo, while unfolded like a slightly smaller iPad mini, has become my preferred way to watch.

Here’s another surprise: I’ve spent less time with my Kindle, and am instead reading books on my Duo. Unfolded, the Duo is a decent enough replacement for an e-book reader, albeit it can be a bit more stimulating as it’s not an e-ink device. However, the matte inner screen isn’t quite as bright and distracting as your typical glossy OLED display. There’s less glare and softer reflections, but there’s also something about the display that just feels calmer than a smartphone, allowing you to more fully engage with a book’s text.

Unfolded and held vertically, reading on the Duo feels a lot like reading on the standard Kindle, at least in terms of hand-feel. And putting your phone into the “Do Not Disturb” focus mode can help you not be distracted by incoming notifications and messages while you read.

You can also unfold the device while keeping it in landscape orientation, with the screens bent slightly inward like a real book. For even more fun, you can turn on Kindle’s page-turn animation, which makes it seem as if you’re turning the page in a physical book. And I love how you can “performatively read” using an app like Book2Life or Actually Read — they display the book’s cover on the Duo’s outer screen as you read the text on the inner screen.

E-book readers will likely find they can carry one less device when they’re out and about, because the Duo can stand in for whatever Kindle or other reader you typically keep in your purse or bag. You can save the Kindle for other times, whether that’s at home, on long travel days, or when you don’t want to worry about sand getting into your $2,000 phone.

On the big-screened iPhone 17 Pro Max I used to carry, I tended to keep a PopSocket of some sort on the back because the phone was both heavy and large, which made it hard to hold for longer periods of time. The Duo is similarly heavy, but its shape makes it far easier to hold in one hand without the extra help.

Folded, you can do the usual one-handed hold where the bottom edge of the phone rests on your pinky, your other three fingers cradle the back, and your thumb is free to navigate the screen.

Unfolded, the Duo’s like using a small Kindle or iPad mini, where two hands are required. You also don’t need a PopSocket for balancing the phone on the side, because it can be propped up like a tent or opened like a tiny laptop. Or, if you prefer, Apple and others offer Duo cases with a built-in stand. (Apple’s case comes in navy and taupe, though, so if you’re not boring, you’ll probably want to shop from third-party vendors).

If you think you still might need a PopSocket at times, or you just aren’t ready to give it up, the brand now makes a slimmer version: its Low-Pro Grip. There are other competitors, like the OhSnap grips, which aren’t a bad alternative either.

There’s something about the Duo that seems to have kicked off a new iPhone personalization trend, at least judging by what I’ve been seeing on social media.

Unlike a regular iPhone, where every phone case is simply a shell that offers a degree of protection based on the materials used, the Duo case lineup provides far more variety.

You can buy cases with and without built-in screen protectors, and some use adhesive to better stick to the outer screen, while others don’t. Some cases let you customize the band around the outer screen with a different color than the case that protects the rest of the phone.

Then you have cases that cover the hinge, while others leave it exposed. There are cases that offer holes where you can attach straps, wristlets, and charms. Some of those have attachment points at the bottom and others on the side.

Essentially, there are a lot of options for customizing how you want to carry the Duo and how you want to express your own style.

I’m also now starting to see whimsical cases that come with attached pens. (Thank goodness — most of the pen cases so far have been kind of plain!)

I also believe there’s something about the Duo that particularly appeals to women. Maybe because it’s small, or maybe it’s because you can attach a strap to its side and carry it like a little purse. Or maybe because, like a purse, you can deck it out with chains and charms — a choice that’s grown increasingly popular as young people have embraced whimsical, retro tech as a way to express their individuality and sense of fashion.

The Duo easily lends itself to this trend, perhaps even more than a regular iPhone. It becomes an accessory you want on display, not one buried in a bag.

Before switching to the Duo, I spent a lot of time thinking about what I’d be giving up by moving away from the Pro Max.

I’m surprised to find that, a month later, I only think about what I’d have to give up if I switched back to a traditional iPhone. It’s not just the extra space of the unfolded screen I’d miss — it’s all the little habits I’ve developed around having different screen sizes for different purposes.

The Duo makes me pause and think about how I want to use an app. That small moment of deliberation, before I even open the phone, has helped me break some of my more mindless smartphone habits, particularly around social media.

That’s not a benefit I could have anticipated if I’d judged the device by its specifications alone.

#60. Pregnant US inmate escapes in prison vehicle after medical appointment

A pregnant inmate has escaped from custody in the US state of New Mexico, after taking control of a police vehicle while returning to prison from a prenatal appointment, the state's corrections department has said.

Rebecca Taylor, 26, took control of the Dodge Journey and drove away when the team transporting her and another inmate stopped to pick up food in Albuquerque, local media reported.

The New Mexico Corrections Department said that the vehicle and her prison jumpsuit were later recovered, but Taylor remains on the run.

She had been imprisoned for multiple theft and burglary charges, the department said in a Facebook statement.

Local media reported that another inmate, also travelling back from a prenatal appointment, was able to get out of the car as Taylor took control on Wednesday morning.

The corrections department has said that a multi-agency search for Taylor is under way. The BBC has approached it for an update.

The Albuquerque Journal reported that Taylor is currently in her second trimester of pregnancy. For this reason, she was not restrained during transportation, department spokesperson Brittany Roembach said.

"It is the understanding right now that one officer was with the vehicle but not inside of the vehicle when this occurred," she was quoted as saying.

All prisons in New Mexico went into lockdown following Taylor's escape, but it was lifted at 06:00 on Thursday.

However the prison where she was being held, the Western New Mexico Corrections Facility, will remain on "modified movement", and no visitors are currently allowed.

The public has been told not to approach Rebecca Taylor if they see her, and call police straight away.

#59. X finally gets parental controls after 20 years

After 20 years of existence, X, the social network formerly known as Twitter, has finally added parental controls for teenagers, giving parents and guardians a way to manage what their kids see and interact with on the platform. But unlike most platforms’ implementation of such controls, X is putting its own spin on the system by giving teens a say in the matter.

The company said accounts belonging to 13-to-17-year-olds will be set to “protected mode” by default, meaning they won’t be able to see sensitive content, and won’t be served targeted ads. However, X is allowing teenagers to retain a measure of control over their accounts, letting them see what restrictions were made to their accounts. Parents and guardians will also not be able to read their teens’ private messages, see search or viewing history, or post as their teen.

The controls let parents see how much time their teen spends on X per week, set daily limits, require their approval for new followers, limit feed recommendations and videos to already followed accounts, filter specific words, turn off sensitive media, specify who can send their teen DMs, and see who they are messaging.

The controls arrive months after X came under fire for a lack of controls preventing the platform’s AI chatbot from being used to generate sexualized images of children and women. The social network has also long been rife with child sexual abuse material, misinformation, AI bots, and openly lets users post porn.

X has generally not been a platform aimed at teenagers, but that doesn’t mean they don’t use the platform. A 2024 study found that nearly a third of Gen Alpha kids have an X account. The platform itself requires users to be at least 13 years old, though that’s not a high bar given the kind of content on the platform.

“Parents want practical tools, and teens deserve room to grow. With Parental Controls on X, families can set the guardrails that fit their home, while teens keep ownership of their accounts and can see every change. Striking that balance is vital and what we set out to build.” Michael O’Herlihy, head of safety at X, wrote in a blog post.

#57. Guinea-Bissau junta delays presidential vote for 2 years

The decision came exactly one year after the military took power on November 26, 2025. The junta said the conditions were insufficient to hold presidential elections for now.

The ruling junta in the West African nation of Guinea-Bissau announced Thursday that it is postponing elections by two years until November 26, 2028.

The decision came on the 1-year anniversary of the military takeover in Guinea-Bissau on November 26, 2025. The country is currently led by military general Horta Inta-a, who serves as transitional president.

Inta-a said in a statement that there was "an insufficiency of the technical, material and financial conditions" to hold the election.

"It is essential to guarantee effective conditions for preparing and holding elections in accordance with the principles of legality, democratic participation, equal opportunity, transparency and electoral integrity," he said.

The military coup in Guinea-Bissau came three days after a general election last year, with the military deposing incumbent President Umaro Sissoco Embalo. Both Embalo and opposition candidate Fernando Dias da Costa claimed victory in the election, with the military arresting Embalo one day prior to the results being released.

To view this video please enable JavaScript, and consider upgrading to a web browser that supports HTML5 video

West African bloc ECOWAS has urged Guinea-Bissau to transition back to civilian rule.

Guinea-Bissau is a small tropical Portuguese-speaking nation on the West African coast which has seen multiple coups in its history. The country faces a high level of corruption and is also a key point for cocaine trafficking to Europe.

According to Washington DC-based think tank Freedom House, Guinea-Bissau is only "partly free" due to military interference in elections.

Reporters without Borders ranksGuinea-Bissau 100 out of 180 countries on its press freedom index, saying the "sharp deterioration of security for media professionals, combined with political and economic pressure, has severely tested the practice of journalism in recent years."

#55. Is Alice Huang a spy or not?

The central, still-unproven claim in the Nvidia scandal is that Alice Huang, a former executive at the data-center company Megaspeed, is a Chinese spy. She had an affair with Nvidia CEO Jensen Huang, who abruptly resigned on Nov. 12 after acknowledging it and was just as abruptly reinstated on Monday of this week.

What’s publicly known is murkier than the headlines suggest. U.S. intelligence has reportedly tracked Alice Huang’s contact with an officer of China’s spy agency, but officials haven’t offered public evidence that she was commissioned to target Nvidia’s chief. In the past week, after President Trump successfully pushed to bring Jensen Huang back, the administration has played down the allegations it once circulated internally. Whether that reflects new intelligence or political pressure is the big question.

Undisputed reporting shows the claim emerged from the White House itself. It first appeared in a highly classified memo written in March under the oversight of Secretary of State Marco Rubio and circulated to White House officials ahead of Trump’s trip to China in May. The memo formed the partial basis for the New York Times’ initial reporting on the scandal, published Oct. 29 and its existence was first reported on Nov. 8.

The memo did not definitively say, according to the Wall Street Journal, that Alice Huang was a Chinese spy. CNN, by contrast, has reported that the memo concluded she was a Chinese intelligence asset.

What’s better established is that Alice Huang was in touch with Chinese intelligence. The Ministry of State Security (MSS) is Beijing’s civilian spy agency, responsible for both foreign intelligence and domestic counterintelligence. Last weekend, the Times, citing people with knowledge of U.S. intelligence reports, reported that Huang was in contact with an MSS officer from the spring of 2025 until this fall, a period that overlaps her relationship with Jensen Huang. (The Chinese government has called the spy claims a “smear,” and Megaspeed says Huang left the company in August 2025 and that its business is legitimate.)

Top Trump officials have nonetheless all but discredited the espionage claims, along with the suggestion that Nvidia fell prey to spying or foreign influence.

Attorney General Todd Blanche, who was Trump’s personal defense lawyer before taking over the Justice Department, told journalists on Tuesday: “I have no reason to stand up here and say Nvidia is anything but a good corporate citizen.” On the same day, Trump said a pardon was an option for Jensen Huang but that he did not think one would be needed. Senior intelligence officials reportedly told Congress that the government apparently has no actual evidence that the MSS directed Alice Huang to pursue an affair with Jensen Huang.

None of those statements necessarily exonerates Alice Huang as an intelligence agent. Instead, they support the alternate theory that the Chinese government didn’t tell her to begin the affair and that Jensen Huang broke no law by having it. Not being directed to have an affair isn’t the same as not being a spy, though.

From a certain angle, the circumstantial evidence surrounding Alice Huang looks quite suspicious. She was a top executive at a company investigated for illicitly channeling Nvidia chips to China, and her husband, Kang Wei, is said to have even closer ties to the MSS, with some sources saying he worked for the agency.

The timing of the government’s reversal is at the heart of the matter. Trump has said he lobbied the board “directly and indirectly” to reinstate Jensen Huang as CEO, so it wouldn’t be surprising if officials who serve at his pleasure were recasting the case in terms that satisfy his agenda. That wouldn’t necessarily make their assessments wrong. But unless new intelligence has actually resolved the question, and none has been publicly identified as yet, that backdrop is critical to keep in mind. CBS has also reported that Rubio, whose office oversaw the original memo, has privately voiced concerns about aspects of Jensen Huang’s return.

Doubts persist elsewhere, too. Earlier this month, CNN reported that the intelligence community was still working to understand Alice Huang’s relationship with Jensen Huang and that the intelligence collected on her to date does not prove she was acting as a spy for Beijing. After a briefing by top intelligence officials earlier this month, Rep. Jim Himes (D-Conn.), the incoming chair of the House Intelligence Committee, said he was unsure whether she was a spy or a “useful idiot.” And this week, the Wall Street Journal pointed out that under Chinese leader Xi Jinping, top corporate executives routinely maintain ties to the country’s intelligence service without being formally deputized as assets.

The crush of conflicting claims, most from anonymous government sources, obviously leaves large questions unanswered as to what actually transpired. Some of those questions that naturally spring to mind:

There are answers to all of these questions, of course, but when the public will get them is anybody’s guess. Jensen Huang is due to appear before the Senate Banking Committee next week, and by the look from the statements of some of its members, he’s likely in for a rough ride. Unfortunately, congressional hearings, particularly public ones, rarely unearth secrets that haven’t already been disclosed.

#54. Gérard Depardieu to stand trial for alleged rape of actress in 2018, court rules

GÃ©rard Depardieu has faced a series of allegations in recent years on film sets and elsewhere

French film actor GÃ©rard Depardieu is to stand trial for the alleged rape of actress Charlotte Arnould, a Paris appeals court has confirmed.

Arnould, who was 22 at the time, had reported the alleged rape several days after going to his house in Paris to rehearse a play.

She has waived her right to anonymity and her lawyer said Thursday's ruling was a "first" and the forthcoming trial would be historic.

Depardieu, 77, had contested his indictment on two counts of rape. In a letter published in Le Figaro in 2023, Depardieu strongly denied Arnould's allegations, saying "there was never any coercion or violence between us".

Depardieu was for decades a leading figure in French and international cinema for roles in Green Card, The Man in the Iron Mask and the Asterix films. He was awarded France's LÃ©gion d'honneur in 1996.

But in recent years a series of allegations have been made against him over his conduct on film sets and elsewhere in the wake of France's #MeToo movement, known as balance ton porc (call out your pig).

Last year, he was given an 18-month suspended jail term after he was found guilty of sexually assaulting two women on a film set in Paris in May. He was also placed on the sex offenders register and ordered to pay compensation.

He dropped his appeal against the ruling last week and offered "sincere apologies" for his behaviour on the set of director Jean Becker's 2021 film Les Volets Verts (The Green Shutters).

Depardieu had been a friend of Charlotte Arnould's father and on 7 August 2018 she had gone to his home, where much of what happened was captured on the actor's own CCTV.

Arnould's case was initially dismissed for insufficient evidence, but after she filed a civil complaint, a judicial investigation was opened in the summer of 2020.

Speaking outside the Paris appeal court on Thursday, her lawyer Carine Durrieu Diebolt said the ruling was a victory for her client for whom it was "genuinely a relief".

The appeal court said that while Depardieu should stand trial for what took place on that day, he would not be tried for a further alleged rape at a hotel six days later.

Depardieu's lawyer David-Olivier Kaminski told reporters that court had partly ruled in his favour: "As soon as we get the ruling, we will look into the possibility of appealing to the Court of Cassation."

Meanwhile, Depardieu is under a separate investigation alongside French author Michel Houellebecq for "allegations of a sexual nature".

French authorities confirmed their inquiry last week, following reports of an alleged "gang rape" on the set of the film Thalasso in 2018.

#53. Nvidia hits pause on chip shipments to Southeast Asia

Nvidia is under pressure to do things differently, and a new WSJ report shows how far that’s going.

According to the Journal, Nvidia has told manufacturing partners, including Dell and Super Micro, to hit pause on server shipments to Malaysia, Singapore, Thailand, and Indonesia for an unspecified period as it assesses whether its regional partners are meeting U.S. export requirements.

As for why those countries, Chinese companies have been tapping Nvidia chips remotely from data centers there, a loophole the US Commerce Department closed — reportedly grudgingly — earlier this month. The Journal notes that the think tank Institute for Progress estimates remote data centers like these supply about 24% of China’s AI computing power.

#51. Russian authorities detain suspect over St. Petersburg cafe blast

Our live coverage for the day has ended. Follow the latest Ukraine news here or read through the updates below.

The preventive detention hearing for Daria Trepova, who was detained in connection with the explosion at a cafe in St. Petersburg, Russia, will take place on Tuesday at Basmanny District Court in Moscow, according to Russian state news agency Vesti.

Russian authorities detained Trepova, 26, claiming she was involved in the blast that killed well-known military blogger Vladlen Tatarsky at a café in St. Petersburg on Sunday.

The US Ambassador to the United Nations Linda Thomas-Greenfield on Monday described Russia assuming the United Nations Security Council (UNSC) presidency this month as “an April Fool’s joke.”

“But the truth of the matter is, it’s a rotating seat. We expect that they will behave professionally. But we also expect that they will use their seat to spread disinformation and to promote their own agenda as it relates to Ukraine, and we will stand ready to call them out at every single moment that they attempt to do that,” Greenfield added.

Greenfield said she is not surprised if the Russians asked the foreign minister to come to UNSC headquarters in New York but also said council does work beyond Ukraine.

“We haven’t decided yet on what our attendance levels will be, but we intend to carry out the business of the Security Council during this month. The Security Council does more than Ukraine. We work on many issues, and we again expect that Russia will carry their presidency in a professional way, but when they don’t, we will stand ready to call them out,” Greenfield said.

Vasily Nebenzya, Russian ambassador to the United Nations, dismissed concerns that Russia could fairly be the president of the Security Council for April during the ongoing war in Ukraine.

He said there were no complaints in February 2022 when Russia was last president of the council, while Russia invaded and pointed out that the US was president of the council in 2003 — the year Iraq was invaded.

The ambassador said as long as world order is maintained, there will not be any change in UN procedures that might lead to a change in Russia’s status.

Russia took over presidency on Saturday of the UN’s top security body, which rotates every month.

Newly published video shows the moments running up to an explosion that killed Russian military journalist Vladen Tartovsky in a St Petersburg café on Sunday.

Tatarsky died when a blast tore through the cafe where he was appearing as a guest of a pro-war group called Cyber Front Z. Prior Russian media reports suggested that Tatarsky may have been killed by a device hidden in a figurine presented to him by a woman before the blast.

The 25-second video shows Tartovsky putting a statuette given to him at the event into a box after passing the microphone to another man.

He places the statuette back into the box and then covers it with what appears to be paper wrapping. As he presses down the paper, there is an explosion.

In the last two seconds of the video, Tartovsky is pixillated, but the rest of the scene is not.

More to know: The well-known Russian blogger was killed in what appeared to be an audacious attack on a high-profile pro-Kremlin figure, officials said.

Authorities said they were treating the case as suspected murder.

At least 32 people were injured in the blast, with 10 people in serious condition, state media Ria Novosti reported, citing the Russian Ministry of Health.

Russian ambassador to the United Nations Vasily Nebenzya on Monday scoffed at concerns that his country could fairly be the president of the Security Council for April during the war in Ukraine.

Nebenzya pointed out that the US was president of the council in 2003 — the year Iraq was invaded.

He said there were no complaints in February 2022 when Russia was last president of the council, while Russia invaded.

The ambassador said as long as world order is maintained, there will not be any change in UN procedures that might lead to a change in Russia’s status.

Russia took over presidency on Saturday of the UN’s top security body, which rotates every month.

Ukraine has received the first tranche of $2.7 billion from a new International Monetary Fund (IMF) program, Ukrainian Finance Minister Sergii Marchenko said in a tweet Monday.

The IMF, which regularly makes emergency loans to countries in crisis, on Friday approved a new four-year extended arrangement under the Extended Fund Facility (EFF) of around $15.6 billion as part of a $115 billion total support package for Ukraine.

The program aims to “anchor policies that sustain fiscal, external, price and financial stability and support economic recovery, while enhancing governance and strengthening institutions to promote long-term growth in the context of post-war reconstruction and Ukraine’s path to EU accession,” the IMF said in a statement.

The program also helps Ukraine to carry out “more ambitious structural reforms,” it said.

The Extended Fund Facility (EFF) loan is the first major conventional financing program approved by the IMF for a country involved in a large-scale war, Reuters reported.

The risks to the arrangement are “exceptionally high,” said Gita Gopinath, first deputy managing director of the global lender.

“The success of the program depends on the size, composition, and timing of external financing on concessional terms to help close fiscal and external financing gaps and restore debt sustainability on a forward-looking basis under the baseline and downside scenarios,” she added.

German Chancellor Olaf Scholz on Monday assured Moldova of Berlin’s support on its path to accession to the European Union as Moldovan and American officials allege Russia of trying to weaken the government in Chisinau.

“Moldova is part of our European family. In the summer, we granted it candidate status. And I very much welcome how resolutely Moldova has tackled the necessary reforms that are indispensable for EU accession,” Scholz told a news conference in Bucharest, Romania alongside Romanian President Klaus Iohannis and Moldovan President Maia Sandu.

“Moldova can be sure of our support on this path. I assured the President [Sandu] of this once again today. Moldova does not stand alone, but receives massive international support,” the German chancellor continued.

Scholz expressed “great concern” about reports of alleged Russian attempts to destabilize Moldova and said Germany would do its “utmost” to support Moldova in arming itself against “attempts of destabilization by Russia.”

In February, Moldova’s President Sandu accused Russia of planning to use “saboteurs who have undergone military training and are disguised as civilians” to destabilize the country — claims which were rejected by Russia’s foreign ministry as “unfounded.”

According to White House officials, the US believes that Russia is working to weaken the Moldovan government, as it continues to seek closer ties with the European Union.

“The sovereignty and territorial integrity of any state is inviolable. This requirement of the Helsinki Final Act and other agreements under international law was also signed by Russia. And it is still valid. Therefore, we do our utmost to support Moldova in arming itself against attempts of destabilization by Russia,” Scholz said.

Speaking at the same event, Sandu said that “it is very important, and I am happy that Moldova is a dialogue partner with Romania and Germany. The projects we are involved in together are very useful for our people and will strongly lead us to the accession (to the European Union).”

CNN’s Radina Gigova, Anna Chernova and Natasha Bertrand contributed reporting to this post.

NATO Secretary General Jens Stoltenberg said Monday that the alliance has not seen any changes in Russia’s nuclear posture since Russian President Vladimir Putin announced late last month Moscow’s plans to station tactical nuclear weapons in neighboring Belarus.

“We monitor very closely what Russia does. But so far, we haven’t seen any changes in their nuclear posture that requires any change in our nuclear posture,” the NATO chief said, while answering questions from reporters on the topic at a news conference in Brussels.

Stoltenberg described Putin’s announcement as a “pattern of dangerous, reckless nuclear rhetoric.”

“President Putin tries to use nuclear weapons as a way to prevent us from supporting Ukraine… intimidation, coercion to stop NATO Allies and partners for supporting Ukraine in their right to defend their own country,” he said.

“We will not be intimidated. We will continue to support Ukraine,” Stoltenberg continued.

NATO’s chief Jens Stoltenberg said on Monday that Western allies have delivered more than $70 billion in military aid to Ukraine over the last year since the Russian invasion started.

“There are no signs that President Putin is preparing for peace. He is preparing for more war,” Stoltenberg warned, speaking at a news conference in Brussels.

“We do not know when this war will end. But when it does, we will need to put in place arrangements so that Ukraine can deter future aggression. And history does not repeat itself. We cannot allow Russia to continue to chip away at European security,” he said.

Stoltenberg added that allies have delivered 65 billion euros (around $70.7 billion) in military aid, and said he welcomed the recent arrival of modern battle tanks and other armored vehicles in Ukraine.

“This can make a real difference on the front lines and allow the Ukrainian forces to liberate more territory,” he said.

The NATO head affirmed that the alliance’s support is for the “long haul” and said, “we will discuss how we can step up our support, including by continuing to strengthen Ukraine’s armed forces and supporting the transition from Soviet-era to NATO equipment and doctrine.”

Stoltenberg also said allies must address other threats and challenges, citing instability, terrorism, and the growing influence of Iran, Russia, and China. He urged allies to invest more in defense to tackle all these wide range of issues.

Norway and Denmark will cooperate to donate 8,000 artillery rounds to Ukraine, the Norwegian government announced in a statement on Monday.

“Norway donates the shells, while Denmark donates the associated fuzes, propellant bags and primer cartridges,” the statement said.

Ukrainian President Volodymyr Zelensky reacted to the announcement, saying in a tweet that he was grateful for the donation.

“Grateful to the Danish and Norwegian governments led by @Statsmin and @jonasgahrstore for the joint initiative to transfer an additional batch of artillery shells to Ukraine. Timely military assistance from partners is the key to bringing our joint victory closer!” Zelensky said.

The rounds will be “standard NATO 155 mm artillery ammunition and will supplement the Danish donation of 19 Caesar self-propelled howitzers,” Norway’s statement added.

Norway’s artillery shells will be delivered from the country’s armed forces’ own stock, according to the statement.

Norwegian defense minister Bjørn Arild Gram said “Ukraine has a significant need for artillery ammunition. Norway will contribute where we can. It is important for both Europe’s and Norway’s security that Ukraine succeeds in standing up to Russia’s attack.”

“The Ukrainians still have a great and urgent need for ammunition for their fight for freedom against Russia. Denmark, together with other EU and NATO countries, is in the process of accommodating with a number of different military contributions. Our cooperation with Norway is an important example of this,” the Danish acting defense minister, Troels Lund Poulsen, said in the statement.

The Ukrainian military has reported little change to the front lines but heavy fire from Russian forces at various parts of the front line in the Donetsk region.

The General Staff said that more than 45 enemy attacks had been repelled over the past day, with the focus on Lyman, Bakhmut, Avdiivka and Mariinka.

It said there were 17 air strikes and more than 20 strikes by multiple launch rocket systems.

“In the Bakhmut direction, the enemy is trying to take full control over the city of Bakhmut and continues to storm it,” the General Staff said.

West of the city, Ukrainian defense forces repelled about 20 enemy attacks, according to the General Staff.

Oleksandr Syrskyi, commander of Ukrainian Land Forces, posted on Telegram that in Bakhmut he had met with “soldiers and commanders who destroyed the myth of invincibility of the Wagner fighters and Russian paratroopers.”

“The enemy is weakening and trying to cover up its failures with new fakes about the capture of Bakhmut,” Syrskyi said.

Syrskyi referenced the possibility of a future counter-offensive, saying that “the forward movement of our entire army depends on what every soldier is doing right now on their section of the front.”

Some of the most intense fighting is to the northwest and west of the city of Donetsk – as the Russians continue months-long efforts to dislodge Ukrainian defenses in Avdiivka and Mariinka. The General Staff said that some 20 assaults had been repelled.

“There are also some operational successes of our military in the Donetsk area. Our military have taken some positions. They have taken a more strategic and favourable position,” said Oleksii Dmytrashkivskyi, spokesperson for the military in that zone.

He also noted that Russian special forces had been introduced in the area.

“The enemy is trying to attack our positions between Avdiivka and Marinka. These attempts do not stop. It should be noted that the enemy has suffered quite significant losses,” Dmytrashkivskyi said.

He also claimed that the Russians “don’t have the same amount of equipment and, as they themselves say, they are on a ‘diet’ of ammunition for assaults.”

Social media video showed both sides engaged in heavy fire in the Avdiivka area, which the Russians have surrounded on three sides.

Denis Pushilin, the leader of the self-declared Donetsk People’s Republic, claimed that Russian forces were still advancing in the Avdiivka direction.

“It is still premature to speak of encirclement, but the situation is being created, if not critical, then extremely difficult for the enemy… After clearing Kamianka, our units are moving further. There are difficulties both with the terrain, which must also be overcome, and there are also similarities with weather conditions. But these difficulties are mutual — both for the enemy and for us,” Pushilin said.

The war in Ukraine has cost an estimated $2.6 billion of damage to heritage and cultural sites in the country, the UN cultural body UNESCO said in a recently published report.

The report, which covers one year of war in Ukraine between February 24, 2022, and February 24, 2023, was a joint assessment conducted by the Ukrainian government, the World Bank, the European Commission, and the United Nations.

UNESCO was in charge of the part of the report dedicated to culture and tourism.

“As of February 24, 2023, the total damage cost from identified assets is estimated at $2.6 billion,” the report said in its assessment of damage related to culture and tourism.

Some $1.7 billion of that cost was distributed to “historic cities, buildings, and sites imbued with recognized cultural/social values,” $650 million to tourism facilities, $143 million to “movable cultural properties and collections, repositories of culture,” and $150 million to “buildings/workshops/ateliers dedicated to cultural and creative industries,” according to UNESCO.

The most impacted region in Ukraine was Kharkivska, which has suffered 30% of the total damage to its cultural assets, the report added.

UNESCO said that losses are estimated at around $15.2 billion, which includes revenue losses from tourism, art, sports, entertainment, recreation, cultural education, and creative and cultural industries.

“The war has significantly impacted the diversity and richness of culture and cultural heritage in Ukraine, causing damage to cultural infrastructure and assets, reducing livelihoods for cultural creators, bearers and practitioners, limiting access to culture, and impeding the exercise of cultural rights,” the report said.

UNESCO’s head Audrey Azoulay is currently in Ukraine, the organization told CNN on Monday.

NATO Secretary General Jens Stoltenberg said it will be historic when the military alliance raises the Finnish flag for the first time at its headquarters on Tuesday.

“This is a historic week. Tomorrow we will welcome Finland as the 31st member of NATO, making Finland safer and our alliance stronger. We will raise the Finnish flag for the first time here at NATO Headquarters,” Stoltenberg said, speaking in Brussels Monday.

“It will be a good day for Finland’s security, for Nordic security, and for NATO as a whole,” Stoltenberg added.

Finland is set to become a member of the alliance on Tuesday, with Finnish President Sauli Niinistö traveling to Brussels to attend an accession ceremony at NATO headquarters.

Stoltenberg also spoke about Sweden’s bid to join NATO, saying Finland’s accession is “in itself something we should celebrate” but is also good for Sweden.

“It makes Sweden more integrated into NATO and makes Sweden even more safer,” Stoltenberg said, adding “at the same time we celebrate and enjoy that Finland is now full-fledged member, we should continue work to finalize the Swedish accession process.”

CNN’s Laurent Kent contributed to this report.

#49. Diesel tanker carrying up to 7,000 tonnes capsizes near Ibiza, killing one

The Med Emre vessel capsized near Ibiza and Spain's Clara Campoamor, a pollution control vessel, was sent to assess the environmental impact

An oil tanker carrying up to 7,000 tonnes of diesel has capsized off the coast of Ibiza, leaving one crew member dead, authorities have said.

The Med Emre, a Malta-flagged vessel, was carrying 17 crew members who were all rescued. However seven were taken to hospital and one was later pronounced dead, Balearic Islands emergency services confirmed.

The six crew receiving treatment reportedly suffered minor injuries, such as hypothermia, Spanish media reports.

The possible environmental damage is being assessed by a plane and Spain's pollution control vessel, Clara Campoamor, which is owned by the country's national maritime rescue service.

"There is still little information available about a possible fuel spill," Balearic Islands' President Marga Prohens wrote on X.

Regional emergencies director Pablo GÃ¡rriz said the tanker had not completely sunk, as had been originally reported. The vessel remains, however, partially submerged.

That made conditions more favourable for carrying out recovery operations, he said, "as we have gone from the possibility of the vessel being at a depth of 1,000m to a situation that allows operations to be carried out on the surface".

The Med Emre capsized 25 nautical miles (46 km; 29 miles) from Ibiza's coastline and 57 nautical miles from Valencia. It is unclear what caused the sinking.

The chemical tanker was en route from Algeciras, in southern Spain, to Barcelona in the north-east.

Rescuers in Valencia received a distress call around 22:30 local time (21:30 GMT) on Wednesday, according to Spanish media. Minutes later, the vessel reported a leak and the crew said it was leaning at a 90-degree angle.

The injured crew members have been taken to hospitals in Valencia and Can Misses Hospital in Ibiza, President Prohens said.

Ibiza's marine environment was added to the Unesco World Heritage List in 1999. In particular, it is home to dense prairies of Posidonia oceanic seagrass, which is found only in the Mediterranean basin and plays a vital role in supporting local marine biodiversity.

In 2002, Spain experienced one of its worst environmental disasters when the Prestige oil tanker ran into trouble off Galicia's coastline. It spilled more than 60,000 tonnes of heavy fuel oil into the sea.

#48. Gerard Depardieu to stand trial in France for rape and sexual assault

The Paris Court of Appeal upheld the order for the 77-year-old French actor to stand trial on charges of raping and sexually assaulting an actress in 2018.

French actor Gerard Depardieu failed in his attempt to overturn an order for him to stand trial on rape charges.

On Thursday, the Paris Court of Appeal ruled that Depardieu should face trial for the alleged rape and sexual assault that occurred on August 7, 2018. However, the court dismissed the allegations relating to a second incident that occurred six days later.

Depardieu still has the option of appealing to a higher court. His lawyer, Delphine Meillet, told the Associated Press that she disputes Thursday's ruling, though she did not specify whether she will challenge it in France's highest civil and criminal court.

This decision comes just two weeks after the 77-year-old Depardieu dropped his appeal against a sexual assault conviction in a separate case. He accepted an 18-month suspended prison sentence and issued a public apology.

An actress, who was 22 years old at the time, accused Depardieu of raping and sexually assaulting her at his Paris apartment on August 7 and 13, 2018. Depardieu was 69 at the time of the alleged incident.

She reported the allegations to the police a few days later. Depardieu denies the allegations, claiming that the encounter was consensual.

Last year, a trial was ordered regarding the allegations. If the trial goes ahead, Depardieu could face up to 20 years in prison.

Carine Durrieu Diebolt, the victim's lawyer, said that the Thursday decision was a genuine relief and a victory for her client.

Depardieu was born in 1948 into a working-class family in Chateauroux, a city in central France. He overcame a speech impediment to become one of France's most prolific actors.

The film star was a dominant figure in French cinema for half a century, becoming a renowned cultural export. He earned an Oscar nomination for "Cyrano de Bergerac" and a Golden Globe for "Green Card."

Accusations of sexual misconduct and his subsequent conviction, however, have increasingly overshadowed his cinematic legacy.

The international media has also been paying attention to Depardieu's off-screen antics. One of the most notable instances was when he accepted a Russian passport from President Vladimir Putin in 2013 amid a dispute over French taxes.

#43. Albania's youth are protesting in Flamingo Revolution, not leaving the country anymore

Youth protests against a rigid political system and corruption have shaken the government of Albanian Prime Minister Edi Rama. They also show that fewer young people are willing to emigrate in search of a better future.

In mid-October, students in the Albanian capital Tirana set a suitcase on fire and threw it into the courtyard of the Education Ministry. Everyone in the country understood the symbolism: According to the 2023 census, the population of this Western Balkan country shrunk by about 420,000 between 2011 and 2023 — by nearly 15% — primarily because of emigration.

For a generation of young Albanians, this was the response to a system that gave them little voice. But now young people want to stay, and the protesters are saying that the system has to change.

Known as the "Flamingo Revolution," the social movement began in May with demonstrations against a luxury resort in a coastal reserve that US President Donald Trump's daughter Ivanka Trump and her husband Jared Kushner were involved in.

It quickly evolved into broader criticism against corruption, Prime Minister Edi Rama's government and his ruling Socialist Party and the country's entire political class.

Thousands marched almost every evening for four months in protests that were largely peaceful. But on October 13, a student protest escalated and there were clashes with police. Since then, the unrest has continued, with police using tear gas, water cannons and rubber bullets against demonstrators. There have been hundreds of arrests and many injuries, amid damage to public and private property.

The protesters have called for Rama's resignation, a transitional government, electoral reform and fresh elections. The movement, which has no leader, has support from people across the political spectrum. And while the prime minister has blamed "foreign hands" for the movement, there seems to be little evidence that external actors are orchestrating the protests, though they may have attempted to exploit the unrest online.

The protesters, who do not feel represented by Albania's democracy, are driven by domestic issues: corruption, the high cost of living and low wages. This is nothing new, but many factors have converged recently: a series of high-level corruption charges, including against ministers and two of Rama's former deputies, rising prices and the fact that Albania is being measured against EU standards by a new generation.

In certain areas, Rama has transformed Albania since taking office in 2013: the economy has grown significantly, as the government likes to point out, and tourism is booming. But the prosperity remains unevenly distributed. Critics accuse the government of channeling too much taxpayer money into a small number of well-connected companies through prestigious projects and public-private partnerships, and in some cases through corrupt business deals, at the expense of healthcare, education and better economic prospects for the wider population.

Rama has rejected the calls for his resignation and has invited protesters to engage in dialogue, which they have so far refused.

In 2008, Rama and Sali Berisha, the long-time chairman of the opposition Democratic Party, worked on a reform to Albania's constitution and electoral law, which was approved by parliament. Since then, it is party leaders who have determined who enters Parliament, which weakens ties between lawmakers and their constituents.

In addition, it has become significantly more difficult for new parties to win seats. In 2025, a new party received about 64,000 votes, but only one seat. The Socialists, by contrast, won about one seat for every 10,000 votes.

Rama has been the leader of the Socialists since 2005, while Berisha first became the chairman of the Democratic Party in 1991. For 21 years, Albania's prime minister has either been Rama or Berisha. Freedom House, a US nongovernmental organization that promotes democracy worldwide, classifies Albania as "partly free" and describes the country's political parties as being "often organized around leading personalities."

Protesters on the streets have said that they want both men to be jailed. Rama has argued that governments should be replaced at the ballot box, not on the streets.

Though the Organization for Security and Co-operation in Europe (OSCE) acknowledged that parliamentary elections in 2025 were "competitive and professionally managed" it said that they had taken place "in a highly polarized environment and contestants did not enjoy a level playing field."

It added that there were "reports of intimidation, misuse of public resources and pressure on public employees, which undermined equal opportunities."

The Democratic Party also spoke of election fraud and intimidation. Turnout was below 45%.

Many protesters also allege that when it comes to business, what matters is the proximity to people in power. They criticize the public procurement, public auctions and concessions system, which has led to state-owned land, including coastal and protected areas, being sold to private investors.

Ordinary Albanians are feeling the financial brunt. According to the Albanian Institute of Statistics (INSTAT), half of all employees earn less than 60,000 lek (roughly €620 or $680) per month after taxes. But according to Eurostat, food prices are slightly above the EU average and real estate prices in Tirana have nearly doubled since 2018, with rents rising just as quickly.

For many years, young Albanians who were dissatisfied with the system opted to leave the country. The number of Albanian citizens in Germany more than doubled between 2018 and 2024, reaching almost 120,000.

Though it is still too early to say whether there are fewer Albanians leaving today, for many protesters staying has become a political statement.

Rama has argued that his resignation would jeopardize Albania's bid to join the European Union by 2030, but the vast majority of Albanians are pro-European, including those on the streets and in the opposition. From the protesters' perspective, it is the government headed by Rama that is the real obstacle on the path to the EU.

Though there is currently a wave of protests across Europe, the only obvious comparison is with Serbia, where a student-led movement recently forced a long-time ruler from office. Whether Albania will follow this example depends on whether young people, who have traditionally not been deeply engaged in politics, can turn their protests into votes.

What is certain is that for three decades, Albania's leaders have relied on their citizens' suitcases. Now, the country's students have burned that very luggage.

Andi Balla is a doctoral candidate at the Leibniz Institute for East and Southeast European Studies (IOS) in Regensburg, where he is conducting research on demographic debates in Albania. He has written numerous articles on Albania for an international audience.

#36. Watch: BBC confronts German man suspected of supplying migrant gangs with small boats

A German boat dealer is suspected of being a key supplier to migrant smugglers operating in the English Channel, the BBC can reveal.

Nearly a third of small-boat crossings to the UK, in the first four months of 2026, allegedly involved boats or engines from MaReBoote, a small firm in western Germany, according to files we have seen.

The managing director, Marvin Reuter, is contesting extradition to France, where he could face smuggling, criminal conspiracy and involuntary manslaughter charges.

His equipment is suspected of being linked to two fatal accidents, in which four men died.

#30. German boat dealer suspected of supplying Channel smuggling gangs, BBC finds

Watch: BBC confronts German boat dealer Marvin Reuter

A German boat dealer is suspected by European authorities of being a key supplier to migrant smugglers operating in the English Channel, the BBC can reveal.

Nearly a third of small-boat crossings in the first four months of this year allegedly involved boats or engines from MaReBoote, a small firm in western Germany, according to files we have seen.

The managing director, Marvin Reuter, is contesting extradition to France, where he could face smuggling, criminal conspiracy and involuntary manslaughter charges.

His equipment is suspected of being linked to two fatal accidents, in which four men died.

We confronted the 35-year-old at his business near Dortmund, but he would not answer our questions. He said he was reporting us to the police.

Reuter cannot be formally charged until extradited to France but could face jail time, if tried and convicted. He is presumed innocent until proven guilty.

Investigators, we have learned, believe they have uncovered clear, persistent links between Reuter and smuggling networks.

Evidence has been gathered through the confessions of alleged accomplices and covert jail-cell recordings of an alleged gang chief - as well as the two deadly crossing attempts.

A press photo from 2021 - migrants in a boat from Marvin Reuter's firm (logo circled)

At least 82 boats from Reuter's firm reached the English coast in just over two years to May, according to French investigations.

There are also press photos dating back five years showing the MaReBoote logo on dinghies crowded with migrants.

In 2021, Reuter told the Sun and Guardian he felt "frustrated" and "anxious" that his vessels had ended up in the hands of smugglers.

There was no suggestion, at that time, that authorities suspected him of wrongdoing as a German detective reportedly told the Sun: "This would be like if somebody buys a Mercedes and does something in another country which is wrong. The Mercedes seller can't help that."

It is not exactly clear when police suspicions were aroused.

French prosecutor Samuel Finielz would not comment on the details of the investigation but confirmed the head of a German company is currently the subject of a European Arrest Warrant.

The warrant was issued in May this year and relates to alleged offences dating back to December 2024.

While selling boats is "perfectly legal" it becomes illegal, said Finielz, "when one is fully aware that they will be used by networks involved in illegal immigration or human trafficking".

Case files we have seen say that German police had learned from UK authorities that 29% of detected crossings this year, up to the end of April, involved boats and/or engines linked to MaReBoote.

In France, five other men have already been charged as part of the investigation - an Iranian, three Iraqis and a Syrian.

In one of the two fatal incidents where Reuter's equipment is suspected to be involved, one of those who died was also Syrian.

Suliman al Husein would have just turned 20 when he died, according to a birth date listed in the files.

News reports at the time say he suffered a cardiac arrest after likely being crushed on a packed dinghy during a failed launch, west of Calais, in January 2025.

He was the first migrant to die in the English Channel that year. His body was found on the floor of the boat which, investigators allege, bore the MaReBoote logo.

One night in May, French police stopped a car and seized boating equipment and fuel cans

The case against Reuter also stems from a chance arrest when French police pulled over a vehicle with German plates, near the northern port city of Boulogne-sur-Mer.

The BMW, which was towing a trailer, was spotted overtaking another vehicle at speed in the early hours of a morning in May this year - police records say.

Inside the car were two German nationals who - according to a subsequent search - were transporting jerry cans of fuel, a boat engine, pumps and a rolled up inflatable dinghy.

The men claimed they had been paid up to â¬1,000-a-time (Â£830) to make several, similar drops to "foreigners" on the coast, interrogation records show.

"Of course I knew that it wasn't legal," one is said to have admitted under questioning. "The money made me turn a blind eye."

Take a look at BBC Verify's UK migration tracker - the latest numbers on net migration, visas, asylum and small boats

Both men, who have since been convicted for smuggling offences, allegedly implicated Marvin Reuter as the man who had hired and paid them for this work.

Police files say both the BMW and the boat it was carrying had MaReBoote logos on them.

Marvin Reuter was arrested in Germany in May and later released, pending extradition.

On the day of his arrest, German police also seized more than 30 dinghies and 9,000 life jackets. Equipment which, we can now reveal, was allegedly linked to Reuter.

The BBC was previously granted access to see this haul, held in a police warehouse at a secret location in western Germany.

Part of the haul of dinghies and life jackets shown to us by German police

Meanwhile, further evidence has been gleaned from the bugging of a jail cell of a suspected gang chief, who is being held in pre-trial detention in France.

Recordings captured the inmate talking about buying nautical equipment from a German firm run by a man with Reuter's first name: Marvin - according to files we have seen.

The MaReBoote company address was also discovered on the alleged smuggler's phone, the case files say.

When we confronted Reuter last month at his business in the town of Werne, in the state of North Rhine-Westphalia, he told us to leave and drove off.

He then returned a short time later and said he was reporting us to the police. He did not answer our questions.

We approached him in writing before and after this encounter including a detailed request for comment. In response to that request, he did not address the specific allegations but accused us of harassment.

It is understood Reuter's lawyers have challenged his extradition, citing overcrowding in French prisons as one reason. Their client should face any allegations in Germany, they argue.

Reuter's firm is based in Werne, near Dortmund

In Germany, it became explicitly illegal to aid smuggling to the UK at the end of last year after a prior BBC investigation exposed the country's role as a storage hub for gangs. Before then, some anti-smuggling laws in Germany had no longer applied to the UK because of Brexit.

The raid that saw the seizure of equipment that was allegedly linked to Reuter marked the first time the new law had been used.

Small boat crossings have dropped significantly in 2026, with British authorities citing law enforcement as a "key factor". However, illegal migration into Europe has also been falling.

The UK National Crime Agency's (NCA) deputy director for organized immigration crime, Dan Cannatella-Barcroft, praised Germany's "commitment" to tackling people-smuggling but acknowledged the country would remain "an important piece of the puzzle".

While a gap in the law has been closed, Germany's proximity to France, wider transport links and the fact that some of the Kurdish-dominated gangs are based there, means it is expected to stay as a transit point in the illegal small-boats trade.

The "vast majority" of small boats equipment in the past few years has come through Germany, Cannatella-Barcroft told the BBC - much of it made in China and having been routed through Turkey.
